### The Old Maid / 老姑娘
===Original===

To M. Eugène Auguste Georges Louis Midy de la Greneraye Surville, Civil Engineer of the Corps Royal, a token of affection from his brother-in-law.
De Balzac.
Plenty of people must have come across at least one Chevalier de Valois in the provinces; there was one in Normandy, another was extant at Bourges, a third flourished at Alençon in the year 1816, and the South very likely possessed one of its own. But we are not here concerned with the numbering of the Valois tribe. Some of them, no doubt, were about as much of Valois as Louis XIV was a Bourbon; and every Chevalier was so slightly acquainted with the rest, that it was anything but politic to mention one of them when speaking to another. All of them, however, agreed to leave the Bourbons in perfect tranquillity on the throne of France, for it is a little too well proven that Henri IV succeeded to the crown in default of heirs male in the Orléans, otherwise the Valois branch; so that if any Valois exist at all, they must be descendants of Charles of Valois, Duke of Angoulême, and Marie Touchet; and even there the direct line was extinct (unless proof to the contrary is forthcoming) in the person of the Abbé de Rothelin. As for the Valois Saint-Remy, descended from Henri II, they likewise came to an end with the too famous Lamothe-Valois of the Diamond Necklace affair.
Every one of the Chevaliers, if information is correct, was, like the Chevalier of Alençon, an elderly noble, tall, lean, and without fortune. The Bourges Chevalier had emigrated, the Touraine Valois went into hiding during the Revolution, and the Alençon Chevalier was mixed up in the Vendean war, and implicated to some extent in Chouannerie. The last-named gentleman spent the most part of his youth in Paris, where, at the age of thirty, the Revolution broke in upon his career of conquests. Accepted as a true Valois by persons of the highest quality in his province, the Chevalier de Valois d’Alençon (like his namesakes) was remarkable for his fine manners, and had evidently been accustomed to move in the best society.
He dined out every day, and played cards of an evening, and, thanks to one of his weaknesses, was regarded as a great wit; he had a habit of relating a host of anecdotes of the times of Louis Quinze, and those who heard his stories for the first time thought them passably well narrated.

===Chinese===

献给皇家工程团土木工程师欧仁·奥古斯特·乔治·路易·米迪·德·拉·格雷纳雷·絮尔维尔先生——内兄谨以此书略表手足之情。
——德·巴尔扎克
外省的人一定见过至少一位瓦卢瓦骑士（Chevalier de Valois）：诺曼底出过一位，布尔日也活着一位，1816 年阿朗松（Alençon）又有一位正风光得意，南方多半也有自家的一位。不过我们在此并不打算清点瓦卢瓦一族的人数。他们中间有些人，与瓦卢瓦的血统恐怕同路易十四之于波旁相差无几；而且各位骑士彼此知之甚浅，在这一位面前提起那一位，实在算不得圆通。然而他们全都客客气气地听任波旁家族安坐法兰西王位，因为亨利四世是在奥尔良一支——亦即瓦卢瓦支——男嗣断绝之后继承王冠的，这一点早已考证得再明白不过；所以世上倘若还有瓦卢瓦，那必是昂古莱姆公爵查理·德·瓦卢瓦与玛丽·图谢的后裔；而即便是这一支，嫡系也已断绝（除非拿出相反的证据来），终结在罗特兰修道院院长（Abbé de Rothelin）其人身上。至于亨利二世传下的瓦卢瓦·圣雷米一支，也同样断绝在那位因钻石项链案而大名鼎鼎的拉莫特-瓦卢瓦手里。
据可靠的传闻，每一位这样的骑士都同阿朗松这位一样，是上了年纪的贵族，身材瘦高，身无恒产。布尔日那位骑士当年流亡国外，都兰那位瓦卢瓦在大革命年间藏匿不出，阿朗松这位骑士则卷进了旺代战争，多少还沾着点朱安党叛乱的边。这位末提到的绅士，青年时代大半在巴黎度过；三十岁上，大革命打断了他情场征伐的生涯。瓦卢瓦·德·阿朗松骑士（同他的同名者们一样）被本省最高贵的人家认作真正的瓦卢瓦，以翩翩的风度著称，显然是出入过上流社会的。
他天天在外头吃饭，晚上打牌，多亏他的一项小弱点，人家把他当作了不起的才子：他惯于讲述一堆路易十五时代的轶事，头一回听的人还觉得讲得相当动人。

===Original===

The Chevalier de Valois, moreover, had one virtue; he refrained from repeating his own good sayings, and never alluded to his conquests, albeit his smiles and airs were delightfully indiscreet. The old gentleman took full advantage of the old-fashioned Voltairean noble’s privilege of staying away from Mass, but his irreligion was very tenderly dealt with out of regard for his devotion to the Royalist cause.
One of his most remarkable graces (Molé must have learned it of him) was his way of taking snuff from an old-fashioned snuffbox with a portrait of a lady on the lid. The Princess Goritza, a lovely Hungarian, had been famous for her beauty towards the end of the reign of Louis XV; and the Chevalier could never speak without emotion of the foreign great lady whom he loved in his youth, for whom he had fought a duel with M. de Lauzun.
But by this time the Chevalier had lived fifty-eight years, and if he owned to but fifty of them, he might safely indulge himself in that harmless deceit. Thin, fair-complexioned men, among other privileges, retain that youthfulness of shape which in men, as in women, contributes as much as anything to stave off any appearance of age. And, indeed, it is a fact that all the life, or rather, all the grace, which is the expression of life, lies in the figure. Among the Chevalier’s personal traits, mention must be made of the portentous nose with which Nature had endowed him. It cut a pallid countenance sharply into two sections which seemed to have nothing to do with each other; so much so, indeed, that only one-half of his face would flush with the exertion of digestion after dinner; all the glow being confined to the left side, a phenomenon worthy of note in times when physiology is so much occupied with the human heart. M. de Valois’ health was not apparently robust, judging by his long, thin legs, lean frame, and sallow complexion; but he ate like an ogre, alleging, doubtless by way of excuse for his voracity, that he suffered from a complaint known in the provinces as a “hot liver.” The flush on his left cheek confirmed the story; but in a land where meals are developed on the lines of thirty or forty dishes, and last for four hours at a stretch, the Chevalier’s abnormal appetite might well seem to be a special mark of the favor of Providence vouchsafed to the good town.

===Chinese===

况且瓦卢瓦骑士还有一样美德：他从不复述自己的妙语，也从不提自己的战绩，尽管他的微笑与神态早已可爱地不知分寸。老先生把旧式伏尔泰派贵族不上弥撒的特权享用了个十足，但他那点不敬神，看在他忠于保王事业的份上，人人待之格外温厚。
他最出众的风雅之一（莫莱准是跟他学的），是开一只旧式鼻烟盒取鼻烟的手势，盒盖上嵌着一位夫人的画像。戈里察公主（Princess Goritza），一位美丽的匈牙利女子，在路易十五朝末年以美貌名重一时；骑士一提起他年轻时爱过的这位外国贵妇——为了她，他曾同德·洛赞先生决斗——总是激动不已。
不过骑士此时已活到五十八岁，纵然他只认五十，尽可以放心地沉迷于这无害的欺骗。皮肤白皙的瘦个子男人享有种种特权，其中之一便是体形经久不衰的年轻，这在男人身上同在女人身上一样，最能顶住老态的显现。而且事实上，全部的生命——或者不如说全部的优雅，那是生命的表情——都在体形上，这可是千真万确。在骑士的个人特征之中，还得提一提造化赋予他的那只惊天动地的鼻子。它把一张苍白的面孔干脆利落地劈成互不相干的两半，以至于饭后消化用劲时，只有半边脸会泛红，热度全集中在左半边——这个现象，在生理学如此埋头研究人心脏的时代，值得记上一笔。看德·瓦卢瓦先生那双长腿、精瘦的身架和蜡黄的脸色，他的身子骨似乎并不结实；可他吃起饭来赛过生番，还说自己害着一种外省人叫作“肝热”的毛病，多半算是给他那副好胃口找个说辞。左颊上那片红晕正好证实了这说法；不过，在一顿饭要摆三四十道菜、一吃就是四个钟头的地方，骑士这反常的好胃口，倒真像是上天赐给这座好城的格外恩典。

===Original===

That flush on the left cheek, according to divers medical authorities, is a sign of prodigality of heart; and, indeed, the Chevalier’s past record of gallantry might seem to confirm a professional dictum for which the present chronicler (most fortunately) is in nowise responsible. But in spite of these symptoms, M. de Valois was of nervous temperament, and in consequence long-lived; and if his liver was hot, to use the old-fashioned phrase, his heart was not a whit less inflammable. If there was a line worn here and there in his face, and a silver thread or so in his hair, an experienced eye would have discerned in these signs and tokens the stigmata of desire, the furrows traced by past pleasure. And, in fact, in his face, the unmistakable marks of the crow’s foot and the serpent’s tooth took the shape of the delicate wrinkles so prized at the court of Cytherea.
Everything about the gallant Chevalier revealed the “ladies’ man.” So minutely careful was he over his ablutions, that it was a pleasure to see his cheeks; they might have been brushed over with some miraculous water. That portion of his head which the hair refused to hide from view shone like ivory. His eyebrows, like his hair, had a youthful look, so carefully was their growth trained and regulated by the comb. A naturally fair skin seemed to be yet further whitened by some mysterious preparation; and while the Chevalier never used scent, there was about him, as it were, a perfume of youth which enhanced the freshness of his looks. His hands, that told of race, were as carefully kept as if they belonged to some coxcomb of the gentler sex; you could not help noticing those rose-pink neatly-trimmed fingernails. Indeed, but for his lordly superlative nose, the Chevalier would have looked like a doll.
It takes some resolution to spoil this portrait with the admission of a foible; the Chevalier put cotton wool in his ears, and still continued to wear earrings﻿—two tiny Negroes’ heads set with brilliants. They were of admirable workmanship, it is true, and their owner was so far attached to the singular appendages, that he used to justify his fancy by saying “that his sick headaches had left him since his ears were pierced.” He used to suffer from sick headaches.

===Chinese===

据好几位医学权威说，左颊上的红晕是心肠挥霍的征象；而骑士过去的风流账，似乎也证实了这条行家的断语——好在笔者（万幸）对此毫无责任。然而虽有这些征候，德·瓦卢瓦先生是神经质体质，因而是长寿之相；如果说用老话讲他的肝是热的，他的心也一点不更难着火。他脸上纵然这儿那儿有一两道纹路，头发里夹着一两根银丝，有经验的眼睛却能在这些记号里辨认出欲望的烙印、往日欢爱犁出的沟痕。而事实上，他脸上那确凿无疑的鱼尾纹和“蛇牙纹”，恰恰长成库忒拉（Cytherea）宫廷上最受人珍视的那类细巧皱纹。
这位风流骑士从头到脚处处显出“妇女之友”的本色。他盥洗得那样精心，看他的脸蛋便是一桩乐事，仿佛刷过一层灵水。头上头发遮不住的那一块，莹润得赛过象牙。他的眉毛同头发一样年轻，梳理驯服得服服帖帖。天生的白皮肤，似乎又被什么神秘的香膏涂得愈发白皙；骑士从不洒香水，可他周身仿佛透着一股青春的芬芳，把他容色的鲜润又添了几分。他那双见得出血统的手，保养得像某位女性纨绔的手一般精心；那玫瑰色、修剪得整整齐齐的指甲，叫人不能不多瞧两眼。说真的，若不是那只气派十足的超级鼻子，骑士活脱脱像个蜡娃娃。
要往这幅画像上添一笔癖好，难免下不了狠心：骑士耳朵里塞着棉花，却仍旧戴着耳环——两颗镶钻的黑种小人头。做工确实精巧绝伦，主人对这对奇特的饰物也偏爱到这般地步，常常替自己的癖好辩白，说“自从穿了耳朵，偏头风就再没有犯过”。他从前是常犯偏头风的。

===Original===

The Chevalier is not held up as a flawless character; but even if an old bachelor’s heart sends too much blood to his face, is he never therefore to be forgiven for his adorable absurdities? Perhaps (who knows?) there are sublime secrets hidden away beneath them. And besides, the Chevalier de Valois made amends for his Negroes’ heads with such a variety of other and different charms, that society ought to have felt itself sufficiently compensated. He really was at great pains to conceal his age and to make himself agreeable.
First and foremost, witness the extreme care which he gave to his linen, the one distinction in dress which a gentleman may permit himself in modern days. The Chevalier’s linen was invariably fine and white, as befitted a noble. His coat, though remarkably neat, was always somewhat worn, but spotless and uncreased. The preservation of this garment bordered on the miraculous in the opinion of those who noticed the Chevalier’s elegant indifference on this head; not that he went so far as to scrape his clothes with broken glass (a refinement invented by the Prince of Wales), but he set himself to carry out the first principles of dress as laid down by Englishmen of the very highest and finest fashion, and this with a personal element of coxcombry which Alençon was scarcely capable of appreciating. Does the world owe no esteem to those that take such pains for it? And what was all this labor but the fulfilment of that very hardest of sayings in the Gospel, which bids us return good for evil? The freshness of the toilet, the care for dress, suited well with the Chevalier’s blue eyes, ivory teeth, and bland personality; still, the superannuated Adonis had nothing masculine in his appearance, and it would seem that he employed the illusion of the toilet to hide the ravages of other than military campaigns.
To tell the whole truth, the Chevalier had a voice singularly at variance with his delicate fairness. So full was it and sonorous, that you would have been startled by the sound of it unless, with certain observers of human nature, you held the theory that the voice was only what might be expected of such a nose. With something less of volume than a giant double-bass, it was a full, pleasant baritone, reminding you of the hautboy among musical instruments, sweet and resistant, deep and rich.
M.

===Chinese===

骑士并不曾被树成十全十美的楷模；可是，一个老单身汉的心纵然把太多的血送上脸，难道就该凭这个不饶恕他那可爱的荒唐么？说不定（谁知道呢？）这荒唐底下还藏着崇高的秘密哩。何况瓦卢瓦骑士自有形形色色别样的风致，足够抵偿那对黑种小人头，社交界大可觉得自己早已得着补偿。他确实煞费苦心地遮掩年纪、讨人喜欢。
首屈一指的，请看他内衣何等讲究，那可是如今一位绅士在穿着上唯一容许自己的讲究了。骑士的内衣永远又细又白，同贵族身份相称。他的礼服虽然素净得出奇，却总是半旧的，然而一尘不染，不见一道褶皱。在留意到骑士对此事雍容的漫不经心的人看来，这件衣服的保全近乎奇迹；他倒还不至于用碎玻璃去刮衣服（那是威尔士亲王的发明），但他切实奉行英国最顶尖时髦人士立下的穿衣第一原则，还带着一股阿朗松人未必领会的个人纨绔气。世人难道不该敬重这般为它操劳的人么？这份辛劳，不正是应了《福音书》里那句最难行的训条——以德报怨么？衣饰的鲜洁、穿着的讲究，同骑士的蓝眼睛、象牙色的牙齿、温文的性情正相般配；不过这位过时的阿多尼斯（Adonis）身上毫无丈夫气概，看来他是想借梳妆造成的幻象，去遮掩另一类——并非军旅——的战役留下的残迹。
说句实话，骑士的嗓音同他纤白的面色出奇地不相称。它那样饱满洪亮，冷不丁听见，准叫你吃一惊，除非你同某些观人的行家一样抱持一套理论，认为这样的鼻子生出这样的嗓音，原在意料之中。那嗓音虽不及巨型低音提琴的音量，却是一副饱满悦耳的男中音，令人想起乐器中的双簧管：甜润而有筋骨，深沉而丰腴。
德

===Original===

de Valois had discarded the absurd costume still worn by a few antiquated Royalists, and frankly modernized his dress. He always appeared in a maroon coat with gilt buttons, loosely-fitting breeches with gold buckles at the knees, a white sprigged waistcoat, a tight stock, and a collarless shirt; this being a last vestige of eighteenth century costume, which its wearer was the less willing to relinquish because it enabled him to display a throat not unworthy of a lay abbé. Square gold buckles of a kind unknown to the present generation shone conspicuous upon his patent leather shoes. Two watch chains hung in view in parallel lines from a couple of fobs, another survival of an eighteenth century mode which the *incroyable* did not disdain to copy in the time of the Directory. This costume of a transition period, reuniting two centuries, was worn by the Chevalier with the grace of an old-world marquis, a grace lost to the French stage since Molé’s last pupil, Fleury, retired from the boards and took his secret with him.
The old bachelor’s private life, seemingly open to all eyes, was in reality inscrutable. He lived in a modest lodging (to say the least of it) up two pairs of stairs in a house in the Rue du Cours, his landlady being the laundress most in request in Alençon﻿—which fact explains the extreme elegance of the Chevalier’s linen. Ill luck was so to order it that Alençon one day could actually believe that he had not always conducted himself as befitted a man of his quality, and that in his old age he privately married one Césarine, the mother of an infant which had the impertinence to come without being called.
“He gave his hand to her who for so long had lent her hand to iron his linen,” said a certain M. du Bousquier.
The sensitive noble’s last days were the more vexed by this unpleasant scandal, because, as shall be shown in the course of this present Scene, he had already lost a long-cherished hope for which he had made many a sacrifice.
Mme. Lardot’s two rooms were let to M. le Chevalier de Valois at the moderate rent of a hundred francs per annum. The worthy gentleman dined out every night, and only came home to sleep; he was therefore at charges for nothing but his breakfast, which always consisted of a cup of chocolate with butter and fruit, according to the season.

===Chinese===

瓦卢瓦先生早已丢弃了少数老古董保王党还穿在身上的可笑装束，坦然把衣着改成了新式。他露面永远是一件缀镀金纽扣的栗色礼服，膝头带金扣的宽松短裤，绣小白花的背心，紧窄的领带，无领衬衫——这是十八世纪服装的最后一点遗迹，穿的人格外舍不得撒手，因为它正好显出一段不辱没在俗修道院院长的喉咙。方形金鞋扣，如今这一代人见所未见，在他的漆皮鞋上闪闪夺目。两条表链从两只表袋里平行地垂挂出来，这是十八世纪风尚的又一孑遗，连督政府年代的“奇装公子”（incroyable）也不屑于效仿。这套承上启下、连缀两个世纪的过渡期服装，骑士穿来带着旧世界侯爵的翩翩风度；这风度，自莫莱的末代弟子弗勒里退下舞台、把秘诀随身带走之后，法兰西戏剧舞台上便失传了。
这位老单身汉的私生活，看似人人看在眼里，实则无从窥测。他住在库尔街一幢房子的四层楼上（往客气里说）一套简朴的寓所里，女房东是阿朗松最抢手的洗衣妇——这一节，正说明了骑士内衣为何那般考究。倒霉的是，阿朗松有一天竟当真相信，他从前行事未必处处合乎身份，又相信他老来私下娶了一个名叫塞扎里娜的女人，那女人的婴孩不请自来，冒失地降生了。
“他把手交给了那只替他熨了这么久衬衣的手。”一位杜·布斯基耶先生（M. du Bousquier）这样说。
这位敏感的贵族，暮年因这桩恼人的丑闻愈发不得安宁，因为——本篇场景往下自会交代——他早已失去一个珍爱已久、为之牺牲过许多的希望。
拉尔多太太（Mme. Lardot）的两间房以每年一百法郎的公道租金，租给了瓦卢瓦骑士先生。这位可敬的绅士每晚在外吃饭，只回家睡觉；因此他只需破费一顿早餐，照例是一杯巧克力，佐以黄油和时鲜水果。

===Original===

A fire was never lighted in his rooms except in the very coldest winters, and then only while he was dressing. Between the hours of eleven and four M. de Valois took his walks abroad, read the newspapers, and paid calls.
When the Chevalier first settled in Alençon, he magnanimously owned that he had nothing but an annuity of six hundred livres paid in quarterly instalments by his old man of business, with whom the certificates were deposited. This was all that remained of his former wealth. And every three months, in fact, a banker in the town paid him a hundred and fifty francs remitted by one M. Bordin of Paris, the last of the *procureurs du Châtelet*. These particulars everybody knew, for the Chevalier had taken care to ask his confidant to keep the matter a profound secret. He reaped the fruits of his misfortunes. A cover was laid for him in all the best houses in Alençon; he was asked to every evening party. His talents as a cardplayer, a teller of anecdotes, a pleasant and well-bred man of the world, were so thoroughly appreciated that an evening was spoiled if the connoisseur of the town was not present. The host and hostess and all the ladies present missed his little approving grimace. “You are adorably well dressed,” from the old bachelor’s lips, was sweeter to a young woman in a ballroom than the sight of her rival’s despair.
There were certain old-world expressions which no one could pronounce so well. “My heart,” “my jewel,” “my little love,” “my queen,” and all the dear diminutives of the year 1770 took an irresistible charm from M. de Valois’ lips; in short, the privilege of superlatives was his. His compliments, of which, moreover, he was chary, won him the goodwill of the elderly ladies; he flattered everyone down to the officials of whom he had no need.
He was so fine a gentleman at the card-table, that his behavior would have marked him out anywhere. He never complained; when his opponents lost he praised their play; he never undertook the education of his partners by showing them what they ought to have done.

===Chinese===

他的房间里从不生火，除非在三九严寒，而且也只趁他穿衣的当口。十一点到四点之间，德·瓦卢瓦先生出门散步，读报，拜客。
骑士初到阿朗松定居时，宽宏大量地自认全部家当只有一笔六百里弗尔的年金，由他的老经纪人按季支付，票据便存放在那位经纪人手里。这是他从前家产的残存。事实上，城里一位银行家每三个月付他一百五十法郎，钱由巴黎一位博尔丹先生汇来——此公是夏特莱法院诉讼代理人（procureurs du Châtelet）的最后几位之一。这些底细人人尽知，因为骑士特意叮嘱他的心腹，务必把这件事守成天大的秘密。他的不幸结出了果实。阿朗松所有上等人家都为他摆着一副刀叉；家家晚间聚会都请他。他作为牌手、轶事名家、讨人喜欢的有教养的社交人物，才华深受赏识，晚会上若缺了本城这位鉴赏家，便算缺了魂。主人和主妇、在场的太太们，个个惦记他那个小小赞许的鬼脸。老单身汉嘴里道出的一句“您穿得可爱极了”，落在舞会上的少妇耳中，比亲眼看见情敌绝望还要甜蜜。
有些旧时代的辞令，谁也念不到他一半好听。“我的心肝”“我的宝石”“我的小爱人”“我的王后”，还有 1770 年那一整套可亲的爱称，出自德·瓦卢瓦先生之口，便添了一种不可抗拒的韵味；总之，最高级形容词的专用权归他所有。他的恭维又极吝啬，为他赢得老太太们的好感；他见人捧人，连他用不着的官吏也不漏掉。
他在牌桌上是那样一位绅士，这份气派放在哪里都出挑。他从不抱怨；对手输了，他夸他们打得好；他也从不越俎代庖，指点搭档本该怎么出牌。

===Original===

If a nauseating discussion of this kind began while the cards were making, the Chevalier brought out his snuffbox with a gesture worthy of Molé, looked at the Princess Goritza’s portrait, took off the lid in a stately manner, heaped up a pinch, rubbed it to a fine powder between finger and thumb, blew off the light particles, shaped a little cone in his hand, and by the time the cards were dealt he had replenished the cavities in his nostrils and replaced the Princess in his waistcoat pocket﻿—always to the left-hand side.
None but a noble of the Gracious as distinguished from the Great Century could have invented such a compromise between a disdainful silence and an epigram which would have passed over the heads of his company. The Chevalier took dull minds as he found them, and knew how to turn them to account. His irresistible evenness of temper caused many a one to say, “I admire the Chevalier de Valois!” Everything about him, his conversation and his manner, seemed in keeping with his mild appearance. He was careful to come into collision with no one, man or woman. Indulgent with deformity as with defects of intellect, he listened patiently (with the help of the Princess Goritza) to tales of the little woes of life in a country town; to anecdotes of the undercooked egg at breakfast, or the sour cream in the coffee; to small grotesque details of physical ailments; to tales of dreams and visitations and wakings with a start. The Chevalier was an exquisite listener. He had a languishing glance, a stock attitude to denote compassion; he put in his “Ohs” and “Poohs” and “What-did-you-dos?” with charming appropriateness. Till his dying day no one ever suspected that while these avalanches of nonsense lasted, the Chevalier in his own mind was rehearsing the warmest passages of an old romance, of which the Princess Goritza was the heroine. Has anyone ever given a thought to the social uses of extinct sentiment?﻿—or guessed in how many indirect ways love benefits humanity?
Possibly this listener’s faculty sufficiently explains the Chevalier’s popularity; he was always the spoiled child of the town, although he never quitted a drawing-room without carrying off about five livres in his pocket. Sometimes he lost, and he made the most of his losses, but it very seldom happened.

===Chinese===

洗牌的时候若有人挑起这类叫人作呕的争论，骑士便掏出鼻烟盒，那手势配得上莫莱，端详一眼戈里察公主的画像，郑重其事地揭开盒盖，撮起一撮鼻烟，拇指食指之间捻成细末，吹去浮屑，在掌心拢出一个小小的圆锥，等牌发齐，他两只鼻孔也重新装满，把公主放回背心口袋——永远在左边。
唯有“文雅世纪”——而非“伟大世纪”——的贵族，才想得出这般折中妙法：一面保持着轻蔑的缄默，一面又放出一句从满座头顶飞掠而过的妙语。迟钝的头脑，骑士照单全收，并且懂得如何派上用场。他那不可抗拒的好脾气，引得多少人赞叹：“我真服了瓦卢瓦骑士！”他的一切，谈吐和举止，都同他温文的外表相称。他小心避免同任何人冲突，无论男女。他对身体的残缺同对智力的缺陷一样宽容，耐心地（借戈里察公主之助）听人絮叨小城生活的小小苦楚：早餐的鸡蛋没煮熟啦，咖啡里的奶油发酸啦；絮叨身体毛病里那些怪诞的细枝末节；絮叨梦见什么、魂灵显形、半夜惊醒。骑士是一位精致的倾听者。他有一缕缠绵的眼神，一副现成的怜悯姿态；他那“哦”“啧”“后来呢”的插话，插得恰到好处。直到咽气那天，也没有人起过疑心：当这些胡话的雪崩倾泻下来时，骑士心里正默诵着一篇古老罗曼史里最火热的段落，女主角便是戈里察公主。可曾有人想到过消亡的感情在社交上的用处？——可曾有人猜到，爱情以多少种迂回的方式造福人类？
或许，单凭这倾听的本事，便足以解释骑士何以得人心；他永远是全城惯坏的宠儿，尽管他每回走出客厅，口袋里总不多不少装走大约五里弗尔。他偶尔也输，输了也善加利用，不过难得输。

===Original===

All those who knew him say with one accord that never in any place have they met with so agreeable a mummy, not even in the Egyptian museum at Turin. Surely in no known country of the globe did parasite appear in such a benignant shape. Never did selfishness in its most concentrated form show itself so inoffensive, so full of good offices as in this gentleman; the Chevalier’s egoism was as good as another man’s devoted friendship. If any person went to ask M. de Valois to do some trifling service which the worthy Chevalier could not perform without inconvenience, that person never went away without conceiving a great liking for him, and departed fully convinced that the Chevalier could do nothing in the matter, or might do harm if he meddled with it.
To explain this problematical existence the chronicler is bound to admit, while Truth﻿—that ruthless debauchee﻿—has caught him by the throat, that latterly after the three sad, glorious Days of July, Alençon discovered that M. de Valois’ winnings at cards amounted to something like a hundred and fifty crowns every quarter, which amount the ingenious Chevalier intrepidly remitted to himself as an annuity, so that he might not appear to be without resources in a country with a great turn for practical details. Plenty of his friends﻿—he was dead by that time, please to remark﻿—plenty of his friends denied this *in toto*, they maintained that the stories were fables and slanders set in circulation by the Liberal party and that M. de Valois was an honorable and worthy gentleman. Luckily for clever gamblers, there will always be champions of this sort for them among the onlookers. Feeling ashamed to excuse wrongdoing, they stoutly deny that wrong has been done. Do not accuse them of wrong-headedness; they have their own sense of self-respect, and the Government sets them an example of the virtue which consists in burying its dead by night without chanting a Te Deum over a defeat. And suppose that M. de Valois permitted himself a neat stratagem that would have won Gramont’s esteem, a smile from Baron de Foeneste, and a shake of the hand from the Marquis de Moncade, was he any the less the pleasant dinner guest, the wit, the unvarying cardplayer, the charming retailer of anecdotes, the delight of Alençon?

===Chinese===

凡是认识他的人都异口同声地说，走遍天下也没见过这样讨人喜欢的木乃伊，连都灵的埃及博物馆里也没有。的确，普天之下，食客何曾以这样一副慈祥面目现过身。最浓缩形态的自私，也从不曾像在这位绅士身上这样无害而多与人方便；骑士的利己主义，抵得上旁人的赤胆忠心。倘若有人去求德·瓦卢瓦先生办一件区区小事，而这事叫可敬的骑士办起来实在为难，那人临走必对他生出大大的好感，并且深信不疑：骑士在此事上实在无能为力，他要硬插一手，说不定反倒坏事。
要解释这一存在之谜，记事者不得不承认——此刻“真理”那位无情的浪荡子正掐着他的喉咙——七月那悲壮的三日之后，阿朗松发现德·瓦卢瓦先生打牌每季赢进大约一百五十埃居，这笔巧妙的进项，机灵的骑士大无畏地给自己定成了年金，免得在这个凡事讲实证的国度里显得身无分文。他的许多朋友——请注意，那时他已过世——他的许多朋友对此全盘否认，一口咬定这些传闻是自由党放出的寓言与诽谤，德·瓦卢瓦先生是位可敬可靠的正人君子。所幸天下的聪明赌徒，看客里永远不缺这一路卫道之士。他们羞于替错事开脱，便斩钉截铁地否认错事存在。别骂他们糊涂；他们自有自尊，何况政府还给他们做出榜样，垂示那种深更半夜埋葬败绩、绝不为溃败高唱《赞美颂》的美德。退一步说，就算德·瓦卢瓦先生使过一手妙计，准能博得格拉蒙的称许、德·费内斯特男爵的一笑、蒙卡德侯爵的一握，他难道就不再是那可人的座上宾、那才子、那雷打不动的牌手、那迷人的轶事贩子、阿朗松的欢喜冤家了么？

===Original===

In what, moreover, does the action, lying, as it does, outside the laws of right and wrong, offend against the elegant code of a man of birth and breeding? When so many people are obliged to give pensions to others, what more natural than of one’s own accord to allow an annuity to one’s own best friend? But Laïus is dead.﻿ ﻿…
After some fifteen years of this kind of life, the Chevalier had amassed ten thousand and some odd hundred francs. When the Bourbons returned, he said that an old friend of his, M. le Marquis de Pombreton, late a lieutenant in the Black Musketeers, had returned a loan of twelve hundred pistoles with which he emigrated. The incident made a sensation. It was quoted afterwards as a set-off against droll stories in the *Constitutionnel* of the ways in which some émigrés paid their debts. The poor Chevalier used to blush all over the right side of his face whenever this noble trait in the Marquis de Pombreton came up in conversation. At the time everyone rejoiced with M. de Valois; he used to consult capitalists as to the best way of investing this wreck of his former fortune; and, putting faith in the Restoration, invested it all in Government stock when the funds had fallen to fifty-six francs twenty-five centimes. MM. de Lenoncourt, de Navarreins, de Verneuil, de Fontaine, and La Billardière, to whom he was known, had obtained a pension of a hundred crowns for him from the privy purse, he said, and the Cross of St. Louis. By what means the old Chevalier obtained the two solemn confirmations of his title and quality, no one ever knew; but this much is certain, the Cross of St. Louis gave him brevet rank as a colonel on a retiring pension, by reason of his services with the Catholic army in the West.
Besides the fiction of the annuity, to which no one gave a thought, the Chevalier was now actually possessed of a genuine income of a thousand francs. But with this improvement in his circumstances he made no change in his life or manners; only﻿—the red ribbon looked wondrous well on his maroon coat; it was a finishing touch, as it were, to this portrait of a gentleman.

===Chinese===

再说，这种行为既然置身于是非法律之外，又究竟在哪一点上触犯了出身高贵、教养有素之人的雅致法典呢？天下这许多人不得不掏钱养活别人，自己情愿给最好的朋友立一笔年金，还有什么比这更顺理成章？可惜拉伊俄斯已经死了。……
过了大约十五年这样的日子，骑士攒下了一万零几百法郎。波旁王室卷土重来时，他说他的一位老友，前黑衣火枪队中尉蓬布雷东侯爵先生，归还了他当年流亡时借去的一千二百皮斯托尔。这件事轰动一时，后来《宪政报》上登了些流亡者还债的花样，这桩事便被引来作对照。每逢有人提起蓬布雷东侯爵这桩高义之举，可怜的骑士总是半边脸涨得通红。当时人人都替德·瓦卢瓦先生高兴；他去请教几位资本家，如何安放他这份残破家业的最后一块板；出于对复辟的信心，他在公债跌到五十六法郎二十五生丁时，把钱尽数买了政府公债。德·勒农库、德·纳瓦兰、德·韦尔纳伊、德·丰丹诸位先生和拉·比亚尔迪埃尔——都是他的熟人——据他说，为他从王室私库里求得一份一百埃居的年金，外加一枚圣路易十字勋章。老骑士用什么路数弄来这两件对他头衔与身份的庄严确认，无人知晓；但有一点确凿无疑：凭着他在西部天主教军队里的战功，圣路易十字勋章使他取得了领退役金的荣誉上校衔。
除了那笔无人在意的年金虚构，骑士如今实实在在拥有一千法郎的进项了。可是境况虽见好转，他的生活与做派分毫未改；只是——那条红绶带配他那件栗色礼服好看极了，恰似为这幅绅士画像补上最后一笔。

===Original===

Ever since the year 1802 the Chevalier had sealed his letters with an ancient gold seal, engraved roughly enough, but not so badly but that the Castérans, d’Esgrignons, and Troisvilles might see that he bore the arms of France impaled with his own, to wit, France per pale, gules two bars gemelles, a cross of five mascles conjoined or, on a chief sable a cross pattee argent over all; with a knight’s casquet for crest and the motto﻿—**Valeo**. With these noble arms the so-called bastard Valois was entitled to ride in all the royal coaches in the world.
Plenty of people envied the old bachelor his easy life, made up of boston, trictrac, reversis, whist, and piquet; of good play, dinners well digested, pinches of snuff gracefully taken, and quiet walks abroad. Almost all Alençon thought that his existence was empty alike of ambitions and cares; but where is the man whose life is quite as simple as they suppose who envy him?
In the remotest country village you shall find human mollusks, rotifers inanimate to all appearance, which cherish a passion for lepidoptera or conchology, and are at infinite pains to acquire some new butterfly, or a specimen of *Concha Veneris*. And the Chevalier had not merely shells and butterflies of his own, he cherished an ambitious desire with a pertinacity and profound strategy worthy of a Sixtus V. He meant to marry a rich old maid; in all probability because a wealthy marriage would be a stepping-stone to the high spheres of the Court. *This* was the secret of his royal bearing and prolonged abode in Alençon.
Very early one Tuesday morning in the middle of spring in the year ’16 (to use his own expression), the Chevalier was just slipping on his dressing-gown, an old-fashioned green silk damask of a flowered pattern, when, in spite of the cotton in his ears, he heard a girl’s light footstep on the stairs. In another moment someone tapped discreetly three times on the door, and then, without waiting for an answer, a very handsome damsel slipped like a snake into the old bachelor’s apartment.
“Ah, Suzanne, is that you?” said the Chevalier de Valois, continuing to strop his razor. “What are you here for, deal little jewel of mischief?”
“I have come to tell you something which perhaps will give you as much pleasure as annoyance.”
“Is it something about Césarine?”

===Chinese===

早在 1802 年，骑士封缄书信便用一枚古金印章，镌工粗陋，却也不算太糟，卡斯特朗家、埃斯格里尼翁（Esgrignon）家、特鲁瓦维尔（Troisville）家都能看清，他的纹章是法兰西王纹与自家纹并列：说来便是，左半为法兰西之纹，右半红底两对横细条、金五连环十字，顶带黑底银空心十字；盔顶立骑士盔，铭言为——Valeo（我力足恃）。凭着这副贵胄纹章，这位所谓的瓦卢瓦私生后人，尽可以乘坐普天之下一切王家马车。
多少人艳羡老单身汉这份清福日子：波士顿牌、十五子棋、雷韦尔西、惠斯特、皮克轮着来；牌打得好，晚饭消化得动，撮鼻烟的姿势优雅，出门散步安安静静。差不多整个阿朗松都以为，他这个人既无野心也无忧虑；可是，天下哪里有被人艳羡的人，日子真像艳羡者设想的那么简单呢？
哪怕在最偏僻的村落，你也会发现人类中的软体动物、貌似不动的轮虫，痴心于鳞翅目或贝类学，千辛万苦要到手一只新蝴蝶，或一枚维纳斯贝（Concha Veneris）标本。而骑士手里可不光有贝壳和蝴蝶，他还揣着一颗野心，那份执着与深谋远虑，配得上一位于尔班教皇西克斯图斯五世。他打算娶一个有钱的老姑娘；多半是因为，一门阔气的婚事，正是踏进宫廷上层的进身之阶。这才是他那王者气派与久居阿朗松的底细。
“十六”年（这是他的说法）仲春的一个星期二清晨，骑士正往身上套晨衣——一件旧式绿花绸的，耳边虽塞着棉花，却听见楼梯上一个姑娘轻巧的脚步声。转眼间，有人斯斯文文叩了三下门，接着不等答话，一个十分标致的姑娘便蛇一般溜进了老单身汉的屋子。
“啊，苏珊娜（Suzanne），是你呀？”瓦卢瓦骑士说着，手上的剃刀照旧在磨。“你来做什么，我的小祸水儿？”
“我来告诉你一件事，兴许叫你又高兴又烦恼。”
“是塞扎里娜的事？”

===Original===

“Much I trouble myself about your Césarine,” pouted she, half careless, half in earnest.
The charming Suzanne, whose escapade was to exercise so great an influence on the lives of all the principal characters in this story, was one of Mme. Lardot’s laundry girls. And now for a few topographical details.
The whole ground floor of the house was given up to the laundry. The little yard was a drying-ground where embroidered handkerchiefs, collarettes, muslin slips, cuffs, frilled shirts, cravats, laces, embroidered petticoats, all the fine washing of the best houses in the town, in short, hung out along the lines of hair rope. The Chevalier used to say that he was kept informed of the progress of the receiver-general’s wife’s flirtations by the number of slips thus brought to light; and the amount of frilled shirts and cambric cravats varied directly with the petticoats and collarettes. By this system of double entry, as it were, he detected all the assignations in the town; but the Chevalier was always discreet, he never let fall an epigram that might have closed a house to him. And yet he was a witty talker! For which reason you may be sure that M. de Valois’ manners were of the finest, while his talents, as so often happens, were thrown away upon a narrow circle. Still, for he was only human after all, he sometimes could not resist the pleasure of a searching side glance which made women tremble, and nevertheless they liked him when they found out how profoundly discreet he was, how full of sympathy for their pretty frailties.
Mme. Lardot’s forewoman and factotum, an alarmingly ugly spinster of five-and-forty, occupied the rest of the second floor with the Chevalier. Her door on the landing was exactly opposite his; and her apartment, like his own, consisted of two rooms, looking respectively upon the street and the yard. Above, there was nothing but the attics where the linen was dried in winter. Below lodged Mme. Lardot’s grandfather. The old man, Grévin by name, had been a privateer in his time, and had served under Admiral Simeuse in the Indies; now he was paralyzed and stone deaf. Mme. Lardot herself occupied the rooms beneath her forewoman, and so great was her weakness for people of condition, that she might be said to be blind where the Chevalier was concerned. In her eyes, M.

===Chinese===

“你的塞扎里娜才不干我事！”她撅着嘴说，半真半假。
迷人的苏珊娜——她这一趟出走，日后将牵动这个故事里所有主要人物的命运——是拉尔多太太洗衣坊里的熨衣姑娘。这里且交代几笔地理详情。
房子的整个底层都作了洗衣坊。小院子是晾晒场，城里上等人家的全部细软——绣花手帕、小披肩、平纹细布衬裙、袖口、镶褶衬衫、领巾、花边、绣花裙子——总之所有精洗的东西，都顺着麻绳晾出来。骑士常说，凭这样晾出来的衬裙数目，他便能推知总税务官夫人调情的进度；镶褶衬衫和细麻布领巾的数量，则同裙子和披肩成正比。靠这套复式记账法，全城的幽会他无一漏网；不过骑士永远谨慎，从不失口说出一句可能叫一户人家对他闭门羹的俏皮话。而他原是个会说俏皮话的人！因此你可以断定，德·瓦卢瓦先生的仪态是一等一的，只是他的才华同常有的事一样，白白糟蹋在一个窄小的圈子里。不过他究竟是血肉之躯，有时也忍不住要飞快地斜瞟一眼，瞟得女人们心里发颤；可等她们发觉他守口如瓶到了极点、对她们可爱的脆弱满怀同情时，反倒都喜欢起他来。
拉尔多太太的领班兼总管事，一个四十五岁、丑得吓人的老姑娘，同骑士合住二楼。她的房门在楼梯口正对着他的门；她的住处同他的一样，是两间屋，一间朝街，一间朝院子。再往上只有顶楼，冬天在那儿晾衣物。楼下住着拉尔多太太的祖父。老人名叫格雷万，年轻时干过私掠船，在印度在西默兹海军上将手下当过差；如今他半身不遂，聋得像块石头。拉尔多太太本人住在领班楼下，她那样崇拜有身份的人，可以说一涉及骑士，她的眼睛便是瞎的。在她眼里，德

===Original===

de Valois was an absolute monarch, a king that could do no wrong; even if one of her own workgirls had been said to be guilty of finding favor in his sight, she would have said, “He is so amiable!”
And so, if M. de Valois, like most people in the provinces, lived in a glass house, it was secret as a robber’s cave so far as he at least was concerned. A born confidant of the little intrigues of the laundry, he never passed the door﻿—which almost always stood ajar﻿—without bringing something for his pets﻿—chocolate, bonbons, ribbons, laces, a gilt cross, and the jokes that grisettes love. Wherefore the little girls adored the Chevalier. Women can tell by instinct whether a man is attracted to anything that wears a petticoat; they know at once the kind of man who enjoys the mere sense of their presence, who never thinks of making blundering demands of repayment for his gallantry. In this respect womankind has a canine faculty; a dog in any company goes straight to the man who respects animals. The Chevalier de Valois in his poverty preserved something of his former life; he was as unable to live without some fair one under his protection as any *grand seigneur* of a bygone age. He clung to the traditions of the *petite maison*. He loved to give to women, and women alone can receive gracefully, perhaps because it is always in their power to repay.
In these days, when every lad on leaving school tries his hand at unearthing symbols or sifting legends, is it not extraordinary that no one has explained that portent, the Courtesan of the Eighteenth Century? What was she but the tournament of the Sixteenth in another shape? In 1550 the knights displayed their prowess for their ladies; in 1750 they displayed their mistresses at Longchamps; today they run their horses over the course. The noble of every age has done his best to invent a life which he, and he only, can live. The painted shoes of the Fourteenth Century are the *talons rouges* of the Eighteenth; the parade of a mistress was one fashion in ostentation; the sentiment of chivalry and the knight errant was another.
The Chevalier de Valois could no longer ruin himself for a mistress, so for bonbons wrapped in bank-bills he politely offered a bag of genuine cracknels; and to the credit of Alençon, be it said, the cracknels caused far more pleasure to the recipients than M.

瓦卢瓦先生是绝对的君主，一位永不犯错的君王；哪怕有人说她自家的女工得了他的青睐，她也只会说：“他多讨人喜欢呀！”
于是，德·瓦卢瓦先生虽同外省多数人一样住在玻璃房子里，至少对他自己来说，这房子秘密得赛过强盗窝。他天生是洗衣坊里种种小风月的心腹，每次经过那扇几乎永远半开的门，从不空着手——总给他的宝贝们捎点什么：巧克力、糖果、缎带、花边、一个镀金十字架，还有女工们爱听的笑话。所以小丫头们个个把骑士当心肝。女人凭本能就知道，一个男人是不是喜欢一切穿裙子的活物；她们一眼就认得出那种只消同她们待在一处便觉受用、从不想着讨还殷勤的男人。在这方面，女性有着犬类般的天赋；一群人里，狗总是径直奔向那位敬重动物的人。瓦卢瓦骑士虽穷，却保留着昔日生活的一点排场；他同往昔任何大贵人一样，膝下没有一位受他保护的美人便活不下去。他死守着“小公馆”（petite maison）的老传统。他爱给女人送礼，而唯有女人才收得优雅，或许因为她们永远还得起。
在如今这个年代，哪个学生一出校门都试着发掘象征、考订传奇，竟没有一个人解说得了那件奇物——十八世纪的交际花，岂非咄咄怪事？她不过是十六世纪比武大会的另一副面孔罢了。1550 年，骑士们为贵妇炫耀武艺；1750 年，他们在隆尚炫示情妇；如今他们赛马。每个时代的贵族都竭力发明一种唯有他过得起的生活。十四世纪的彩绘鞋，便是十八世纪的红高跟；炫示情妇是摆阔的一种时新，骑士风度与游侠义气则是另一种。
瓦卢瓦骑士再也供不起为他倾家荡产的情妇了，于是他彬彬有礼地送上一袋货真价实的脆皮椒盐卷，来代替裹着钞票的糖果；而且应当替阿朗松说句公道话，这脆皮卷给姑娘们带来的欢喜，远比德

===Original===

d’Artois’ presents of carriages or silver-gilt toilet sets ever gave to the fair Duthé. There was not a girl in the laundry but recognized the Chevalier’s fallen greatness, and kept his familiarities in the house a profound secret.
In answer to questions, they always spoke gravely of the Chevalier de Valois; they watched over him. For others he became a venerable gentleman, his life was a flower of sanctity. But at home they would have lighted on his shoulders like parakeets.
The Chevalier liked to know the intimate aspects of family life which laundresses learn; they used to go up to his room of a morning to retail the gossip of the town; he called them his “gazettes in petticoats,” his “living feuilletons.” M. Sartine himself had not such intelligent spies at so cheap a rate, nor yet so loyal in their rascality. Remark, moreover, that the Chevalier thoroughly enjoyed his breakfasts.
Suzanne was one of his favorites. A clever and ambitious girl with the stuff of a Sophie Arnould in her, she was besides as beautiful as the loveliest courtesan that Titian ever prayed to pose against a background of dark velvet as a model for his *Venus*. Her forehead and all the upper part of her face about the eyes were delicately moulded; but the contours of the lower half were cast in a commoner mould. Hers was the beauty of a Normande, fresh, plump, and brilliant-complexioned, with that Rubens fleshiness which should be combined with the muscular development of a Farnese Hercules: This was no Venus de’ Medici, the graceful feminine counterpart of Apollo.
“Well, child,” ’ said the Chevalier, “tell me your adventures little or big.”
The Chevalier’s fatherly benignity with these grisettes would have marked him out anywhere between Paris and Peking. The girls put him in mind of the courtesans of another age, of the illustrious queens of opera of European fame during a good third of the eighteenth century. Certain it is that he who had lived for so long in a world of women now as dead and forgotten as the Jesuits, the buccaneers, the abbés, and the farmers-general, and all great things generally﻿—certain it is that the Chevalier had acquired an irresistible good humor, a gracious ease, an unconcern, with no trace of egoism discernible in it.

阿尔图瓦先生送马车、送镀金梳妆器具给美丽的迪泰带来的欢喜要多。洗衣坊里没有一个姑娘看不出骑士家道中落的底细，也没有一个不把他在坊里的亲昵守成天大的秘密。
有人问起，她们总是一本正经地谈瓦卢瓦骑士；她们护着他。在外人跟前，他是一位可敬的老人家，他的生活是一朵圣洁的花。可一回到家里，她们便要像小鹦鹉似的落上他的肩头。
骑士爱打听那些只有洗衣妇才摸得着的家庭生活的底面；姑娘们惯于早晨上他屋里去，贩卖城里的闲话；他管她们叫“穿裙子的报纸”“活的连载小说”。萨尔蒂纳先生本人，也未曾用这样便宜的价钱雇到这样聪明的密探，而且密探起坏来又这样忠心。再者请注意，骑士的早餐吃得有滋有味。
苏珊娜是他宠爱的姑娘之一。这姑娘又机灵又有野心，一身索菲·阿努尔德的坯子，人又生得美，美得能让提香央求她衬着深色天鹅绒站定，给他《维纳斯》（Venus）当模特。她的额头和眼睛周围的上半张脸，塑得纤秀；下半张脸的轮廓却铸得俗了几分。她是诺曼底型的美人：鲜嫩、丰腴、容光焕发，带着鲁本斯笔下那种肉感，这肉感本该配上法尔内塞的海格立斯（Hercules）那身筋肉才相称：这可不是美第奇的维纳斯——阿波罗那婀娜的女性对应物。
“好啊，孩子，”骑士说，“讲讲你的奇遇，大的小的都行。”
骑士待这些女工如父辈般慈祥，这份本事从巴黎到北京走到哪里都出众。这些姑娘叫他想起另一个时代的名妓，想起十八世纪足有小半世纪名震全欧的歌剧皇后们。的确，他久居女人之国——那国度同耶稣会士、海盗、修道院长、包税商以及一切伟大事物一样，今日已死去、被人遗忘——的确，骑士由此养出了一种不可抗拒的好脾气，一种雍容的自在，一种寻不出半点自私痕迹的达观。

===Original===

So might Jupiter have appeared to Alemena﻿—a king that chooses to be a woman’s dupe, and flings majesty and its thunderbolts to the winds, that he may squander Olympus in follies, and “little suppers,” and feminine extravagance; wishful, of all things, to be far enough away from Juno.
The room in which the Chevalier received company was bare enough, with its shabby bit of tapestry to do duty as a carpet, and very dirty, old-fashioned easy-chairs; the walls were covered with a cheap paper, on which the countenances of Louis XVI and his family, framed in weeping willow, appeared at intervals among funeral urns, bearing the *sublime testament* by way of inscription, amid a whole host of sentimental emblems invented by Royalism under the Terror; but in spite of all this, in spite of the old flowered green silk dressing-gown, in spite of its owner’s air of dilapidation, a certain fragrance of the eighteenth century clung about the Chevalier de Valois as he shaved himself before the old-fashioned toilet glass, covered with cheap lace. All the graceless graces of his youth seemed to reappear; he might have had three hundred thousand francs’ worth of debts to his name, and a chariot at his door. He looked a great man, great as Berthier in the Retreat from Moscow issuing the order of the day to battalions which were no more.
“M. le Chevalier,” Suzanne replied archly, “it seems to me that I have nothing to tell you﻿—you have only to look!”
So saying, she turned and stood sidewise to prove her words by ocular demonstrations; and the Chevalier, deep old gentleman, still holding his razor across his chin, cast his right eye downwards upon the damsel, and pretended to understand.
“Very good, my little pet, we will have a little talk together presently. But you come first, it seems, to me.”
“But, M. le Chevalier, am I to wait till my mother beats me and Mme. Lardot turns me away? If I do not go to Paris at once, I shall never get married here, where the men are so ridiculous.”
“These things cannot be helped, child! Society changes, and women suffer just as much as the nobles from the shocking confusion which ensues. Topsy-turvydom in politics ends in topsy-turvy manners. Alas! woman soon will cease to be woman” (here he took the cotton wool out of his ears to continue his toilet).

===Chinese===

朱庇特显身在阿尔克墨涅面前，想必就是这副模样——一位甘愿受女人欺骗的君王，把威严和雷霆抛到九霄云外，只为了在荒唐事、“小小晚宴”和女人的挥霍上散尽奥林匹斯山，一心只想离朱诺越远越好。
骑士会客的那间屋子够寒碜的：一块破挂毯权充地毯，几把又脏又旧的安乐椅；墙上糊着廉价墙纸，路易十六和他一家的面容，在垂柳框饰里不时露面，穿插着骨灰瓮，铭文是那份“崇高的遗嘱”，还夹着恐怖时期保王党发明的一大堆缠绵图案；然而尽管有这一切，尽管有那件旧绿花绸晨衣，尽管主人一副破落相，当骑士对着那面罩着廉价花边的老式穿衣镜刮脸时，他周身仍旧萦绕着十八世纪的一缕芬芳。他年轻时那些不成体统的风雅，仿佛一一还了魂；他名下兴许背着三十万法郎的债，门口停着一辆四轮马车。他像个大人物，大得像莫斯科撤退中的贝尔蒂埃，向早已不存在的营团发布当日命令。
“骑士先生，”苏珊娜调皮地答道，“依我看，我没什么可告诉您的——您只要瞧一瞧就够了！”
说着，她转过身子，侧身站定，用眼睛看得见的证据来证明自己的话；骑士这位老于风月的绅士，剃刀还横在下巴上，垂下右眼扫了那姑娘一眼，装作心领神会。
“很好，我的小宝贝，回头咱们细谈。不过看来，你是先上我这儿来的。”
“可是骑士先生，难道要我等到我妈揍我、拉尔多太太撵我走么？要是不马上动身去巴黎，我在这儿一辈子嫁不出去，这儿的男人可笑死了。”
“这些事是没法子的，孩子！社会变了，女人同贵族一样，吃够了随后那场大混乱的苦头。政治上的颠倒，到头来便是风化上的颠倒。唉！女人很快就要不成其为女人了。”（说到这儿，他从耳朵里掏出棉花，接着梳洗。）

===Original===

“Women will lose a great deal by plunging into sentiment; they will torture their nerves, and there will be an end of the good old ways of our time, when a little pleasure was desired without blushes, and accepted without more ado, and the vapors” (he polished the earrings with the Negroes’ heads)﻿—“the vapors were only known as a means of getting one’s way; before long they will take the proportions of a complaint only to be cured by an infusion of orange-blossoms.” (The Chevalier burst out laughing.) “Marriage, in short,” he resumed, taking a pair of tweezers to pluck out a gray hair, “marriage will come to be a very dull institution indeed, and it was so joyous in my time. The reign of Louis Quatorze and Louis Quinze, bear this in mind, my child, saw the last of the finest manners in the world.”
“But, M. le Chevalier,” urged the girl, “it is your little Suzanne’s character and reputation that is at stake, and you are not going to forsake her, I hope!”
“What is all this?” cried the Chevalier, with a finishing touch to his hair; “I would sooner lose my name!”
“Ah!” said Suzanne.
“Listen to me, little masquerader.” He sat down in a large, low chair, a *duchess*, as it used to be called, which Mme. Lardot had picked up somewhere for her lodger. Then he drew the magnificent Suzanne to him till she stood between his knees; and Suzanne submitted﻿—Suzanne who held her head so high in the streets, and had refused a score of overtures from admirers in Alençon, not so much from self-respect as in disdain of their pettiness. Suzanne so brazenly made the most of the supposed consequences of her errors, that the old sinner, who had fathomed so many mysteries in persons far more astute than Suzanne, saw the real state of affairs at once. He knew well enough that a grisette does not laugh when disgrace is really in question, but he scorned to throw down the scaffolding of an engaging fib with a touch.
“We are slandering ourselves,” said he, and there was an inimitable subtlety in his smile. “We are as well conducted as the fair one whose name we bear; we can marry without fear. But we do not want to vegetate here; we long for Paris, where charming creatures can be rich if they are clever, and we are not a fool. So we should like to find out whether the City of Pleasure has young Chevaliers de Valois in store for us, and a carriage and diamonds and an opera box.

===Chinese===

“女人们一头栽进感情里，要亏掉好多东西；她们要煎熬自己的神经，我们那个时代的好日子就算到头了——那时候，一点小小的欢乐，要得不红脸，收下不啰嗦，‘忧郁症’”（他擦着那对黑种小人头耳环）——“‘忧郁症’不过是个使性子的由头；往后呢，它要闹成一种病，一种只有橙花汤剂才治得好的病。”（骑士放声大笑。）“总而言之，”他接着说，一面拿起小镊子拔一根白发，“婚姻要变成一种乏味透顶的制度了，可在我们那时候，它多么快活。路易十四和路易十五的朝代——记住这句，我的孩子——是世上最好的风度的绝响。”
“可是骑士先生，”姑娘急道，“赌上的可是您小苏珊娜的名声品行，您总不至于把她丢下不管吧！”
“这是从何说起！”骑士叫道，往头发上作最后一道工序；“我宁肯丢掉我的姓氏！”
“啊！”苏珊娜道。
“听我说，小戏子。”他坐进一把宽大的矮椅——旧时称作“公爵夫人”（duchesse）的那种，是拉尔多太太不知从哪儿替她的房客搜罗来的。随后他把光艳照人的苏珊娜拉到跟前，让她立在自己两膝之间；苏珊娜顺从了——这个在街上把头抬得老高、回绝了阿朗松一打献殷勤的苏珊娜，倒不是出于自重，而是瞧不起他们的小家子气。苏珊娜把她那凭空捏造的失足后张扬到这步田地，老罪人——他参透过多少比苏珊娜精明十倍的心思——一眼便看穿了实情。他心里雪亮：一个女工真到了丢脸的关头是笑不出来的；可他不屑于抬一根指头，去推倒这座动人谎言搭起的脚手架。
“咱们这是往自己脸上抹黑呢，”他说，笑意里透着无从摹仿的精细。“咱们的品行端方得很，同那位与咱们同名的小娘子一样；咱们嫁人嫁得心安理得。只是咱们不愿在此地发霉；咱们向往巴黎，那儿可人的尤物只要有头脑便发得了财，而咱们又不是傻子。所以咱们想打听打听，欢乐之城肯不肯给咱们预备下年轻的瓦卢瓦骑士们，预备下马车、钻石和剧院的包厢。

===Original===

There are Russians and English and Austrians that are bringing millions to spend in Paris, and some of that money mamma settled on us as a marriage portion when she gave us our good looks. And besides, we are patriotic; we should like to help France to find her own money in these gentlemen’s pockets. Eh! eh! my dear little devil’s lamb, all this is not bad. The neighbors will cry out upon you a little at first perhaps, but success will make everything right. The real crime, my child, is poverty; and you and I both suffer for it. As we are not lacking in intelligence, we thought we might turn our dear little reputation to account to take in an old bachelor, but the old bachelor, sweetheart, knows the alpha and omega of woman’s wiles; which is to say, that you would find it easier to put a grain of salt upon a sparrow tail than to persuade me to believe that I have had any share in your affair.
“Go to Paris, my child, go at the expense of a bachelor’s vanity; I am not going to hinder you, I will help you, for the old bachelor, Suzanne, is the cashbox provided by nature for a young girl. But do not thrust me into the affair. Now, listen, my queen, understanding life so well as you do﻿—you see, you might do me a good deal of harm and give me trouble; harm, because you might spoil my marriage in a place where people are so particular; trouble on your account, because you will get yourself in a scrape for nothing, a scrape entirely of your own invention, sly girl; and you know, my pet, that I have no money left, I am as poor as a church mouse. Ah! if I were to marry Mlle. Cormon, if I were rich again, I would certainly rather have you than Césarine. You were always fine gold enough to gild lead, it seemed to me; you were made to be a great lord’s love; and as I knew you were a clever girl, I am not at all surprised by this trick of yours, I expected as much. For a girl, this means that you burn your boats. It is no common mind, my angel, that can do it; and for that reason you have my esteem,” and he bestowed confirmation upon her cheek after the manner of a bishop, with two fingers.
“But, M. le Chevalier, I do assure you that you are mistaken, and﻿—” she blushed, and dared not finish her sentence, at a glance he had seen through her, and read her plans from beginning to end.
“Yes, I understand, you wish me to believe you. Very well, I believe.

===Chinese===

如今多少俄国人、英国人、奥地利人正带着百万家财到巴黎去花，其中一份，妈妈在发给咱们好相貌的时候，早给咱们充了嫁妆。再说咱们还是爱国者呢；咱们乐意帮法兰西从这几位先生的口袋里，找回她自己的一份钱。嗳！嗳！我亲爱的小魔羊羔，这套打算不赖吧。左邻右舍起初兴许要嚷嚷你几句，可是一成功，什么都顺当了。真正的罪过，孩子，是穷；你我都在受这份罪。既然咱们脑力不缺，便想着动用咱们亲爱的小名声，去套住一个老单身汉；可是老单身汉哪，心肝，对女人的手腕，从头一个字母数到末一个字母全知道；这就是说，要叫我相信我在你的事里搭过一脚，比把一粒盐搁上麻雀尾巴还难。
“上巴黎去吧，孩子，去花一个单身汉的虚荣钱；我不拦你，我还要帮你，因为老单身汉呀，苏珊娜，就是老天爷给年轻姑娘备下的钱匣子。只是别把我扯进去。听着，我的王后，你这么通晓人情——你瞧，你兴许害得了我，给我惹麻烦；害我，因为你可能坏了我在这个人人讲究的地方的亲事；惹麻烦，是因为你要白白陷进一场丑闻，一场全凭你自己编排的丑闻，鬼丫头；你知道，宝贝，我已经一个铜子不剩，穷得像教堂里的老鼠。啊！我要是娶了科尔蒙小姐（Mlle. Cormon），要是我重新阔起来，我要的是你，不是塞扎里娜。你从来都是足赤的金子，镀得动铅；依我看，你天生是做贵人情妇的坯子；我早知道你是个聪明姑娘，所以你这点儿手腕我一点不吃惊，我早料着了。对一个姑娘家，这就叫破釜沉舟。孩子，能下这个决心的，可绝不是寻常脑瓜；就冲这一条，我敬重你。”说罢，他伸出两根指头，照主教的样子，在她的脸颊上行了坚振礼。
“可是骑士先生，我担保您弄错了，而且——”她红了脸，不敢把话说完；他一眼便看穿了她，从头至尾读出了她的全盘计划。
“是，我明白，你要我相信你。好吧，我信。

===Original===

But take my advice and go to M. du Bousquier. You have taken M. du Bousquier’s linen home from the wash for five or six months, have you not?﻿—Very good. I do not ask to know what has happened between you; but I know *him*, he is vain, he is an old bachelor, he is very rich, he has an income of two thousand five hundred livres, and spends less than eight hundred. If you are the clever girl that I take you for, you will find your way to Paris at his expense. Go to him, my pet, twist him round your fingers, and of all things, be supple as silk, and make a double twist and a knot at every word; he is just the man to be afraid of a scandal; and if he knows that you can make him sit on the stool of repentance﻿—In short, you understand, threaten to apply to the ladies of the charitable fund. He is ambitious besides. Well and good, with a wife to help him there should be nothing beyond a man’s reach; and are you not handsome enough and clever enough to make your husband’s fortune? Why, plague take it, you might hold your own with a court lady.”
The Chevalier’s last words let the light into Suzanne’s brain; she was burning with impatience to rush off to du Bousquier; but as she could not hurry away too abruptly, she helped the Chevalier to dress, asking questions about Paris as she did so. As for the Chevalier, he saw that his remarks had taken effect, and gave Suzanne an excuse to go, asking her to tell Césarine to bring up the chocolate that Mme. Lardot made for him every morning, and Suzanne forthwith slipped off in search of her prey.
And here follows du Bousquier’s biography.﻿—He came of an old Alençon family in a middle rank between the burghers and the country squires. On the death of his father, a magistrate in the criminal court, he was left without resource, and, like most ruined provincials, betook himself to Paris to seek his fortune. When the Revolution broke out, du Bousquier was a man of affairs; and in those days (in spite of the Republicans, who are all up in arms for the honesty of their government), the word “affairs” was used very loosely. Political spies, jobbers, and contractors, the men who arranged with the syndics of communes for the sale of the property of émigrés, and then bought up land at low prices to sell again﻿—all these folk, like ministers and generals, were men of affairs.

===Chinese===

不过听我的劝，去找杜·布斯基耶。杜·布斯基耶先生的送洗衣物，你已经送回家去五六个月了，是不是？——很好。你们中间有过什么，我不问；可我了解他这个人：他虚荣，他是个老单身汉，他很有钱，两千五百里弗尔的年进项，花的不到八百。你要真是我看准的聪明姑娘，你就能花他的钱进巴黎。去呀，宝贝，把他绕在手指头上玩，千万要柔得像丝，一个字打一个双结再加一道绕；他正是那种最怕闹出丑闻的男人；他一旦知道你能叫他坐上忏悔凳——总之，你懂的，就吓唬他要去求善济金的太太们。何况他还有野心。那敢情好；有个贤内助的男人，天下没有够不着的事；凭你这副相貌、这副头脑，还怕帮丈夫发不了财？嗐，见鬼，就是同宫廷命妇斗一斗，你也未必输。”
骑士末了这几句，点亮了苏珊娜的脑子；她心急火燎，恨不得一步跨到杜·布斯基耶跟前；可又不好走得太急，便一面伺候骑士穿衣，一面打听巴黎的种种。骑士呢，眼见自己的话见了效，便递给苏珊娜一个抽身的由头，托她叫塞扎里娜把拉尔多太太每天早晨替他调的那杯巧克力端上来；苏珊娜立刻溜出门去寻她的猎物了。
下面是杜·布斯基耶的传。——他出身阿朗松一个旧家，门第介乎市民与乡绅之间。父亲是刑事法庭的推事，死后他断了生计，便同多数破了产的外省人一样，上巴黎去闯运。大革命爆发时，杜·布斯基耶是“办事情的”；在那个年头（尽管共和党人个个跳出来替自家政府的清廉拍胸脯），“事情”二字用得极宽。政治密探、投机掮客、军需承包人，同乡镇公会理事串通着倒卖流亡者产业、再贱进贵出土地的各路人马——这些角色，同部长、将军一样，统统算“办事情的”。

===Original===

From 1793 to 1799 du Bousquier held contracts to supply the army with forage and provisions. During those years he lived in a splendid mansion; he was one of the great capitalists of the time; he went shares with Ouvrard; kept open house and led the scandalous life of the times. A Cincinnatus, reaping where he had not sowed, and rich with stolen rations and sacks of corn, he kept *petites maisons* and a bevy of mistresses, and gave fine entertainments to the directors of the Republic. Citizen du Bousquier was one of Barras’ intimates; he was on the best of terms with Fouché, and hand and glove with Bernadotte. He thought to be a Minister of State one day, and threw himself heart and soul into the party that secretly plotted against Bonaparte before the battle of Marengo. And but for Kellermann’s charge and the death of Desaix, du Bousquier would have played a great part in the state. He was one of the upper members of the permanent staff of the promiscuous government which was driven by Napoleon’s luck to vanish into the side-scenes of 1793.1
The victory unexpectedly won by stubborn fighting ended in the downfall of this party; they had placards ready printed, and were only waiting for the First Consul’s defeat to proclaim a return to the principles of the Mountain.
Du Bousquier, feeling convinced that a victory was impossible, had two special messengers on the battlefield, and speculated with the larger part of his fortune for a fall in the funds. The first courier came with the news that Mélas was victorious; but the second arriving four hours afterwards, at night, brought the tidings of the Austrian defeat. Du Bousquier cursed Kellermann and Desaix; the First Consul owed him millions, he dared not curse him. But between the chance of making millions on the one hand, and stark ruin on the other, he lost his head. For several days he was half idiotic; he had undermined his constitution with excesses to such an extent that the thunderbolt left him helpless. He had something to hope from the settlement of his claims upon the Government; but in spite of bribes, he was made to feel the weight of Napoleon’s displeasure against army contractors who speculated on his defeat. M. de Fermon, so pleasantly nicknamed “*Fermons la caisse*,” left du Bousquier without a penny.

===Chinese===

从 1793 年到 1799 年，杜·布斯基耶承办军队的草料粮秣。那几年他住着富丽堂皇的宅第，是当时的大资本家之一；他同乌弗拉尔合伙；家里食客常满，过的净是那个时代伤风败俗的日子。他是个不折不扣的辛辛纳图斯——不耕而获，靠偷来的给养和一袋袋粮食发家——养着几处“小公馆”、一群情妇，盛宴款待共和国的督政们。杜·布斯基耶公民是巴拉斯的知己之一；同富歇交情顶好，同贝尔纳多特打得火热。他盘算着有朝一日当上国务大臣，便把一颗心整个押给了马伦哥战役之前暗中倒波拿巴的那一党。若不是凯勒曼那一场冲锋和德赛之死，杜·布斯基耶原能在国家舞台上扮演一个要角。他是那个大杂烩政府常设班底里的上层人物之一——那个政府被拿破仑的运气逼得退进了 1793 年的侧幕。1
这一场靠死打硬拼意外到手的胜利，结果是这一党的垮台；他们的布告都印好了，只等第一执政一败，便宣告回到山岳派的诸项原则上去。
杜·布斯基耶认定胜利绝无可能，在战场上派了两名专差，拿家产的大半去赌公债落价。第一个信使带来梅拉斯得胜的消息；可四个钟头之后，深夜里赶到的第二个，带来了奥军溃败的音讯。杜·布斯基耶咒骂凯勒曼和德赛；第一执政欠着他几百万，他不敢骂。可是一边是几百万的进项，一边是净光的绝境，他昏了头。一连好几天他半痴半呆；纵欲早把他的身子掏空了，这一记闷雷劈下来，他竟毫无招架。他还指望政府清偿他名下的债权；可是尽管暗中使钱，他还是结结实实领教了拿破仑的雷霆——那是对拿他战败做投机的军需商的雷霆。费尔蒙先生——外号起得妙，叫“费尔蒙·拉·凯斯”（Fermons la caisse，意即“关门歇业”）——弄得杜·布斯基耶一个子儿不剩。

===Original===

The First Consul was even more incensed by the immorality of his private life and his connection with Barras and Bernadotte than by his speculations on the Bourse; he erased M. du Bousquier’s name from the list of Receivers-general, on which a last remnant of credit had placed him for Alençon.
Of all his former wealth, nothing now remained to du Bousquier save an income of twelve hundred francs from the funds, an investment entirely due to chance, which saved him from actual want. His creditors, knowing nothing of the results of his liquidation, only left him enough in consols to bring in a thousand francs per annum; but their claims were paid in full after all, when the outstanding debts had been collected, and the Hôtel de Bauséant, du Bousquier’s town house, sold besides. So, after a close shave of bankruptcy, the sometime speculator emerged with his name intact. Preceded by a tremendous reputation due to his relations with former heads of government departments, his manner of life, his brief day of authority, and final ruin through the First Consul, the man interested the city of Alençon, where Royalism was secretly predominant. Du Bousquier, exasperated against Bonaparte, with his tales of the First Consul’s pettiness, of Joséphine’s lax morals, and a whole store of anecdotes of ten years of Revolution, seen from within, met with a good reception.
It was about this period of his life that du Bousquier, now well over his fortieth year, came out as a bachelor of thirty-six. He was of medium height, fat as became a contractor, and willing to display a pair of calves that would have done credit to a gay and gallant attorney. He had strongly marked features; a flattened nose with tufts of hair in the equine nostrils, bushy black brows, and eyes beneath them that looked out shrewd as M. de Talleyrand’s own, though they had lost something of their brightness. He wore his brown hair very long, and retained the side-whiskers (*nageoires*, as they were called) of the time of the Republic. You had only to look at his fingers, tufted at every joint, or at the blue knotted veins that stood out upon his hands, to see the unmistakable signs of a very remarkable muscular development; and, in truth, he had the chest of the Farnese Hercules, and shoulders fit to bear the burden of the national debt; you never see such shoulders nowadays.

===Chinese===

第一执政对这个人私生活的伤风败俗、同巴拉斯和贝尔纳多特的瓜葛，比对他在交易所里的投机还要恼火；他把杜·布斯基耶先生的名字从总收税官的名单上勾掉了——凭着他最后一点残存的信誉，这个位子原已替他在阿朗松安排停当。
昔日那样豪阔的家产，如今杜·布斯基耶只剩下公债上一千二百法郎的年息；这笔产业全凭运气，才救他免于当真挨饿。他的债主们不明清算的结果，只给他留下年息一千法郎的统一公债；可是外账收清之后，债到底连本带利全都偿了，杜·布斯基耶城里的博塞昂公馆也一并卖掉了。于是，在破产的鬼门关前打了个照面，这位昔日的投机家全身而退，名声毫发未损。他同前几届政府首脑的关系、他的排场、他短短一度的当权和他被第一执政搞垮的下场，替他垫起了赫赫的名头，阿朗松全城对这个人来了兴趣——那座城暗地里以保王为正宗。杜·布斯基耶恨波拿巴入骨，肚里装着第一执政如何小气、约瑟芬如何风流、还有十年革命从里往外看的成堆轶闻，处处吃得开。
正是在他一生的这个时期，年过四十的杜·布斯基耶，以三十六岁单身汉的面目重新出台。他中等身材，胖得合乎承包商的身份，还乐意炫耀两条小腿，那两条腿放在一位风流倜傥的诉讼代理人身上也不丢份。他脸上轮廓分明：一只塌鼻子，马鼻孔里钻出几撮毛，两道浓黑的眉毛，眉下一双眼睛，精明得不下于德·塔列朗本人，只是少了些光采。他棕色的头发留得很长，还留着共和国年代的连鬓胡（时人唤作“鱼鳍”）。你只消看看他那每个关节都长毛的手指，或看看手背上条条盘结的青筋，便看得出那副异乎寻常的筋骨的无误印记；的的确确，他有法尔内塞海格立斯的胸膛，有一副扛得起国债的肩膀；这样的肩膀如今是见不着了。

===Original===

His was a luxuriant virility admirably described by an eighteenth century phrase which is scarcely intelligible today; the gallantry of a bygone age would have summed up du Bousquier as a “payer of arrears”﻿—*un vrai payeur d’arrérages*.
Yet, as in the case of the Chevalier de Valois, there were sundry indications at variance with the ex-contractor’s general appearance. His vocal powers, for instance, were not in keeping with his muscles; not that it was the mere thread of a voice which sometimes issues from the throats of such two-footed seals; on the contrary, it was loud but husky, something like the sound of a saw cutting through damp, soft wood; it was, in fact, the voice of a speculator brought to grief. For a long while du Bousquier wore the costume in vogue in the days of his glory: the boots with turned-down tops, the while silk stockings, the short cloth breeches, ribbed with cinnamon color, the blue coat, the waistcoat à la Robespierre.
His hatred of the First Consul should have been a sort of passport into the best Royalist houses of Alençon; but the seven or eight families that made up the local Faubourg Saint-Germain into which the Chevalier de Valois had the entrance, held aloof. Almost from the first, du Bousquier had aspired to marry one Mlle. Armande, whose brother was one of the most esteemed nobles of the town; he thought to make this brother play a great part in his own schemes, for he was dreaming of a brilliant return match in politics. He met with a refusal, for which he consoled himself with such compensation as he might find among some half-score of retired manufacturers of *Point d’Alençon*, owners of grass lands or cattle, or wholesale linen merchants, thinking that among these chance might put a good match in his way. Indeed, the old bachelor had centered all his hopes on a prospective fortunate marriage, which a man, eligible in so many ways, might fairly expect to make. For he was not without a certain financial acumen, of which not a few availed themselves. He pointed out business speculations as a ruined gambler gives hints to new hands; and he was expert at discovering the resources, chances, and management of a concern. People looked upon him as a good administrator.

===Chinese===

他那一身旺盛的雄风，十八世纪有句现成话形容得妙，今天几乎没人听懂了：旧时代的风流人物准会给杜·布斯基耶下个总评，叫“补欠账的好手”——un vrai payeur d'arrérages。
然而，同瓦卢瓦骑士的情形一样，这位前承包商浑身的气象底下，也有几处同总体外貌拧着来的迹象。比方说，他的嗓门便同他的筋骨不相称；倒不是说那是两脚海豹喉咙里偶尔挤出来的那种细线嗓；恰恰相反，又响又哑，有点像锯子锯又潮又软的木头；说到底，那是一个折了本的投机家的嗓音。很长一段时期，杜·布斯基耶还穿着他走红年代时兴的那套行头：翻口长统靴、白丝袜、肉桂色棱纹短裤、蓝礼服、罗伯斯庇尔式背心。
他对第一执政的仇恨，本该是他进入阿朗松几家头等保王人家的一纸路引；可是组成当地圣日耳曼郊区的那七八户人家——瓦卢瓦骑士登得了门的人家——偏偏同他保持距离。几乎从一开头，杜·布斯基耶便存心要娶一位阿尔芒德小姐（Mlle. Armande），她哥哥是城里最受敬重的一位贵族；他打算让这位哥哥在他的棋盘上充当一枚要子，因为他正做着政治上一场漂亮翻身仗的梦。他碰了钉子，只好退而求其次，到十几户退休的阿朗松花边厂主、草地业主、牛马贩子和布匹批发商中间寻些补偿，心想机缘或许会在这中间替他撮合一门好亲。的确，这位老单身汉把全部指望都押在一门如意亲事上了——一个各方面都拿得出手的男人，本来也尽配得上这份指望。他并非没有几分生财的精明，沾过这光的人不在少数。他给人指点投机门径，活像输光了的老赌客给新手支招；一家企业的资源、机缘与经营，他没有摸不透的。人们把他看作一位理财的好手。

===Original===

It was an often-discussed question whether he should not be mayor of Alençon, but the recollection of his Republican jobberies spoiled his chances, and he was never received at the prefecture.
Every successive government, even the government of the Hundred Days, declined to give him the coveted appointment, which would have assured his marriage with an elderly spinster whom he now had in his mind. It was his detestation of the Imperial Government that drove him into the Royalist camp, where he stayed in spite of insults there received; but when the Bourbons returned, and still he was excluded from the prefecture, that final rebuff filled him with a hatred deep as the profound secrecy in which he wrapped it. Outwardly, he remained patiently faithful to his opinions; secretly, he became the leader of the Liberal party in Alençon, the invisible controller of elections; and, by his cunningly devised manoeuvres and underhand methods, he worked no little harm to the restored Monarchy.
When a man is reduced to live through his intellect alone, his hatred is something as quiet as a little stream; insignificant to all appearance, but unfailing. This was the case with du Bousquier. His hatred was like a Negro’s, so placid, so patient, that it deceives the enemy. For fifteen years he brooded over a revenge which no victory, not even the Three Days of July 1830, could sate.
When the Chevalier sent Suzanne to du Bousquier, he had his own reasons for so doing. The Liberal and the Royalist divined each other, in spite of the skilful dissimulation which hid their common aim from the rest of the town.
The two old bachelors were rivals. Both of them had planned to marry the Demoiselle Cormon, whose name came up in the course of the Chevalier’s conversation with Suzanne. Both of them, engrossed by their idea, and masquerading in indifference, were waiting for the moment when some chance should deliver the old maid to one or other of them. And the fact that they were rivals in this way would have been enough to make enemies of the pair even if each had not been the living embodiment of a political system.
Men take their color from their time. This pair of rivals is a case in point; the historic tinge of their characters stood out in strong contrast in their talk, their ideas, their costume.

===Chinese===

他该不该当阿朗松市长，是城里议了又议的题目；可是他当年共和党投机营生的名声坏了事，省长官邸从没接待过他。
历届政府，连百日王朝的政府也算上，都拒绝给他那个眼馋的职位，而那职位本可保他娶上如今记挂在心里的那位老姑娘。正是对帝国政府的憎恶把他赶进了保王营垒，在那里他忍气吞声待着不走；可波旁王室复辟之后，他仍被排斥在省长官邸之外，这最后一记冷落，在他心里灌满了深仇，深得同他包藏这仇恨的秘而不宣一样。表面上，他耐心地忠于自己的见解；暗地里，他成了阿朗松自由党的领袖，选举的无形操纵者；凭着他巧设的计谋和地下的手段，他给复辟王朝使了不少坏。
一个人落到只靠脑力活命的地步，他的仇恨便如一条小溪，看似不起眼，却永不干涸。杜·布斯基耶正是如此。他的仇恨像黑人的仇恨，那样平静，那样有耐心，能骗过仇家。十五年来他孕育着一场复仇，任什么胜利——连 1830 年七月那三日——也填它不满。
骑士把苏珊娜打发到杜·布斯基耶那里去，自有他的道理。自由党同保王党彼此心照，尽管两人巧妙做作，把共同的目标瞒过了全城。
两个老单身汉是情敌。两人都打着娶科尔蒙小姐的主意——骑士同苏珊娜闲谈时，这个名字自己冒了出来。两人都被这个念头盘踞，又都装作满不在乎，都在等某个机缘把这位老姑娘送到这一个或那一个手里。单凭这层情敌关系，就足够叫这两个人结成冤家，何况他们还各自是一种政治制度的活化身。
人是从他的时代取得颜色的。这对情敌便是一例；两人性格里历史的色调，在谈吐、思想、衣着上形成强烈的对照。

===Original===

The one, blunt and energetic, with his burly abrupt ways, curt speech, dark looks, dark hair, and dark complexion, alarming in appearance, but impotent in reality as insurrection, was the Republic personified; the other, bland and polished, elegant and fastidious, gaining his ends slowly but surely by diplomacy, and never unmindful of good taste, was the typical old-world courtier. They met on the same ground almost every evening. It was a rivalry always courteous and urbane on the part of the Chevalier, less ceremonious on du Bousquier’s, though he kept within the limits prescribed by Alençon, for he had no wish to be driven ignominiously from the field. The two men understood each other well; but no one else saw what was going on. In spite of the minute and curious interest which provincials take in the small details of which their lives are made up, no one so much as suspected that the two men were rivals.
M. le Chevalier’s position was somewhat the stronger; he had never proposed for Mlle. Cormon, whereas du Bousquier had declared himself after a rebuff from one of the noblest families, and had met with a second refusal. Still, the Chevalier thought so well of his rival’s chances, that, he considered it worth while to deal him a *coup de Jarnac*, a treacherous thrust from a weapon as finely tempered as Suzanne. He had fathomed du Bousquier; and, as will shortly be seen, he was not mistaken in any of his conjectures.
Suzanne tripped away down the Rue du Cours, along the Rue de la Porte de Séez and the Rue du Bercail to the Rue du Cygne, where du Bousquier, five years ago, had bought a small countrified house built of the gray stone of the district, which is used like granite in Normandy, or Breton schist in the West. The sometime forage-contractor had established himself there in more comfort than any other house in the town could boast, for he had brought with him some relics of past days of splendor; but provincial manners and customs were slowly darkening the glory of the fallen Sardanapalus. The vestiges of past luxury looked about as much out of place in the house as a chandelier in a barn. Harmony, which links the works of man or of God together, was lacking in all things large or small.

===Chinese===

一个粗豪有力，大大咧咧，说话唐突，眉发乌黑，面色深暗，样子吓人，实际却同起义一样虚张声势——他是共和国的化身；另一个温文尔雅，优雅挑剔，靠外交手腕缓慢而稳当地达到目的，从不忘却良好的趣味——他是典型的旧时代朝臣。两人几乎每晚在同一处场合碰面。这场竞争，骑士一方永远客气斯文，杜·布斯基耶一方便不那么讲礼数了，不过他守着阿朗松划定的界限，因为他可不打算被人扫地出门。两人彼此摸底；旁人却什么也看不出。外省人虽对构成他们生活的芝麻细节怀着细致入微的好奇，却无一人疑心这两个男人是情敌。
骑士先生的地位略占上风：他从未向科尔蒙小姐求过婚，而杜·布斯基耶是在最显赫的门户碰了钉子之后才转而表态的，跟着又吃了第二个闭门羹。尽管如此，骑士对情敌的机会估计得很高，认为值得赏他一记“雅尔纳克式致命一击”（coup de Jarnac），一记背信的突刺，用的兵器经过苏珊娜这样精良的淬火。他早把杜·布斯基耶摸透了；而下文很快可见，他的种种推断没有一条落空。
苏珊娜迈着轻快的步子下了库尔街，顺着塞兹门街和贝尔凯街走到天鹅街；五年前，杜·布斯基耶在那里买了一所乡土气的小房子，用的是本地灰石——在诺曼底这石头当花岗岩使，在西部当布列塔尼页岩使。昔日的草料承包商在那儿安顿下来，其舒适程度全城无一处可比，因为他随身带来了几分昔日豪阔的遗物；只是外省的风俗习惯，正慢慢熏黑这位失势的萨丹纳帕路斯的荣光。往日奢侈的残迹搁在这房子里，就像谷仓里挂一盏枝形吊灯一样格格不入。和谐——那把人间或上天的造物连缀在一起的东西——在大小诸物上一概缺席。

===Original===

A ewer with a metal lid, such as you only see on the outskirts of Brittany, stood on a handsome chest of drawers; and while the bedroom floor was covered with a fine carpet, the window-curtains displayed a flower pattern only known to cheap printed cottons. The stone mantelpiece, daubed over with paint, was out of all keeping with a handsome clock disgraced by a shabby pair of candlesticks. Local talent had made an unsuccessful attempt to paint the doors in vivid contrasts of startling colors; while the staircase, ascended by all and sundry in muddy boots, had not been painted at all. In short, du Bousquier’s house, like the time which he represented, was a confused mixture of grandeur and squalor.
Du Bousquier was regarded as well-to-do, but he led the parasitical life of the Chevalier de Valois, and he is always rich enough that spends less than his income. His one servant was a country bumpkin, a dull-witted youth enough; but he had been trained, by slow degrees, to suit du Bousquier’s requirements, until he had learned, much as an orangutan might learn, to scour floors, black boots, brush clothes, and to come for his master of an evening with a lantern if it was dark, and a pair of sabots if it rained. On great occasions, du Bousquier made him discard the blue-checked cotton blouse with loose sagging pockets behind, which always bulged with a handkerchief, a clasp knife, apples, or “stickjaw.” Arrayed in a regulation suit of clothes, he accompanied his master to wait at table, and over-ate himself afterwards with the other servants. Like many other mortals, René had only stuff enough in him for one vice, and his was gluttony. Du Bousquier made a reward of this service, and in return his Breton factotum was absolutely discreet.
“What, have you come our way, miss?” René asked when he saw Suzanne in the doorway. “It is not your day; we have not got any linen for Mme. Lardot.”
“Big stupid!” laughed the fair Suzanne, as she went up the stairs, leaving René to finish a porringer full of buckwheat bannocks boiled in milk.
Du Bousquier was still in bed, ruminating his plans for fortune. To him, as to all who have squeezed the orange of pleasure, there was nothing left but ambition. Ambition, like gambling, is inexhaustible. And, moreover, given a good constitution, the passions of the brain will always outlive the heart’s passions.
“Here I am!”

===Chinese===

一把带金属盖的水壶——那玩意儿只在布列塔尼边地见得着——摆在一口漂亮的五斗柜上；卧室地上铺着讲究的地毯，窗帘却是只有廉价印花布才认得的花样。石砌壁炉架胡乱涂过一层漆，同一座蒙着一对寒酸烛台的漂亮座钟全不相配。本地才艺曾试图把门漆成触目的强烈对比色，没有成功；而那道人人穿着泥靴子踩的楼梯，压根儿没漆过。总而言之，杜·布斯基耶的房子，同他所代表的那个时代一样，是豪阔与寒碜的一锅糊涂粥。
杜·布斯基耶被看作殷实人家，可他过的却是同瓦卢瓦骑士一样的食客生涯——花的比挣的少的人，永远算得上阔。他唯一的仆人是个乡下憨小子，脑子相当不灵；可他被慢慢调教出来，事事合乎主人的需要，终于学会了——学法同猩猩学戏差不多——刷地板、擦靴子、刷衣裳，天黑了提着灯笼、下雨了拎着木鞋去接主人。逢到大日子，杜·布斯基耶便叫他脱下那件背后垂着松垮口袋的蓝格布罩衫——口袋里永远塞着手帕、折刀、苹果或者“黏牙糖”——换上一套规规矩矩的衣服，跟着主人去侍席，事后同别的仆人一起大吃一顿撑个半死。同许多凡人一样，勒内身上只装得下一样毛病，他的毛病是贪嘴。杜·布斯基耶把这份差使当作赏赐，他的布列塔尼总管事也就报以绝对的守口如瓶。
“哟，小姐今天怎么上我们这儿来了？”勒内看见苏珊娜站在门口，问道。“今天不是你的日子；拉尔多太太的衣物我们一件也没有。”
“大笨蛋！”美丽的苏珊娜笑着上了楼，丢下勒内去对付他那满满一锅牛奶煮的荞麦饼。
杜·布斯基耶还躺在床上，翻来覆去盘算他的发财大计。对他来说——对一切挤干了享乐这只橙子的人来说——剩下的只有野心。野心同赌钱一样，是永不枯竭的。何况，只要底子好，头脑的情欲永远比心脏的情欲活得长。
“我来啦！”

===Original===

said Suzanne, sitting down on the bed; the curtain-rings grated along the rods as she swept them sharply back with an imperious gesture.
“*Quésaco*, my charmer?” asked du Bousquier, sitting upright.
“Monsieur,” Suzanne began, with much gravity, “you must be surprised to see me come in this way; but, under the circumstances, it is no use my minding what people will say.”
“What is all this about?” asked du Bousquier, folding his arms.
“Why, do you not understand?” returned Suzanne. “I know” (with an engaging little pout), “I know how ridiculous it is when a poor girl comes to bother a man about things that you think mere trifles. But if you really know me, monsieur, if you only knew all that I would do for a man, if he cared about me as I could care about you, you would never repent of marrying me. It is not that I could be of so much use to you *here*, by the way; but if we went to Paris, you should see how far I could bring a man of spirit with such brains as yours, and especially just now, when they are remaking the Government from top to bottom, and the foreigners are the masters. Between ourselves, does this thing in question really matter after all? Is it not a piece of good fortune for which you would be glad to pay a good deal one of these days? For whom are you going to think and work?”
“For myself, to be sure!” du Bousquier answered brutally.
“Old monster! you shall never be a father!” said Suzanne, with a ring in her voice which turned the words to a prophecy and a curse.
“Come, Suzanne, no nonsense; I am dreaming still, I think.”
“What more do you want in the way of reality?” cried Suzanne, rising to her feet. Du Bousquier scrubbed his head with his cotton nightcap, which he twisted round and round with a fidgety energy that told plainly of prodigious mental ferment.
“He actually believes it!” Suzanne said within herself. “And his vanity is tickled. Good Lord, how easy it is to take them in!”
“Suzanne! What the deuce do you want me to do? It is so extraordinary﻿ ﻿… I that thought﻿—The fact is.﻿ ﻿… But no, no, it can’t be﻿—”
“Do you mean that you cannot marry me?”
“Oh, as to that, no. I am not free.”
“Is it Mlle. Armande or Mlle. Cormon, who have both refused you already? Look here, M. du Bousquier, it is not as if I was obliged to get gendarmes to drag you to the registrar’s office to save my character.

===Chinese===

苏珊娜说着，往床沿上一坐；她一个不容分说的手势把床帏猛地拉开，帐圈在杆上刮出一串声响。
“‘这是什么意思’（Quésaco），我的小可人？”杜·布斯基耶坐起身来问。
“先生，”苏珊娜郑重其事地开了腔，“我这般闯进来，您一定奇怪；可是事到如今，人家闲话什么，我也顾不得了。”
“这是唱的哪一出？”杜·布斯基耶抱起胳膊问。
“怎么，您不明白？”苏珊娜答道。“我知道”（她卖俏地一撅嘴），“我知道一个穷姑娘来麻烦一个男人，说的又是您眼里鸡毛蒜皮的事，这有多可笑。可您要是真了解我，先生，要是您知道，一个男人只要像我能疼您这样疼我，我什么都肯为他做，那您娶了我，一辈子不会后悔。倒不是说我在此地能帮您多大忙；可咱们要是去了巴黎，您就瞧吧，凭您这样的头脑，一个有血性的男人让我扶一把能走多远——尤其眼下，人家正把政府从上到下翻新改组，外国人当家做主。咱们私房说说，这档子事当真要紧么？这可不是一注好运么？往后哪一天，您花大价钱买还买不来呢。您还要为谁着想、为谁卖力气？”
“当然为我自己！”杜·布斯基耶粗声恶气地答道。
“老怪物！你一辈子当不成爹！”苏珊娜说，嗓音里带着一种腔调，把这句话变成了预言兼诅咒。
“得了，苏珊娜，别胡闹；我这是做梦呢，大概。”
“您还要什么比这更真的实事？”苏珊娜叫起来，站直了身子。杜·布斯基耶抓着布睡帽在脑袋上搓，一圈又一圈，那股子焦躁劲儿，明明白白显出心里翻江倒海。
“他倒真信了！”苏珊娜心里说。“他的虚荣心也痒了。老天爷，蒙这些男人多容易！”
“苏珊娜！你到底要我怎么样？这太离奇了……我原以为……实情是……不，不，不会是——”
“您是说不肯娶我？”
“哦，说到娶，不行。我不自由。”
“是阿尔芒德小姐还是科尔蒙小姐？她们俩不都回绝过您了么？听我说，杜·布斯基耶先生，我又不是非得叫宪兵拖您到户籍官跟前保全我的名声不可。

===Original===

There are plenty that would marry me, but I have no intention whatever of taking a man that does not know my value. You may be sorry some of these days that you behaved like this; for if you will not take your chance today, not for gold, nor silver, nor anything in this world will I give it you again.”
“But, Suzanne﻿—are you sure﻿—?”
“Sir, for what do you take me?” asked the girl, draping herself in her virtue. “I am not going to put you in mind of the promises you made, promises that have been the ruin of a poor girl, when all her fault was that she looked too high and loved too much.”
But joy, suspicion, self-interest, and a host of contending emotions had taken possession of du Bousquier. For a long time past he had made up his mind that he would marry Mlle. Cormon; for after long ruminations over the Charter, he saw that it opened up magnificent prospects to his ambition through the channels of a representative government. His marriage with that mature spinster would raise his social position very much; he would acquire great influence in Alençon. And here this wily Suzanne had conjured up a storm, which put him in a most awkward dilemma. But for that private hope of his, he would have married Suzanne out of hand, and put himself openly at the head of the Liberal party in the town. Such a marriage meant the final renunciation of the best society, and a drop into the ranks of the wealthy tradesmen, shopkeepers, rich manufacturers, and graziers who, beyond a doubt, would carry him as their candidate in triumph. Already du Bousquier caught a glimpse of the Opposition benches. He did not attempt to hide his solemn deliberations; he rubbed his hand over his head, made a wisp of the cotton nightcap, and a damaging confession of the nudity beneath it. As for Suzanne, after the wont of those who succeed beyond their utmost hopes, she sat dumbfounded. To hide her amazement at his behavior, she drooped like a hapless victim before her seducer, while within herself she laughed like a grisette on a frolic.
“My dear child, I will have nothing to do with hanky-panky of this sort.”
This brief formula was the result of his cogitations. The ex-contractor to the Government prided himself upon belonging to that particular school of cynic philosophers which declines to be “taken in” by women, and includes the whole sex in one category as suspicious characters.

===Chinese===

想娶我的男人有的是；可我绝不要一个不识我的价值的男人。往后哪天您会后悔今天这个样子的；今日您不肯要的机会，往后拿金子银子、拿世上随便什么，我也不会再给您了。”
“可是，苏珊娜——你拿得准——？”
“先生，您把我当什么人了？”姑娘问，把她的贞洁像斗篷一样裹在身上。“我不打算向您提您许过的愿——那些愿毁了一个可怜的姑娘，她全部的错处，不过是眼眶太高，爱得太深。”
可是欢喜、疑心、私利，连同一大把互相打架的心绪，一齐攫住了杜·布斯基耶。许久以来他拿定主意要娶科尔蒙小姐；因为对宪章反复咀嚼之后，他看出代议制的路子，为他的野心敞开了锦绣前程。同那位成熟的老姑娘成亲，能把他的社会地位抬高一大截；他在阿朗松将握有莫大的势力。偏偏这个刁钻的苏珊娜唤来一场风暴，把他架在了极其尴尬的两难里。若不是存着那桩私念，他早一口娶了苏珊娜，公开出面去当城里自由党的头儿了。那样一门亲事意味着同上流社会最后诀别，跌进有钱店主、铺子老板、阔厂主和牛贩子的行列——可无疑正是这路人，会敲锣打鼓把他当候选人抬出去。杜·布斯基耶已经望见反对党的议席了。他并不掩饰自己庄严的深思熟虑；他一只手在头顶上来回搓，把布睡帽搓成一绺，把帽子底下的秃裸暴露了个正着。至于苏珊娜，照着那些希望超额实现的人的惯例，她呆坐着说不出话。为掩饰她对他的表现的那份惊愕，她垂下头去，活像个被引诱者逼到墙角的无告的牺牲品，心里头却笑得像个出来撒欢的女工。
“我的好孩子，这种吊儿郎当的把戏，我不沾手。”
这句简短的公式，是他一番推敲的成果。前政府承包商引以为荣的是，自己属于犬儒哲学家的那么一派——绝不被女人“蒙”，把整个女性一锅端归入可疑分子之类。

===Original===

Strong-minded men of this stamp, weaklings are they for the most part, have a catechism of their own in the matter of womankind. Every woman, according to them, from the Queen of France to the milliner, is at heart a rake, a hussy, a dangerous creature, not to say a bit of a rascal, a liar in grain, a being incapable of a serious thought. For du Bousquier and his like, woman is a maleficent *bayadère* that must be left to dance, and sing, and laugh. They see nothing holy, nothing great in woman; for them she represents, not the poetry of the senses, but gross sensuality. They are like gluttons who should mistake the kitchen for the dining-room. On this showing, a man must be a consistent tyrant, unless he means to be enslaved. And in this respect, again, du Bousquier and the Chevalier de Valois stood at opposite poles.
As he delivered himself of the above remark, he flung his nightcap to the foot of the bed, much as Gregory the Great might have flung down the candle while he launched the thunders of an excommunication; and Suzanne learned that the old bachelor wore a false front.
“Bear in mind, M. du Bousquier, that by coming here I have done my duty,” she remarked majestically. “Remember that I was bound to offer you my hand and to ask for yours; but, at the same time, remember that I have behaved with the dignity of a self-respecting woman; I did not lower myself so far as to cry like a fool; I did not insist; I have not worried you at all. Now you know my position. You know that I cannot stay in Alençon. If I do, my mother will beat me; and Mme. Lardot is as high and mighty over principles as if she washed and ironed with them. She will turn me away. And where am I to go, poor workgirl that I am? To the hospital? Am I to beg for bread? Not I. I would sooner fling myself into the Brillante or the Sarthe. Now, would it not be simpler for me to go to Paris? Mother might find some excuse for sending me, an uncle wants me to come, or an aunt is going to die, or some lady takes an interest in me. It is just a question of money for the traveling expenses and﻿—you know what﻿—”
This news was immeasurably more important to du Bousquier than to the Chevalier de Valois, for reasons which no one knew as yet but the two rivals, though they will appear in the course of the story.

===Chinese===

这一路意志坚强的汉子——多半其实是软汉——在女人问题上自有他们的一套教理问答。照他们的说法，从法兰西王后到卖帽子的女工，天下女人骨子里全是不正经的、轻佻的、危险的尤物，说难听点，还是小半个骗子、彻头彻尾的撒谎精，一种想不了正经事的生物。在杜·布斯基耶和他的同类看来，女人是一支招灾惹祸的“庙妓”（bayadère），只该由她去跳、去唱、去笑。他们在女人身上看不见任何神圣、任何伟大；对他们来说，女人代表的不是感官的诗，而是粗鄙的肉欲。他们活像把厨房错当饭厅的馋鬼。照这个说法，男人就得做个一以贯之的暴君，否则便要沦为奴隶。而在这上头，杜·布斯基耶同瓦卢瓦骑士又恰好站在两极。
他一边说出上面那句话，一边把睡帽甩到床脚，那气派，好比大格里高利一面发出绝罚的雷霆、一面摔下蜡烛；苏珊娜这才看清，老单身汉戴的是假发。
“记住，杜·布斯基耶先生，我上这儿来，是尽我的本分，”她庄严地说。“记住，我理当把手伸给您，也向您讨一只手；可同时也要记住，我自始至终保持着自重女人的体面；我没有作践自己，傻乎乎地哭；我没有纠缠；我一点没有搅扰您。现在您知道我的处境了。您知道我在阿朗松待不下去了。再待下去，我妈要打我；拉尔多太太讲究起原则来，架子大得好像她是拿原则洗衣服熨衣裳的。她要撵我。我一个穷女工，往哪里去？进济贫院么？上街讨饭么？我不干。我宁可一头扎进布里扬特河或者萨尔特河。这么说，我上巴黎去岂不更省事？妈妈总能找出个由头送我去——舅舅家叫我去，或者哪个姨妈要咽气了，或者哪位太太看中了我。只差一笔盘缠，还有——您懂的——”
这个消息，对杜·布斯基耶可非同小可，比对瓦卢瓦骑士要紧得多，其中的缘由，眼下还只有两个情敌知道，故事往下自会分晓。

===Original===

At this point, suffice it to say that Suzanne’s fib had thrown the sometime forage-contractor’s ideas into such confusion that he was incapable of thinking seriously. But for that bewilderment, but for the secret joy in his heart (for a man’s own vanity is a swindler that never lacks a dupe), it must have struck him that any honest girl, with a heart still unspoiled, would have died a hundred deaths rather than enter upon such a discussion, or make a demand for money. He must have seen the look in the girl’s eyes, seen the gambler’s ruthless meanness that would take a life to gain money for a stake.
“Would you really go to Paris?” he asked.
The words brought a twinkle to Suzanne’s gray eyes, but it was lost upon du Bousquier’s self-satisfaction.
“I would indeed, sir.”
But at this du Bousquier broke out into a singular lament. He had just paid the balance of the purchase-money for his house; and there was the painter, and the glazier, and the bricklayer, and the carpenter. Suzanne let him talk; she was waiting for the figures. Du Bousquier at last proposed three hundred francs, and at this Suzanne got up as if to go.
