两个男人默默站着，科尔蒙小姐天真地琢磨怎么给他们解闷。
“打一局雷韦尔西吧。”她提议，毫无恶意。
杜·布斯基耶笑了笑，以宅子未来男主人的身份走向牌桌。瓦卢瓦骑士究竟是昏了头，还是存心留下研究败因、寻找补救，可以断定的是：他任凭人家把自己像羊羔一样牵去宰。可他刚挨的是最重的一记闷棍；贵族换了谁，至少发个懵，也算情有可原。不多时，忠厚的德·斯蓬德神父和德·特鲁瓦维尔先生回来了，科尔蒙小姐立刻迎进前厅，把舅公拉到一边，低声告诉了他自己的决定。随后听说天鹅街的房子合德·特鲁瓦维尔先生的心意，她便求未婚夫帮个忙，就说舅公早知道那房子待售。她不敢把这句谎托给神父，怕他忘。这句谎注定比善行还要发速。当晚全阿朗松听说了这条大新闻。四天来，全城的话料赶上了 1814、1815 那不祥年头。有的当笑话，有的当真；有的骂这门亲，有的夸。阿朗松的资产阶级把它看作一场征服，最得意的正是他们。
第二天，瓦卢瓦骑士在自己的圈子里拋出这句毒辣的俏皮话：“科尔蒙家怎么起家的，怎么收场；管家和包商，彼此彼此。”
科尔蒙小姐择婿的消息传到可怜的阿塔纳斯心里；可他心里那场可怕的翻江倒海，面上一点不露。他是在德·龙瑟雷庭长家听到婚讯的，他母亲正打着一局波士顿。格朗松太太抬头，从镜子里看见儿子的脸；他脸色发白，她想——不过从清早传来含糊的流言起，他就一直苍白。科尔蒙小姐是阿塔纳斯押上性命的那张牌，大祸将临的寒森森的预感已经把他裹住了。

===Original===

When intellect and imagination have exaggerated a calamity till it becomes a burden too heavy for shoulders and brow to bear, when some long-cherished hope fails utterly, and with it the visions which enable a man to forget the fierce vulture cares gnawing at his heart; then, if that man has no belief in himself, in spite of his powers; no belief in the future, in spite of the Power Divine﻿—he is broken in pieces. Athanase was a product of education under the Empire. Fatalism, the Emperor’s creed, spread downwards to the lowest ranks of the army, to the very schoolboys at their desks. Athanase followed Mme. du Ronceret’s play with a stolidity which might so easily have been taken for indifference, that Mme. Granson fancied she had been mistaken as to her son’s feelings.
Athanase’s apparent carelessness explained his refusal to sacrifice his so-called “Liberal” opinions. This word, then recently coined for the Emperor Alexander, proceeded into the language, I believe, by way of Mme. de Staël through Benjamin Constant.
After that fatal evening the unhappy young man took to haunting one of the most picturesque walks along the Sarthe; every artist who comes to Alençon sketches it from that point of view, for the sake of the watermills, and the river gleaming brightly out among the fields, between the shapely well-grown trees on either side. Flat though the land may be, it lacks none of the subdued peculiar charm of French landscape; for in France your eyes are never wearied by glaring Eastern sunlight, nor saddened by too continual mist. It is a lonely spot. Dwellers in the provinces care nothing for beautiful scenery, perhaps because it is always about them, perhaps because there is a sense lacking in them. If there is such a thing as a promenade, a mall, or any spot from which you see a beautiful view, it is sure to be the one unfrequented part of the town. Athanase liked the loneliness, with the water like a living presence in it, and the fields just turning green in the warmth of the early spring sunlight. Occasionally someone who had seen him sitting at a poplar foot, and received an intent gaze from his eyes, would speak to Mme. Granson about him.
“There is something the matter with your son.”
“I know what he is about,” the mother would say with a satisfied air, hinting that he was meditating some great work.

===Chinese===

当智力和想象力把一场灾祸夸张到肩膀和额头都扛不动的地步，当久怀的希望彻底破灭，连同那些让人忘却猛禽般的愁苦撕咬的幻象一并破灭；那时候，倘若这人不信自己——尽管有力能；不信将来——尽管有神思——他就碎了。阿塔纳斯是帝国教育的产物。宿命论，皇帝的信条，一路向下渗到军队最低层，直到课桌前的学童。阿塔纳斯盯着德·龙瑟雷太太的牌，那份呆定，太容易被当成漠然，以致格朗松太太以为自己错看了儿子的心事。
阿塔纳斯表面的无所谓，也解释了他何以不肯牺牲他那所谓的“自由派”政见。这个词，当时新铸出来形容亚历山大皇帝，我相信，是经德·斯塔尔夫人之手、由邦雅曼·贡斯当引入语言的。
自那个致命的晚上起，不幸的青年常去萨尔特河畔一条最美的散步道徘徊；到阿朗松来的艺术家个个都在那个角度画它——为了那些水磨，为了那条在两岸亭亭嘉树间、从田野里亮闪闪流出的河。地虽平坦，法国风景那含蓄的特有妩媚一样不缺；在法兰西，你的眼睛永远不会叫东方耀眼的白日晒倦，也不会叫连绵的雾气惹愁。那是个冷清去处。外省人不稀罕好风景，也许因为风景永远在身边，也许因为少一根弦。凡有一条观景的路、一片场子、一个望得见好景致的角，那必是全城最没人去的地方。阿塔纳斯喜欢这份冷清，喜欢水像活物一样在场，喜欢早春暖阳里刚刚转绿的田野。偶尔有人见他坐在白杨树下、挨过他凝视的一眼，会去对格朗松太太提起。
“您儿子有心事。”
“我知道他在忙什么。”母亲便带着得意的神气说，暗示他在酝酿一部大作品。

===Original===

Athanase meddled no more in politics; he had no opinions; and yet, now and again, he was merry enough, merry at the expense of others, after the wont of those who stand alone and apart in contempt of public opinion. The young fellow lived so entirely outside the horizon of provincial ideas and amusements, that he was interesting to few people; he did not so much as rouse curiosity. Those who spoke of him to his mother did so for her sake, not for his. Not a creature in Alençon sympathized with Athanase; the Sarthe received the tears which no friend, no loving woman dried. If the magnificent Suzanne had chanced to pass that way, how much misery might have been prevented﻿—the two young creatures would have fallen in love.
And yet Suzanne certainly passed that way. Her ambition had been first awakened by a sufficiently marvelous tale of things which happened in 1799; an old story of adventures begun at the sign of the Three Moors had turned her childish brain. They used to tell how an adventuress, beautiful as an angel, had come from Paris with a commission from Fouché to ensnare the Marquis de Montauran, the Chouan leader sent over by the Bourbons; how she met him at that very inn of the Three Moors as he came back from his Mortagne expedition; and how she won his love, and gave him up to his enemies. The romantic figure of this woman, the power of beauty, the whole story of Marie de Verneuil and the Marquis de Montauran, dazzled Suzanne, till, as she grew older, she too longed to play with men’s lives. A few months after the flight, she could not resist the desire to see her native place again, on her way to Brittany with an artist. She wanted to see Fougères, where the Marquis de Montauran met his death; and thought of making a pilgrimage to the scenes of stories told to her in childhood of that War in the West, so little known even yet. She wished, besides, to revisit Alençon with such splendor in her surroundings, and so completely metamorphosed, that nobody should know her again. She intended to put her mother beyond the reach of want in one moment, and, in some tactful way, to send a sum of money to poor Athanase﻿—a sum which for genius in modern days is the equivalent of a Rebecca’s gift of horse and armor to an Ivanhoe of the Middle Ages.
A month went by. Opinions as to Mlle. Cormon’s marriage fluctuated in the strangest way.

===Chinese===

阿塔纳斯不再搅政治；他没有政见了；然而他不时还相当快活，拿旁人打趣取乐——凡是超然物外、藐视舆论的人都有这份兴致。这青年活得整个儿在外省思想与娱乐的地平线之外，引不起几个人的兴趣；他连人家好奇都勾不起。同他母亲提起他的人，是看母亲的情面，不是看他的。阿朗松没有一个人同情阿塔纳斯；他的眼泪，萨尔特河收下了——没有朋友，没有爱他的女人替他揾。倘若那艳光照人的苏珊娜碰巧从那条路上过，多少不幸本可避免——两个年轻人会相爱。
而苏珊娜其实真从那条路上过。她的野心最早是被一个 1799 年的奇妙故事唤醒的；三摩尔人客栈开头的一段冒险传奇，把她的重心搅翻了。人们讲：一个美如天仙的冒险女子，带着富歇的使命从巴黎来，去勾引朱安党首领、波旁家派过来的蒙托朗侯爵；讲她怎样在三摩尔人客栈迎到刚从莫尔塔涅回来的侯爵；怎样赢得他的爱，又把他交给他的敌人。这个女子的罗曼史形象，美色的权力，玛丽·德·韦尔讷伊和蒙托朗侯爵的全部故事，照花了苏珊娜的眼睛；及至年长，她也渴望玩弄男人的性命了。出走几个月后，她抵挡不住重游故土的欲望——她正同一位艺术家去布列塔尼。她想看看富热尔，蒙托朗侯爵丧命的地方；想朝拜童年听到的西边战争的故地——那场战争至今仍少有人知。她还打算风风光光、脱胎换骨地重访阿朗松，教谁也认不出她来。她打算一出手就叫母亲永远不愁衣食；还打算用个巧妙的名目，给可怜的阿塔纳斯送一笔钱——对今日的天才，这笔钱相当于中世纪丽贝卡赠给艾凡赫的战马和盔甲。
一个月过去了。关于科尔蒙小姐婚事的舆论，以最古怪的方式忽起忽落。

===Original===

There was an incredulous section which strenuously denied the truth of the report, and a party of believers who persistently affirmed it. At the end of a fortnight, the doubters received a severe check. Du Bousquier’s house was sold to M. de Troisville for forty-three thousand francs. M. de Troisville meant to live quite quietly in Alençon; he intended to return to Paris after the death of the Princess Scherbelloff, but until the inheritance fell in he would spend his time in looking after his estates. This much appeared to be fact. But the doubting faction declined to be crushed. Their assertion was that, married or no, du Bousquier had done a capital stroke of business, for his house only stood him in a matter of twenty-seven thousand francs. The believers were taken aback by this peremptory decision on the part of their opponents. “Choisnel, Mlle. Cormon’s notary, had not heard a word of marriage settlements,” added the incredulous.
But on the twentieth day the unshaken believers enjoyed a signal victory over the doubters. M. Lepresseur, the Liberal notary, went to Mlle. Cormon’s house, and the contract was signed. This was the first of many sacrifices which Rose made to her husband. The fact was that du Bousquier detested Choisnel; he blamed the notary for Mlle. Armande’s refusal in the first place, as well as for his previous rejection by Mlle. Cormon, who, as he believed, had followed Mlle. Armande’s example. He managed Mlle. Cormon so well, that she, noble-hearted woman, believing that she had misjudged her future husband, wished to make reparation for her doubts, and sacrificed her notary to her love. Still she submitted the contract to Choisnel, and he﻿—a man worthy of Plutarch﻿—defended Mlle. Cormon’s interests by letter. This was the one cause of delay.
Mlle. Cormon received a good many anonymous letters. She was informed, to her no small astonishment, that Suzanne was as honest a woman as she was herself; and that the seducer in the false toupee could not possibly have played the part assigned to him in such an adventure. Mlle. Cormon scorned anonymous letters; she wrote, however, to Suzanne with a view to gaining light on the creeds of the Maternity Society.

===Chinese===

有一派死活不信，一派咬定是真。半个月头上，怀疑派挨了重重一击：杜·布斯基耶的房子四万三千法郎卖给了德·特鲁瓦维尔先生。德·特鲁瓦维尔先生打算在阿朗松安安静静住下；谢尔贝洛夫公主一死他仍要回巴黎，只是继承落定之前，他打算用来照管产业。到此为止像是实情。可怀疑派不肯垮。他们断言：不管结婚不结婚，杜·布斯基耶都是做了笔好买卖，他那房子统共才花他两万七千法郎上下。信的人被对手这一手斩钉截铁弄得着了慌。“科尔蒙小姐的公证人舒瓦兹内尔压根没听说过什么婚书。”不信的又添一句。
可是到第二十天，不动摇的信徒打了个大胜仗。自由党公证人勒普勒瑟先生去了科尔蒙小姐家，婚书签了字。这是罗丝为丈夫做出的第一串牺牲之一。实情是，杜·布斯基耶恨透了舒瓦兹内尔；阿尔芒德小姐那回回绝，还有他先前吃科尔蒙小姐的闭门羹（他认定她是学阿尔芒德小姐的样），他都记在公证人账上。他把科尔蒙小姐掫弄得太好了，这位心比天高的女人，自认错看了未来的丈夫，一心想弥补自己起过的疑心，便把她的公证人牺牲给了爱情。但她还是把婚书送给舒瓦兹内尔过目——那是一位配得上普鲁塔克的人——他写信为科尔蒙小姐的权益辩护了一回。这就是婚事唯一耽搁的缘由。
科尔蒙小姐收到不少匿名信。她万分诧异地被告知：苏珊娜同她一样是个正派女人；那个戴假发的诱奸者，在那样的奇遇里根本扮演不了派给他的角色。科尔蒙小姐把匿名信嗤之以鼻；不过她还是给苏珊娜写了信，想给产科慈善会的信条讨个分明。

===Original===

Suzanne probably had heard of du Bousquier’s approaching marriage; she confessed to her stratagem, sent a thousand francs to the Fund, and damaged the forage-contractor’s character very considerably. Mlle. Cormon called an extraordinary meeting of the Maternity Charity, and the assembled matrons passed a resolution that henceforward the Fund should give help after and not before misfortunes befell.
In spite of these proceedings, which supplied the town with tidbits of gossip to discuss, the banns were published at the church and the major’s office. It was Athanase’s duty to make out the needful documents. The betrothed bride had gone to the Prébaudet, a measure taken partly by way of conventional modesty, partly for general security. Thither du Bousquier went every morning, fortified by atrocious and sumptuous bouquets, returning in the evening to dinner.
At last, one gray rainy day in June, the wedding took place; and Mlle. Cormon and the Sieur du Bousquier, as the incredulous faction called him, were married at the parish church in the sight of all Alençon. Bride and bridegroom drove to the mayor’s office, and afterwards to the church, in a calèche﻿—a splendid equipage for Alençon. Du Bousquier had it sent privately from Paris. The loss of the old cariole was a kind of calamity for the whole town. The saddler of the Porte de Séez lost an income of fifty francs per annum for repairs; he lifted up his voice and wept. With dismay the town of Alençon beheld the luxury introduced by the Maison Cormon; everyone feared a rise of prices all round, an increase of house rent, an invasion of Paris furniture. There were some whose curiosity pricked them to the point of giving Jacquelin ten sous for a nearer sight of so startling an innovation in a thrifty province. A pair of Normandy horses likewise caused much concern.
“If we buy horses for ourselves in this way, we shall not sell them long to those that come to buy of us,” said du Ronceret’s set.
The reasoning seemed profound, stupid though it was, in so far as it prevented the district from securing a monopoly of money from outside. In the political economy of the provinces the wealth of nations consists not so much in a brisk circulation of money as in hoards of unproductive coin.
At length the old maid’s fatal wish was fulfilled.

===Chinese===

苏珊娜多半已听说杜·布斯基耶将娶亲；她招认了自己的计谋，给基金会送去一千法郎，把草料承包商的名声大大糟蹋了一番。科尔蒙小姐召集产科慈善会特别会议，与会的太太们通过决议：今后基金会只雪后送炭，不事前预支。
尽管有这些风波——它们给全城添了不少闲话点心——结婚预告还是在教堂和市政府照章公布。出需要的文书正是阿塔纳斯的差事。订了婚的新娘去了普雷博代，一半为循俗守静，一半为万全之计。杜·布斯基耶每天早晨赶去，带着又吓人又阔气的花束；傍晚回城吃饭。
终于，六月里一个阴沉的雨天，婚礼举行了；科尔蒙小姐同“杜·布斯基耶某”——不信的那派这样称呼他——在全阿朗松的注视下，在本堂教堂结了婚。新郎新娘乘一辆敞篷马车先到市政府，再到教堂——在阿朗松，这可是阔气的排场。车是杜·布斯基耶从巴黎私下订来的。旧柳条车的退役，对全城近乎一场灾难。塞兹门的马具铺老板每年损失五十法郎的修理进项；他嚎啕大哭了。阿朗松惶恐地看着科尔蒙家引进的奢侈；人人怕物价全面上涨，房租上涨，怕巴黎家具大举入侵。有几个人的好奇心痒到了极点，肯出十个苏给雅克兰，换一个近看的机会——在节俭的外省，这样骇人听闻的革新可难得一见。一对诺曼底马同样引起深重的忧虑。
“咱们自己也照这样买起马来，往后就没法再把马卖给来买马的人了。”德·龙瑟雷那一伙人说。
这道理在阻止本地区独吞外来银钱这一点上，显得深奥——尽管蠢。在外省的政治经济学里，国民的财富不在于货币流转得快，而在于不生息的钱园得厚。
末了，老姑娘那致命的愿望实现了。

===Original===

Penelope sank under the attack of pleurisy contracted forty days before the wedding. Nothing could save her. Mme. Granson, Mariette, Mme. du Coudrai, Mme. du Ronceret﻿—the whole town, in fact﻿—noticed that the bride came into church with the left foot foremost, an omen all the more alarming because the word Left even then had acquired a political significance. The officiating priest chanced to open the mass-book at the De profundis. And so the wedding passed off, amid presages so ominous, so gloomy, so overwhelming, that nobody was found to augur well of it. Things went from bad to worse. There was no attempt at a wedding party; the bride and bridegroom started out for the Prébaudet. Paris fashions were to supplant old customs! In the evening Alençon said its say as to all these absurdities; some persons had reckoned upon one of the usual provincial jollifications, which they considered they had a right to expect, and these spoke their minds pretty freely. But Mariette and Jacquelin had a merry wedding, and they alone in all Alençon gainsaid the dismal prophecies.
Du Bousquier wished to spend the profit made by the sale of his house on restoring and modernizing the Hôtel Cormon. He had quite made up his mind to stay for some months at the Prébaudet, whither he brought his uncle de Sponde. The news spread dismay through Alençon; everyone felt that du Bousquier was about to draw the country into the downward path of domestic comfort. The foreboding grew to a fear one morning when du Bousquier drove over from the Prébaudet to superintend his workmen at the Val-Noble; and the townspeople beheld a tilbury, harnessed to a new horse, and René in livery by his master’s side. Du Bousquier had invested his wife’s savings in the funds which stood at sixty-seven francs fifty centimes. This was the first act of the new administration. In the space of one year, by constantly speculating for a rise, he made for himself a fortune almost as considerable as his wife’s. But something else happened in connection with this marriage to make it seem yet more inauspicious, and put all previous overwhelming portents and alarming innovations into the background.
It was the evening of the wedding day. Athanase and his mother were sitting in the salon by the little fire of brushwood (or *régalades*, as they say in the patois), which the servant had lighted after dinner.

===Chinese===

珀涅罗珀染上胸膜炎，在婚礼前四十天倒下了。什么也救不了它。格朗松太太、玛丽埃特、德·杜库德雷太太、德·龙瑟雷太太——实际上全城——都注意到新娘进教堂时左脚在前；这兆头越发吓人，因为“左”这个字在当时已有了政治含义。司铎碰巧把弥撒书翻在了《深渊呼告》上。于是婚礼便在这些凶险、阴惨、压倒一切的预兆之中办完了，没有一个人肯说好。事情越来越糟。婚礼没有办喜筵；新郎新娘径直动身去普雷博代。巴黎的时髦要取代古老的习俗了！当晚阿朗松把这些荒唐事议论了个够；有几户人家原指望照例热闹一场——他们认为这是他们有权期待的——这几户话说得相当不客气。可是玛丽埃特和雅克兰的婚礼办得欢欢喜喜；全阿朗松只有他们两个，不认那些阴惨的预言。
杜·布斯基耶想拿卖房子的利润翻修科尔蒙公馆、改成时新样式。他拿定主意在普雷博代住上几个月，还把舅公德·斯蓬德接了去。消息一出，阿朗松一片惊惶；人人都感到，杜·布斯基耶就要把乡下拉进家庭享受的下坡路了。预感变成恐惧的那个早晨，杜·布斯基耶从普雷博代赶车到瓦卢瓦贵族街监工；全城人看见一辆双轮轻马车，套着一匹新马，勒内穿着号衣坐在主人身边。杜·布斯基耶把太太的积蓄投进了行市六十七法郎五十生丁的公债。这是新政的第一着。不出一年，靠不断做多头，他给自己挣了一份几乎同太太相当的产业。可这桩婚事还另有一件事，使它显得愈发不祥，把先前一切压倒性的预兆和骇人的革新全压了下去。
那是婚礼当天的晚上。阿塔纳斯同母亲坐在客厅里那小小火的荆棘柴（方言叫 régalades）旁——晚饭后女仆生的火。

===Original===

“Well,” said Mme. Granson, “we will go to President du Ronceret’s tonight, now that we have no Mlle. Cormon. Goodness me! I shall never get used to calling her Mme. du Bousquier; that name makes my lips sore.”
Athanase looked at his mother with a sad constraint; he could not smile, and he wanted to acknowledge, as it were, the artless thoughtfulness which soothed the wound it could not heal.
“Mamma,” he began﻿—it was several years since he had used that word, and his tones were so gentle that they sounded like the voice of his childhood﻿—“mamma, dear, do not let us go out just yet; it is so nice here by the fire!”
It was a supreme cry of mortal anguish; the mother heard it and did not understand.
“Let us stay, child,” she said. “I would certainly rather talk with you and listen to your plans than play at boston and perhaps lose my money.”
“You are beautiful tonight; I like to look at you. And besides, the current of my thoughts is in harmony with this poor little room, where we have been through so much trouble you and I.”
“And there is still more in store for us, poor Athanase, until your work succeeds. For my own part, I am used to poverty; but, oh, my treasure, to look on and see your youth go by while you have no joy of it! Nothing but work in your life! That thought is like a disease for a mother. It tortures me night and morning. I wake up to it. Ah, God in heaven! what have I done? What sin of mine is punished with this?”
She left her seat, took a little chair, and sat down beside Athanase, nestling close up to his side, till she could lay her head on her child’s breast. Where a mother is truly a mother, the grace of love never dies. Athanase kissed her on the eyes, on the gray hair, on the forehead, with the reverent love that fain would lay the soul where the lips are laid.
“I shall never succeed,” he said, trying to hide the fatal purpose which he was revolving in his mind.
“Pooh! you are not going to be discouraged? Mind can do all things, as you say. With ten bottles of ink, ten reams of paper, and a strong will, Luther turned Europe upside down. Well, and you are going to make a great name for yourself; you are going to use to good ends the powers which he used for evil. Did you not say so?

===Chinese===

“这样吧，”格朗松太太说，“今晚咱们上德·龙瑟雷庭长家去，反正咱们再没有科尔蒙小姐了。天哪！叫我管她叫杜·布斯基耶太太，我一辈子顺不了嘴；这名字说得我嘴疼。”
阿塔纳斯悲伤而拘谨地望着母亲；他笑不出来，又想领受——不妨这样说——那份天真体贴：它抚得平它治不了的伤。
“妈妈，”他开口——这个词他已有好几年不用了，那嗓音温柔得像他童年的声音——“妈妈，亲爱的，咱们先别出去；炉火边多好！”
那是一声临终般的痛楚呼喊；母亲听见了，却不懂。
“那就留下吧，孩子，”她说。“同你聊聊，听听你的打算，可比打波士顿榆钱强。”
“您今晚真美；我爱看您。再说，我此刻的心思，同这间可怜的小屋正相合——咱们娘儿俩在这儿受过多少苦啊。”
“苦还没受完呢，可怜的阿塔纳斯，等你的事业成了才完。我这人，穷惯了；可是，我的宝贝，眼看着你的青春白白流走，你一点没沾着它的甜！你的日子里只有工作！这个念头，做母亲的像害着病。它白天黑夜刷着我。我一睁眼就是它。啊，老天爷！我做了什么孽？遭这样的报应？”
她离了座，搬过一张小椅子，挨着阿塔纳斯坐下，紧紧偎着他，直到把头靠在儿子胸口。凡是母亲真做母亲的地方，爱的恩宠永不枯死。阿塔纳斯吻她的眼睛，吻她的灰发，吻她的额头，那虔敬的爱恨不得把灵魂安放在唇所安放的地方。
“我是成不了事了。”他说，想藏住胸中打转的致命念头。
“啐！这就灰心了？你常说的，心想事成。十瓶墨水，十令纸，一副坚强的意志，路德把整个欧洲翻了个个儿。你呢，你要挣一个大名；你要把他作恶用的才能，行善用。你不是这样说的么？

===Original===

Now *I* remember what you say, you see; I understand much more than you think; for you still lie so close under my heart, that your least little thought thrills through it, as your slightest movement did once.”
“I shall not succeed *here*, you see, mamma, and I will not have you looking on while I am struggling and heartsore and in anguish. Mother, let me leave Alençon; I want to go through it all away from you.”
“*I* want to be at your side always,” she said proudly. “Suffering alone! *you* without your mother! your poor mother that would be your servant if need were, and keep out of sight for fear of injuring you, if you wished it, and never accuse you of pride! No, no, Athanase, we will never be parted!”
Athanase put his arms about her and held her with a passionate tight clasp, as a dying man might cling to life.
“And yet I wish it,” he said. “If we do not part, it is all over with me.﻿ ﻿… The double pain﻿—yours and mine﻿—would kill me. It is better that I should live, is it not?”
Mme. Granson looked with haggard eyes into her son’s face.
“So this is what you have been brooding over! They said truth. Then you are going away?”
“Yes.”
“But you are not going until you have told me all about it, and without giving me any warning? You must have some things to take with you, and money. There are some louis d’or sewed into my petticoat; you must have them.”
Athanase burst into tears.
“That was all that I wanted to tell you,” he said after a while. “Now, I will see you to the President’s house.”
Mother and son went out together. Athanase left Mme. Granson at the door of the house where she was to spend the evening. He looked long at the shafts of light that escaped through chinks in the shutters. He stood there glued to the spot, while a quarter of an hour went by, and it was with almost delirious joy that he heard his mother say, “Grand independence of hearts.”
“Poor mother, I have deceived her!” he exclaimed to himself as he reached the river.
He came down to the tall poplar on the bank where he had been wont to sit and meditate during the last six weeks. Two big stones lay there; he had brought them himself for a seat. And now, looking out over the fair landscape lying in the moonlight, he passed in review all the so glorious future that should have been his.

===Chinese===

瞧，你说过的话我可都记得呢；我懂的比你以为的多得多；因为你至今还紧贴着我的心口，你最小的一个念头，也像当年你最小的动一动那样，透进它颤抖。”
“在这儿我成不了事，妈妈，您也看见了；我不愿您看着我挣扎、心疼、受煎。妈妈，让我离开阿朗松；这些我要独自一个挨过去，离您远远的。”
“我要永远在你身边，”她骄傲地说。“独自受苦！你离了你的母亲！你那可怜的母亲，必要时甘愿做你的仆人，你要是怕妨着你，她可以躲得看不见，永远不说你骄傲！不，不，阿塔纳斯，咱们永不分离！”
阿塔纳斯张开双臂搂住她，用垂死的人抓住生命的那种狂热，紧紧抱住。
“可我盼望的正是分开。不分开，我就完了。……双份的痛——你的和我的——会杀了我。总得让我活下去，对不对？”
格朗松太太睁着憔悴的眼睛看儿子的脸。
“原来你愁的是这个！人家说的是真话。那么你要走了？”
“是。”
“可你不打算先跟我交代明白，说走就走，连个招呼都不打？你总得带几件东西，总得有钱。我裙子里缝着几枚金路易；你拿去。”
阿塔纳斯放声哭了。
“我要对您说的就是这句话。”过了一会儿他说。“走吧，我送您到庭长家。”
母子一同出门。阿塔纳斯把格朗松太太送到她要消磨晚上的那家门口。他久久望着百叶窗缝里漏出的条条灯光。他钉在那里，一刻钟过去了；听见母亲说“心的独立”，他带着近乎谵妄的欢喜。
“可怜的母亲，我骗了她！”走到河边，他冲自己喊。
他来到岸边那棵高高的白杨下——六个星期来他惯于坐在那里沉思的地方。两块大石头放在那儿，是他自己搬来当座位的。此刻，他望着月色里的锦绣田野，把本该属于他的那一切锦绣前程，一一检阅过。

===Original===

He went through cities stirred to enthusiasm by his name; he heard the cheers of crowded streets, breathed the incense of banquets, looked with a great yearning over that life of his dreams, rose uplifted and radiant in glorious triumph, raised a statue to himself, summoned up all his illusions to bid them farewell in a last Olympian carouse. The magic could only last for a little while; it fled, it had vanished forever. In that supreme moment he clung to his beautiful tree as if it had been a friend; then he put the stones, one in either pocket, and buttoned his overcoat. His hat he had purposely left at home. He went down the bank to look for a deep spot which he had had in view for some time; and slid in resolutely, trying to make as little noise as possible. There was scarcely a sound.
When Mme. Granson came home about half-past nine that night, the maid-of-all-work said nothing of Athanase, but handed her a letter. Mme. Granson opened it and read:
“I have gone away, my kind mother; do not think hardly of me.” That was all.
“A pretty thing he has done!” cried she. “And how about his linen and the money? But he will write, and I shall find him. The poor children always think themselves wiser than their fathers and mothers.” And she went to bed with a quiet mind.
The Sarthe had risen with yesterday’s rain. Fishers and anglers were prepared for this, for the swollen river washes down the eels from the little streams on its course. It so happened that an eel-catcher had set his lines over the very spot where poor Athanase had chosen to drown himself, thinking that he should never be heard of again; and next morning, about six o’clock, the man drew out the young dead body.
One or two women among Mme. Granson’s few friends went to prepare the poor widow with all possible care to receive the dreadful yield of the river. The news of the suicide, as might be expected, produced a tremendous sensation. Only last evening the poverty-stricken man of genius had not a single friend; the morning after his death scores of voices cried, “I would so willingly have helped him!” So easy is it to play a charitable part when no outlay is involved. The Chevalier de Valois, in the spirit of revenge, explained the suicide. It was a boyish, sincere, and noble passion for Mlle. Cormon that drove Athanase to take his own life. And when the Chevalier had opened Mme.

他走过一座座因他的名字而沸腾的城；他听见长街的人潮欢呼，呼吸宴会的香，热望着那梦中的生涯眺望一番；他昂然而起，容光焕发，凯旋而升，为自己立起一座像，召集起全部幻象，同它们作奥林匹斯式的最后痛饮作别。法力只撑得了一小会儿；它退了，永远消失了。在那至高的时刻，他抱住他那棵美丽的树，如同抱住一位朋友；然后他把两块石头各装进一口袋，扣上外套。帽子他有意留在了家里。他下到河岸，去找他早已看中的一处深水；毅然滑了进去，尽力不出声响。几乎没有声音。
当晚九点半前后，格朗松太太回家，打杂女仆没提阿塔纳斯，只递给她一封信。格朗松太太拆开念道：
“我走了，我慈爱的母亲；别怪我。”就这一句。
“这孩子干的好事！”她喊。“他的衣裳和钱怎么办？可他会写信的，我找得着他。可怜的孩子们，总以为自己比爷娘高明。”她安安心心上床睡了。
昨夜的雨叫萨尔特河涨了。捕鱼人都料着这一手，因为涨水把河沿小溪里的鳗鱼冲下来。恰巧有个捕鳗人在可怜的阿塔纳斯选定的自沉处下了钓线——他只道从此无人再听见提起他；第二天早晨六点钟光景，那人拉上来年轻冷却的尸体。
格朗松太太寥寥几位朋友里的一两个女人，去把可怜的寡妇千小心万小心地预备好，来领河里的那件骇人的收成。自杀的消息，不出所料，轰动一时。头天晚上，这穷天才身边一个朋友也没有；他死后第二天早上，几十张嘴喊：“我多想帮他一把！”不出钱的时候，慈善的角色最最好演。瓦卢瓦骑士抱着复仇的兴致来解释这桩自杀：是那股少年人的、真挚而高贵的、对科尔蒙小姐的热情，把阿塔纳斯送上了绝路。骑士打开格朗松

===Original===

Granson’s eyes, she saw a multitude of little things to confirm this view. The story grew touching; women cried over it.
Mme. Granson sorrowed with a dumb concentration of grief which few understood. For mothers there are two ways of bereavement. It often happens that everyone else can understand the greatness of her loss; her boy was admired and appreciated, young or handsome, with fair prospects before him or brilliant successes won already; everyone regrets him, everyone shares her mourning, and the grief that is widely spread is not so hard to bear. Then there is the loss that *one* understands. No one else knew her boy and all that he was; his smiles were for her alone; she, and she only, knew how much perished with that life, too early cut short. Such sorrow hides itself; beside that darkness other woe grows pale; no words can describe it; and, happily, there are not many women who know what it is to have those heartstrings finally severed.
Even before Mme. du Bousquier came back to town, her obliging friend, Mme. du Ronceret, went to fling a dead body down among the roses of her new-wedded happiness, to let her know what a love she had refused. Ever so gently the Présidente squeezed a shower of drops of wormwood over the honey of the first month of married life. And as Mme. du Bousquier returned, it so happened that she met Mme. Granson at the corner of the Val-Noble, and the look in the heartbroken mother’s eyes cut her to the quick. It was a look from a woman dying of grief, a thousand curses gathered up into one glance of malediction, a thousand sparks in one gleam of hate. It frightened Mme. du Bousquier; it boded ill, and invoked ill upon her.
Mme. Granson had belonged to the party most opposed to the curé; she was a bitter partisan of the priest of St. Leonard’s; but on the very evening of the tragedy she thought of the rigid orthodoxy of her own party, and she shuddered. She herself laid her son in his shroud, thinking all the while of the Mother of the Saviour; then with a soul quivering with agony, she betook herself to the house of the perjured priest. She found him busy, the humble good man, storing the hemp and flax which he gave to poor women and girls to spin, so that no worker should ever want work, a piece of wise charity which had saved more than one family that could not endure to beg.

===Chinese===

太太的眼睛，她看见千百件小事都印证这个说法。故事越来越动人；女人们为它落泪。
格朗松太太忧恸得沉默，那份专注少有人懂。丧子于母亲，有两条路。常常是：人人都能掂量她损失之重；她的儿子受人钦佩器重，年轻俊美，前程似锦或功业已就；人人惋惜，人人分忧，而分摊开的哀痛不算难捱。还有一种是只有一个人懂的丧。旁人谁也不知道她的儿子是什么；他的微笑只给她一人；只有她，只有她一人，知道那条过早砍断的生命里殒灭了多少。这样的悲伤自己藏着；在那片黑旁边，别的苦都失色；言语形容不出；所幸，尝过心弦这样彻底崩断的女人不多。
杜·布斯基耶太太还没回城，她那位殷勤的朋友德·龙瑟雷太太就跑去，往她新婚幸福的玫瑰丛里扔下一具尸首，好教她知道自己回绝的是怎样一场爱。庭长夫人温温柔柔地，往蜜月的蜜里挤下一阵苦艾的露水。杜·布斯基耶太太回来时，恰好在瓦卢瓦贵族街拐角遇上格朗松太太；那心碎的母亲眼睛里的一瞥，直扎进她心窝。那是一个悲恸欲死的女人的目光，一千句诅咒并成一瞥的诅咒，一千粒火星并成一闪的恨。它吓坏了杜·布斯基耶太太；它兆着凶，也把它凶到她头上。
格朗松太太原属最反对本堂神父的那一派；她是圣莱奥纳尔司铎的死党；可是惨剧当晚，她想起自家一派的执拗正统，她发抖了。她亲手给儿子穿殓衣，心里一直想着救世主的母亲；随后带着战栗的灵魂，去敲那宣过伪誓的神父的门。她看见他——那谦卑的好人——正忙着把麻和亚麻归仓，那是他发给穷妇人穷姑娘纺的，好教谁也不至于没活干；这一着明智的善举，救下过不止一家拉不下脸讨饭的人家。

===Original===

He left his hemp at once and brought his visitor into the dining-room, where the stricken mother saw the frugality of her own housekeeping in the supper that stood waiting for the curé.
“M. l’Abbé,” she began, “I have come to entreat you﻿—”
She burst into tears, and could not finish the sentence.
“I know why you have come,” answered the holy man, “and I trust to you, madame, and to your relative Mme. du Bousquier to make it right with his Lordship at Séez. Yes, I will pray for your unhappy boy; yes, I will say masses; but we must avoid all scandal, we must give no occasion to ill-disposed people to gather together in the church.﻿ ﻿… I myself, alone, and at night﻿—”
“Yes, yes, as you wish, if only he is laid in consecrated ground!” she said, poor mother; and taking the priest’s hand in hers, she kissed it.
And so, just before midnight, a bier was smuggled into the parish church. Four young men, Athanase’s friends, carried it. There were a few little groups of veiled and black-clad women, Mme. Granson’s friends, and some seven or eight lads that had been intimate with the dead. The bier was covered with a pall, torches were lit at the corners, and the curé read the office for the dead, with the help of one little choir boy whom he could trust. Then the suicide was buried, noiselessly, in a corner of the churchyard, and a dark wooden cross with no name upon it marked the grave for the mother. Athanase lived and died in the shadow.
Not a voice was raised against the curé; his Lordship at Séez was silent; the mother’s piety redeemed her son’s impious deed.
Months afterwards, moved by the inexplicable thirst of sorrow which drives the unhappy to steep their lips in their bitter cup, the poor woman went to see the place where her son drowned himself. Perhaps she felt instinctively that there were thoughts to be gathered under the poplar tree; perhaps, too, she longed to see all that his eyes had seen for the last time. The sight of the spot would kill many a mother; while again there are some who can kneel and worship there.﻿—There are truths on which the patient anatomist of human nature cannot insist too much; verities against which education and laws and systems of philosophy are shattered.

===Chinese===

他撂下麻，把来客让进饭厅；心碎的母亲在那里看见神父等着用的晚饭，清苦得同她自家的日子一样。
“神父先生，”她开口，“我来求您——”
她放声哭了，话说不完。
“我知道您为什么来，”圣人答，“我信得过您，太太，也信得过您的亲戚杜·布斯基耶太太，会替我在塞兹主教大人面前把事办妥。是，我为您不幸的孩子祈祷；是，我为他望弥撒；但咱们必须避人耳目，不能给心术不正的人聚到教堂里的口实。……我一个人，夜里——”
“好，好，都依您，只要他能进圣地去！”可怜的母亲说，把神父的手拉过来吻了。
于是，将近半夜，一口棺材悄悄抬进了本堂教堂。四个青年抬着，都是阿塔纳斯的朋友。有几小队蒙着黑纱的妇女，格朗松太太的朋友，还有七八个同死者亲幾过的少年。棺上盖着棺罩，四角点了火把，本堂神父在一个信得过的小唱经童子协助下，念了亡者日课。随后，自戕者无声无息地下葬在教堂墓地一角，一座无名的黑木十字架，给母亲标出了坟。阿塔纳斯生前生后都活在阴影里。
没有一个人对本堂神父出恶声；塞兹的主教保持了沉默；母亲的虔敬赎了几子的渎圣。
几个月后，被那种没法解释的悲渴驱使着——不幸的人偏要把嘴唇浸进苦杯——可怜的女人去看了儿子自沉的地方。也许她本能地觉得，那棵白杨底下有可以拾掇的思绪；也许她还渴望看一看他眼睛最后看过的那一切。那地方的景象能杀死许多母亲；可也总有那一种，能在那里跪下礼拜。——有些真理，人性的耐心解剖者怎么强调也不为过；教育、法律和一切哲学体系都在这些真理上撞得粉碎。

===Original===

It is absurd﻿—let us repeat it again and again﻿—to try to lay down hard-and-fast rules in matters of feeling; the personal element comes in to modify feeling as it arises, and a man’s character influences his most instinctive actions.
Mme. Granson, by the riverside, saw a woman at some distance﻿—a woman who came nearer, till she reached the fatal spot, and exclaimed:
“Then this is the place!”
One other woman in the world wept there as the mother was weeping, and that woman was Suzanne. She had heard of the tragedy on her arrival that morning at the Three Moors. If poor Athanase had been alive, she might have done what poor and generous people dream of doing, and the rich never think of putting in practice; she would have enclosed a thousand francs with the words, “Money lent by your father to a comrade who now repays you.” During her journey Suzanne had thought of this angelic way of giving. She looked up and saw Mme. Granson.
“I loved him,” she said; then she hurried away.
Suzanne, true to her nature, did not leave Alençon till she had changed the bride’s wreath of orange flowers to waterlilies. She was the first to assert that Mme. du Bousquier would be Mlle. Cormon as long as she lived. And with one jibe she avenged both Athanase and the dear Chevalier de Valois.
Alençon beheld another and more piteous suicide. Athanase was promptly forgotten by a world that willingly, and indeed of necessity, forgets its dead as soon as possible; but the poor Chevalier’s existence became a kind of death-in-life, a suicide continued morning after morning during fourteen years. Three months after du Bousquier’s marriage, people remarked, not without astonishment, that the Chevalier’s linen was turning yellow, and his hair irregularly combed. M. de Valois was no more, for a disheveled M. de Valois could not be said to be himself. An ivory tooth here and there deserted from the ranks, and no student of human nature could discover to what corps they belonged, whether they were native or foreign, animal or vegetable; nor whether, finally, they had been extracted by old age, or were merely lying out of sight and out of mind in the Chevalier’s dressing-table drawer. His cravat was wisped, careless of elegance, into a cord. The Negroes’ heads grew pale for lack of soap and water.

===Chinese===

想给感情立死规矩是荒唐的——这话我们说了还要说；个人的因素时时进来修改感情，人的性格左右着他最本能的举动。
格朗松太太立在河边，望见不远处一个女人——那女人走近来，走到那致命的地方，喊了一声：
“原来就是这里！”
世上还有一个女人在那里同母亲一样流泪，那女人就是苏珊娜。她当天早晨回到三摩尔人客栈，就听说了惨事。倘若可怜的阿塔纳斯还活着，她也许就做了穷苦而慷慨的人梦寐要做、富人从想不到实践的事：附上一千法郎，附一句“你父亲当年借给一个同伴的钱，如今同伴奉还。”旅途中，苏珊娜一直想着这个天使般的馈赠方式。她抬起头，看见了格朗松太太。
“我爱过他。”她说完就匆匆走了。
苏珊娜忠于自己的天性：不把新娘的橙花花冠换成睡莲，她不离阿朗松。她头一个断言：杜·布斯基耶太太只要活着一天，就仍是科尔蒙小姐。她一句挖苦，替阿塔纳斯和亲爱的瓦卢瓦骑士都报了仇。
阿朗松还目睹了另一场更惨的自戕。阿塔纳斯很快被世界忘了——世界巴不得尽快忘掉它的死者，也实在不得不尽快忘；可怜的骑士的存在却变成了一种生而死，一场天天清晨续上一回的自戕，续了十四年。杜·布斯基耶婚后三个月，人们便不无诧异地注意到：骑士的内衣发黄了，头发也不成体统地梳了。德·瓦卢瓦先生不存在了——披头散发的德·瓦卢瓦先生不能算他自己。象牙的牙齿一颗颗开了小差，研究人性的人查不出它们属于哪个兵种：是土著还是外籍，是动物还是植物；末了也查不出，究竟是老朽拔了营，还是仅仅躺在骑士梳妆台的抽屉里，眼不见心不烦。领带胡乱一裹，不问风雅，裹成一条绳。黑种小人头因缺肥皂和水而褪色发白。

===Original===

The lines on the Chevalier’s face deepened into wrinkles and darkened as his complexion grew more and more like parchment; his neglected nails were sometimes adorned with an edge of black velvet. Grains of snuff lay scattered like autumn leaves in the furrows of his waistcoat. The cotton in his ears was but seldom renewed. Melancholy, brooding on his brow, spread her sallow hues through his wrinkles; in short, time’s ravages, hitherto so carefully repaired, began to appear in rifts and cracks in the noble edifice. Here was proof of the power of the mind over matter! The blond cavalier, the jeune premier, fell into decay when hope failed.
Hitherto the Chevalier’s nose had made a peculiarly elegant appearance in public; never had it been seen to distil a drop of amber, to let fall a dark wafer of moist rappee; but now, with a snuff-bedabbled border about the nostrils, and an unsightly stream taking advantage of the channel hollowed above the upper lip, that nose, which no longer took pains to please, revealed the immense trouble that the Chevalier must have formerly taken with himself. In this neglect you saw the extent, the greatness and persistence of the man’s designs upon Mlle. Cormon. The Chevalier was crushed by a pun from du Coudrai, whose dismissal he however procured. It was the first instance of vindictiveness on the part of the urbane gentleman; but then the pun was atrocious, worse by a hundred cubits than any other ever made by the registrar of mortgages. M. du Coudrai, observing this nasal revolution, had nicknamed the Chevalier “Nérestan” (*nez-restant*).
Latterly the Chevalier’s witticisms had been few and far between; the anecdotes went the way of the teeth, but his appetite continued as good as ever; out of the great shipwreck of hopes he saved nothing but his digestion; and while he took his snuff feebly, he despatched his dinner with an avidity alarming to behold. You may mark the extent of the havoc wrought in his ideas in the fact that his colloquies with the Princess Goritza grew less and less frequent. He came to Mlle. Armande’s one day with a false calf in front of his shins. The bankruptcy of elegance was something painful, I protest; all Alençon was shocked by it. It scared society to see an elderly young man drop suddenly into his dotage, and from sheer depression of spirits pass from fifty to ninety years.

===Chinese===

骑士脸上的纹路加深成皱折，颜色转暗，脸也一曰比一曰像羊皮纸；失于修剪的指甲，有时镶上一道黑丝绒边。鼻烟屑像秋叶一样撒在背心的沟渠里。耳朵里的棉花难得一换了。忧郁在他眉宇间孵着，把蜡黄的色调漫进皱纹；一句话，时间的破坏——先前那样精心修缮的——开始在贵族大廈上现出裂缝罅隙。这是精神支配物质的明证！金发的骑士，当年的小生，希望一灭便朽落了。
直到如今，骑士那只鼻子在公共场合向来保持着独特的优雅；从未有人见它蒸馏过一滴琥珀，滴落过一粒湿鼻烟的黑饼；可如今，鼻孔四周沾着鼻烟的花边，一股有碍观瞻的细流趁势占领了上唇上方的沟漕，那只不再费力讨好的鼻子，泄露了骑士当年为自己费过的惊人的劲。从这份疏懒里，你掂得出他对科尔蒙小姐的图谋有多广、多深、多久。骑士被德·杜库德雷一句双关压垮了——不过他随即把此人搞掉了。这是这位温文绅士平生第一回记仇；可那双关也确实毒，比抵押登记官任何一句双关都毒出一百肘。德·杜库德雷先生目睹这场鼻子的革命，给骑士起了个外号：“内瑞斯当”（Nérestan，谐“剩下的鼻子”）。
近来骑士的妙语越来越稀；轶事同牙齿一样风流云散，胃口却一如既往；希望的巨轮沉没，他单救下一样：消化。他撮鼻烟撮得有气无力，对付正餐却以令人胆寒的迅猛干掉。同戈里察公主的密谈越来越稀，从这一点你可以测出他思想的荒芜。有一天他到阿尔芒德小姐家，小腿上绑了一副假腿肚。风雅的破产是件惨事，我敢说；全阿朗松为之震动。眼看一位老青年突然坠入昏聩，纯因意气消沉而从五十岁跨到九十岁，社交界都吓坏了。

===Original===

And besides, he had betrayed his secret. He had been waiting and lying in wait for Mlle. Cormon. For ten long years, persevering sportsman that he was, he had been stalking the game, and he had missed his shot. The impotent Republic had won a victory over a valiant Aristocracy, and that in full flood of Restoration! The sham had triumphed over the real; spirit was vanquished by matter, diplomacy by insurrection; and as a final misfortune, a grisette in an outbreak of bad temper, let out the secret of the Chevalier’s levées!
At once he became a man of the worst character. The Liberal party laid all du Bousquier’s foundlings on the Chevalier’s doorstep, while the Faubourg Saint-Germain of Alençon boastingly accepted them; laughed and cried, “The dear Chevalier! What else could he do?” Saint-Germain pitied the Chevalier, took him to its bosom, and smiled more than ever upon him; while an appalling amount of unpopularity was drawn down upon du Bousquier’s head. Eleven persons seceded from the salon Cormon and went over to the d’Esgrignons.
But the especial result of the marriage was a more sharply-marked division of parties in Alençon. The Maison d’Esgrignon represented undiluted aristocracy; for the Troisvilles on their return joined the clique. The Maison Cormon, skilfully influenced by du Bousquier, was not exactly Liberal, nor yet resolutely Royalist, but of that unlucky shade of opinion which produced the 221 members, so soon as the political struggle took a definite shape, and the greatest, most august, and only real power of Kingship came into collision with that most false, fickle, and tyrannical power which, when wielded by an elective body, is known as the power of Parliament.
The third salon, the salon du Ronceret, out and out Radical in its politics, was secretly allied with the Maison Cormon.
---
With the return from the Prébaudet, a life of continual suffering began for the Abbé de Sponde. He kept all that he endured locked within his soul, uttering not a word of complaint to his niece; but to Mlle. Armande he opened his heart, admitting that taking one folly with another, he should have preferred the Chevalier. M. de Valois would not have had the bad taste to thwart a feeble old man with but a few days to live. Du Bousquier had pulled the old home to pieces.

===Chinese===

何况他还泄了底。他等科尔蒙小姐，守了那么久，伏了那么久。十年整，作为百折不回的猎人，他一路潜近猎物，一枪打空。无能的共和赢了勇武的贵族一仗，而且是在复辟如日中天的时候！假战胜了真，精神败给了物质，外交败给了暴动；而最末一场大不幸是：一个女工撒气之际，泄露了骑士假发的秘密！
他一下子成了品行最坏的人。自由党把杜·布斯基耶的一堆私生子全倒在骑士门口，而阿朗松的圣日耳曼区欣欣然全收下了；笑着喊：“亲爱的骑士！他还能怎么办呢？”圣日耳曼怜悯骑士，把他接进怀里，对他笑得比从前更甜；而一股骇人的不孚众望，灌到了杜·布斯基耶头上。十一个人退出了科尔蒙沙龙，投了埃斯格里尼翁家。
但婚事的特殊后果，是阿朗松党派分野的愈发分明。埃斯格里尼翁府代表纯之又纯的贵族；特鲁瓦维尔家回乡后也入了这一伙。科尔蒙府在杜·布斯基耶的巧妙拨弄下，既不纯是自由党，也不坚决保王，而是那种倒霉的中间色调——它后来产出了那二百二十一名议员：一旦政争成形，王权这一最伟大、最庄严、唯一真实的权力，同那个最虚假、最反复、最专横的权力相撞——后者握在选举出来的机构手里时，便叫作议会权力。
第三家沙龙，德·龙瑟雷沙龙，政治上不折不扣的激进，暗中则同科尔蒙府结着盟。
---
从普雷博代回城起，德·斯蓬德神父开始了连绵不断的受难。他把所受的一切锁在灵魂里，对侄女一字不抱怨；可他对阿尔芒德小姐敞开了心，承认两傻相权，他倒宁可是骑士。德·瓦卢瓦先生还不至于趣味坏到去掣一个没几天活头的老弱病人的肘。杜·布斯基耶把老家拆了个七零八落。

===Original===

“Mademoiselle,” the old Abbé said as the thin tears fell from his faded old eyes, “the lime-tree walk, where I have been used to meditate these fifty years, is gone. My dear lime-trees have all been cut down! Just as I am nearing the end of my days the Republic has come back again in the shape of a horrible revolution in the house.”
“Your niece must be forgiven,” said the Chevalier de Valois. “Republicanism is a youthful error; youth goes out to seek for liberty, and finds tyranny in its worst form﻿—the tyranny of the impotent rabble. Your niece, poor thing, has not been punished by the thing wherein she sinned.”
“What is to become of me in a house with naked women dancing all over the walls? Where shall I find the lime-tree walks where I used to read my breviary?”
Like Kant, who lost the thread of his ideas when somebody cut down the fir-tree on which he fixed his eyes as he meditated, the good Abbé pacing up and down the shadowless alleys could not say his prayers with the same uplifting of soul. Du Bousquier had laid out an English garden!
“It looked nicer,” Mme. du Bousquier said. Not that she really thought so, but the Abbé Couturier had authorized her to say and do a good many things that she might please her husband.
With the restoration, all the glory departed from the old house, and all its quaint, cheerful, old-world look. If the Chevalier de Valois’ neglect of his person might be taken as a sort of abdication, the bourgeois majesty of the salon Cormon passed away when the drawing-room was decorated with white and gold; and blue silk curtains and mahogany ottomans made their appearance. In the dining-room, fitted up in the modern style, the dishes were somehow not so hot, nor the dinners quite what they had been. M. du Coudrai said that the puns stuck fast in his throat when he saw the painted figures on the walls and felt their eyes upon him. Without, the house was provincial as ever; within, the forage-contractor of the Directory made himself everywhere felt. All over the house you saw the stockbroker’s bad taste; stucco pilasters, glass doors, classic cornices, arid decoration﻿—a medley of every imaginable style and ill-assorted magnificence.
Alençon criticised such unheard-of luxury for a fortnight, and grew proud of it at the end of a few months.

===Chinese===

“小姐，”老神父说着，昏花的老眼里落下稀疏的泪，“菩提径，我五十年来在那里默想的那条，没有了。我亲爱的菩提树全砍了！我快到日子的头了，共和制却换了一副模样又回来——在这宅子里闹了一场可怕的革命。”
“得饶恕您的侄女，”瓦卢瓦骑士说，“共和主义是年轻时的错；青春出去找自由，找到的却是最坏形态的暴政——无能暴民的暴政。您侄女那可怜人，没有在她犯罪的地方受罚。”
“四面墙上都是裸体女人跳舞的宅子，叫我可怎么待？我从前念日课的菩提径，又到哪里去找？”
像康德——有人砍掉他凝视冥想的那棵杉树，他思路就断了——好神父在没了树荫的小径上来回踱步，也再不能带着从前那种灵魂的腾升念经了。杜·布斯基耶造了一座英国式花园！
“这样好看些。”杜·布斯基耶太太说。倒不是她真这么想，而是库蒂里耶神父授权她，为讨丈夫欢心，许多事不妨说也不妨做。
随着这次“复辟”，老宅的全部荣光、全部古怪欢快的旧时代气韵都去了。如果说瓦卢瓦骑士怠慢自己的仪容可以算一种退位，那么科尔蒙沙龙的市民威严，也在客厅装上白金的装饰、蓝绸窗帘和红木软榻之后消散了。照时新样式重新装修的饭厅里，菜不知怎么不那么烫了，正餐也不大像从前了。德·杜库德雷先生说，一看见墙上画的那些人物、觉得他们的眼睛盯着自己，双关语就卡在喉咙里。宅子外面，照旧是外省；里面，督政府的草料承包商处处摸得着。满宅子都是证券经纪人的坏趣味：灰泥壁柱，玻璃门，古典的檐口，干巴巴的装点——一切想得出来的式样和不相称的豪阔大杂烩。
阿朗松非议了半月这样的前所未闻的奢侈，几个月后又引以为荣了。

===Original===

Several rich manufacturers refurnished their houses in consequence, and set up fine drawing-rooms. Modern furniture made its appearance; astral lamps might even be seen in some places.
The Abbé de Sponde was the first to see the unhappiness which lay beneath the surface of his dear child’s married life. The old dignified simplicity which ruled their way of living was gone; du Bousquier gave two balls every month in the course of the first winter. The venerable house﻿—oh, to think of it!﻿—echoed with the sound of violins and worldly gaiety. The Abbé, on his knees, prayed while the merriment lasted.
The politics of the sober salon underwent a gradual change for the worse. The Abbé de Sponde divined du Bousquier; he shuddered at his nephew’s dictatorial tone. He saw tears in his niece’s eyes when the disposal of her fortune was taken out of her hands; her husband left her only the control of the linen, the table, and such things as fall to a woman’s lot. Rose had no more orders to give. Jacquelin, now coachman exclusively, took his orders from no one but his master; René, the groom, did likewise, so did the man-cook imported from Paris; Mariette was only the kitchen-maid; and Mme. du Bousquier had no one to tyrannize over but Josette.
Does anyone know how much it costs to give up the delicious exercise of authority? If the triumph of will is one of the most intoxicating of the great man’s joys, to have one’s own way is the whole life of narrow natures. No one but a cabinet minister fallen into disgrace can sympathize with Mme. du Bousquier’s bitter pain when she saw herself reduced to a cipher in her own house. She often drove out when she would rather have stayed at homo; she saw company which she did not like; she who had been free to spend as she pleased, and had never spent at all, had lost the control of the money which she loved. Impose limits, and who does not wish to go beyond them? Is there any sharper suffering than that which comes of thwarted will?
But these beginnings were the roses of life. Every concession was counseled by poor Rose’s love for her husband, and at first du Bousquier behaved admirably to his wife. He was very good to her; he brought forward sufficient reasons for every encroachment. The room, so long left empty, echoed with the voices of husband and wife in fireside talk. And so, for the first few years of married life, Mme.

===Chinese===

好几位阔厂主跟着重新装修了住宅，设起漂亮的客厅。时新家具出现了；有些人家甚至点起了astral牌新式油灯。
德·斯蓬德神父头一个看出爱女婚姻表面底下的不幸。往日支配他们生活方式的那种庄重的简朴没有了；婚后头一个冬天，杜·布斯基耶每月开两场舞会。那可敬的老宅——哦，想想吧！——回荡着小提琴和世俗的欢笑。舞会闹着的当口，神父跪着祈祷。
这间持重沙龙的政治渐渐变坏。德·斯蓬德神父看穿了杜·布斯基耶；他听见内侄婿那专断的口气就发抖。他看见侄女眼里含泪，因为财产的处分权从她手里夺走了；丈夫只留给她衣被、饭桌和归女人管的那些事。罗丝再没有命令可下了。雅克兰如今专做车夫，只听主人的；马夫勒内一样，从巴黎请来的男厨子也一样；玛丽埃特降成了厨房丫头；杜·布斯基耶太太手下可施威的，只剩一个若赛特。
有谁知道，放弃行使权威那桩美妙的差事，代价几何？意志的得胜，是伟人最醺醺的欢乐之一；而事事作主，是窄心人的整个生命。除了倒台的内阁部长，谁也同情不了杜·布斯基耶太太那切肤之痛：她眼看自己降成自家宅子里的一个零。她常常想待在家却被迫出门；她见她不喜欢的客；她从前花钱随心所欲——其实从不花——如今连她心爱的钱也管不着了。设下界限，谁不想越过去？受挫的意志带来的痛，还有更尖锐的么？
可这些还只是生活的玫瑰。每一项让步都是可怜的罗丝对丈夫的爱出的主意；起初杜·布斯基耶待妻子无可挑剔。他对她很好；每一回蚕食都摆出充足的理由。那间久已空着的屋子，回荡起夫妻炉边的絮语。就这样，婚后头几年，杜·

===Original===

du Bousquier wore a face of content, and that little air of emancipation and mystery often seen in a young wife after a marriage of love. She had no more trouble with “heated blood.” This countenance of hers routed scoffers, gave the lie to gossip concerning du Bousquier, and put observers of human nature at fault.
Rose Marie Victoire was so afraid lest she should lose her husband’s affection or drive him from her side by setting her will against his, that she would have made any sacrifice, even of her uncle if need be. And the Abbé de Sponde, deceived by Mme. du Bousquier’s poor foolish little joys, bore his own discomforts the more easily for the thought that his niece was happy.
At first Alençon shared this impression. But there was one man less easy to deceive than all the rest of Alençon put together. The Chevalier de Valois had taken refuge on the Mons Sacer of the most aristocratic section, and spent his time with the d’Esgrignons. He lent an ear to the scandal and tittle-tattle; night and day he studied how to have his revenge before he died. The perpetrator of puns had been already brought low, and he meant to stab du Bousquier to the heart.
The poor Abbé, knowing as he did the cowardliness of his niece’s first and last love, shuddered as he guessed his nephew’s hypocritical nature and the man’s intrigues. Du Bousquier, be it said, put some constraint upon himself; he had an eye to the Abbé’s property, and had no wish to annoy his wife’s uncle in any way, yet he dealt the old man his deathblow.
If you can translate the word Intolerance by Firmness of Principle; if you can forbear to condemn in the old Roman Catholic Vicar-General that stoicism which Scott has taught us to revere in Jeanie Deans’ Puritan father; if, finally, you can recognize in the Roman Church the nobility of a *Potius mori quam foedari* which you admire in a Republican﻿—then you can understand the anguish that rent the great Abbé de Sponde when he saw the apostate in his nephew’s drawing-room; when he was compelled to meet the renegade, the backslider, the enemy of the Church, the aider and abettor of the Oath to the Constitution. It was du Bousquier’s private ambition to lord it over the countryside; and as a first proof of his power, he determined to reconcile the officiating priest of St. Leonard’s with the curé of Alençon. He gained his object.

===Chinese===

布斯基耶太太面带知足，带着恋爱成婚的少妇常有的那种既解脱又神秘的小神气。她再不为“血热”烦恼了。她这副面色驳倒了讥笑者，戳穿了关于杜·布斯基耶的流言，叫观人者们无从捉摸。
罗丝·玛丽·维克图瓦那样怕失去丈夫的爱、怕拿自己的意志顶撞他而把他从身边赶走，什么牺牲都肯做，必要时连舅公也肯牺牲。德·斯蓬德神父呢，被杜·布斯基耶太太那些可怜的傻欢喜所蒙蔽，想着侄女是幸福的，自己的不自在也就好捱了。
起初全阿朗松都这么看。可有一个人，比全阿朗松加在一起还难糊弄。瓦卢瓦骑士避上了最贵族地段的“圣山”，成天待在埃斯格里尼翁家。他听着丑闻和碎嘴；日日夜夜研究着怎样在死前报这个仇。说双关的已经叫他撂倒了，他还打算一刀捅进杜·布斯基耶的心口。
可怜的神父深知侄女这头一场也是末一场爱的懦怯，一面猜度着内侄婿的伪善和那人的种种阴谋，一面发抖。不过说句公道话，杜·布斯基耶还克制；他惦着神父的财产，不愿在任何事上搅扰太太的舅公，可他仍旧给了老人那致命的一击。
倘若你能把“不宽容”译作“原则坚定”；倘若你能不谴责任在旧天主教代理主教身上的那种坚忍——司各特教我们在珍妮·迪恩斯的清教徒父亲身上敬重的正是它；倘若末了你还能在天主教会里认出你钦佩于共和党人的那种“宁死不玷”的高贵——那么你就能懂得，大德高望重的德·斯蓬德神父看见那背教者出现在内侄婿客厅里时，五内俱裂的苦痛；懂得他被迫同那个叛徒、那个变节者、教会的敌人、宪政誓言的教唆犯同席相见时的苦痛。称霸乡里，本就是杜·布斯基耶的私愿；作为权势的第一件凭证，他决意叫圣莱奥纳尔的司铎同阿朗松的本堂神父和解。他达到了目的。

===Original===

His wife imagined that peace had been made where the stern Abbé saw no peace, but surrender of principle. M. de Sponde was left alone in the faith. The Bishop came to du Bousquier’s house, and appeared satisfied with the cessation of hostilities. The Abbé François’ goodness had conquered everyone﻿—everyone except the old Roman of the Roman Church, who might have cried with Cornélie, “Ah, God! what virtues you make me hate!” The Abbé de Sponde died when orthodoxy expired in the diocese.
In 1819 the Abbé de Sponde’s property raised Mme. du Bousquier’s income from land to twenty-five thousand livres without counting the Prébaudet or the house in the Val-Noble. About the same time du Bousquier returned the amount of his wife’s savings (which she had made over to him), and instructed her to invest the moneys in purchases of land near the Prébaudet, so that the estate, including the Abbé de Sponde’s adjoining property, was one of the largest in the department. As for du Bousquier, he invested his money with the Kellers, and made a journey to Paris four times a year. Nobody knew the exact amount of his private fortune, but at this time he was supposed to be one of the wealthiest men in the department of the Orne. A dexterous man, and the permanent candidate of the Liberal party, he always lost his election by seven or eight votes under the Restoration. Ostensibly he repudiated his connection with the Liberals, offering himself as a Ministerial-Royalist candidate; but although he succeeded in gaining the support of the Congrégation and of the magistrature, the repugnance of the administration was too strong to be overcome.
Then the rabid Republican, frantic with ambition, conceived the idea of beginning a struggle with the Royalism and Aristocracy of the country, just as they were carrying all before them. He gained the support of the clergy by an appearance of piety very skilfully kept up; always going with his wife to mass, giving money to the convents, and supporting the confraternity of the Sacré-Coeur; and whenever a dispute arose between the clergy and the town, or the department, or the State, he was very careful to take the clerical side. And so, while secretly supported by the Liberals, he gained the influence of the Church; and as a Constitutional-Royalist kept close beside the aristocratic section, the better to ruin it. And ruin it he did.

===Chinese===

他太太以为媾和达成了；严峻的神父看到的却不是和平，是原则的投降。德·斯蓬德先生孤身留在信仰里。主教到了杜·布斯基耶家，对停战表示满意。弗朗索瓦神父的善良征服了所有人——除了那位罗马教会里的老罗马人，他真可以同科涅莉亚一道喊：“啊，天哪！你叫我恨的都些什么德行啊！”正统在教区断气的那天，德·斯蓬德神父也死了。
1819 年，德·斯蓬德神父的遗产把杜·布斯基耶太太的地租收入抬到两万五千里弗尔——还没算普雷博代和瓦卢瓦贵族街的宅子。差不多同时，杜·布斯基耶归还了太太的积蓄（她早已划归了他），并指点她把钱投在普雷博代周边买地；于是连同德·斯蓬德神父的毗邻地产，这份产业成了全省数一数二的大庄。至于杜·布斯基耶本人，他把钱存进了凯勒家，一年跑四趟巴黎。他的私产究竟多少，无人知晓；但此时他已被认作奥恩省首富之一。他是个机灵人，自由党的终身候选人，复辟期间每回选举总差七八票败北。表面上他声明同自由党无涉，以“保王的部长派”候选人自荐；可尽管他赢得了修会团体和司法界的支持，行政当局的反感终究压不下去。
于是，这个狂热的共和党人，野心烧得发狂，打定了主意，要在保王主义和贵族得势冲天之际，同它们开一场战。他靠一手巧妙维持的虔敬外表赢得教士的支持：总偕太太望弥撒，给修道院捐钱，支持圣心善会；教士同市镇、同省、同国家起了争端，他必站在教士一边。就这样，他暗中得着自由党的支持，又赢下了教会的影响；以立宪保王党的身份紧紧贴着贵族那帮，贴得越近越好毁掉它。他真把它毁掉了。

===Original===

He was always on the watch for any mistake on the part of those high in rank or in office under the Government; with the support of the bourgeoisie he carried out all the improvements which the nobles and officials ought to have undertaken and directed, if the imbecile jealousies of place had not frustrated their efforts. Constitutional opinion carried him through in the affair of the curé, in the theatre question, and in all the various schemes of improvement which du Bousquier first prompted the Liberals to make, and afterwards supported in the course of debate, declaring himself in favor of any measures for the good of the country. He brought about an industrial revolution; and his detestation of certain families on the highroad to Brittany rapidly increased the material prosperity of the province.
And so he paved the way for his revenge upon the *gens à châteaux* in general, and the d’Esgrignons in particular: some day, not so very far distant, he would plunge a poisoned blade into the very heart of the clique. He found capital to revive the manufacture of *point d’Alençon* and to increase the linen trade. Alençon began to spin its own flax by machinery. And while his name was associated with all these interests, and written in the hearts of the masses, while he did all that Royalty left undone, du Bousquier risked not a farthing of his own. With his means, he could afford to wait while enterprising men with little capital were obliged to give up and leave the results of their labors to luckier successors. He posed as a banker. A Laffitte on a small scale, he became a sleeping partner in all new inventions, taking security for his money. And as a public benefactor, he did remarkably well for himself. He was a promoter of insurance companies, a patron of new public conveyances; he got up memorials for necessary roads and bridges. The authorities, being left behind in this way, regarded this activity in the light of an encroachment; they blundered, and put themselves in the wrong, for the prefecture was obliged to give way for the good of the country.
Du Bousquier embittered the provincial noblesse against the court nobles and the peerage. He helped, in short, to bring it to pass that a very large body of Constitutional-Royalists supported the *Journal des Débats* and M. de Chateaubriand in a contest with the throne.

===Chinese===

他时时盯着身居高位和官职的人们的每一个闪失；仗着资产阶级的支持，他把种种改良一一办成——那些本是贵族和官员们该办该领的事，若不是官位上的蠢妒坏事，他们早就办了。在神父案、剧院案以及各种兴利除弊的方案里——那些方案都是杜·布斯基耶先撺掇自由党提出、随后在辩论中支持的，他自称凡有利于国家者无不赞成——立宪的舆论把他抬了过去。他促成了一场工业革命；他对布列塔尼大道边某几户人家的憎恶，飞快地增长了本省的物质繁荣。
他就这样铺平了向“有城堡的人家”总清算、尤其向埃斯格里尼翁家复仇的路：总有一天，为时不远，他要把一柄毒刃捅进那一伙的心口。他寻来资本，复兴阿朗松花边制造业，扩大亚麻布贸易。阿朗松开始用机器纺自己的麻。他的名字同这一切利益连在一起，写在群众的心上；王家不做的事他都做了，而他自己一个子儿也不冒风险。凭他的实力，他等得起；小本而敢闯的人却只好撒手，把劳动的果实留给运气更好的后任。他摆出一副银行家的做派。一个小号的拉菲特，他当一切新发明的匿名股东，钱都拿了抵押。而作为造福公众的人，他给自己挣得漂漂亮亮。他是保险公司的发起人，新式公共交通的赞助者；必要路桥的请愿书由他张罗。当局这样被撇在后头，便把这份活跃看作僭越；他们一错再错，自己站到了理亏的一边，因为为了公益，省长官邸不得不让步。
杜·布斯基耶把外省贵族煽得敌视宫廷贵族和贵族院。总之，他促成了这样一桩事：一大帮立宪保王党，在一场同王权的角力中，站到了《辩论报》和德·夏多布里昂先生一边。

===Original===

It was an ungrateful opposition based on ignoble motives which contributed to bring about the triumph of the bourgeoisie and the press in 1830. Wherefore du Bousquier, like those whom he represented, had the pleasure of watching a funeral procession of royalty5 pass through their district without a single demonstration of sympathy from a population alienated from them in ways so numerous that they cannot be indicated here.
Then the old Republican, with all that weight of masses on his conscience, hauled down the white flag above the townhall amid the applause of the people. For fifteen years he had acted a part to satisfy his vendetta, and no man in France beholding the new throne raised in August 1830 could feel more intoxicated than he with the joy of revenge. For him, the succession of the younger branch meant the triumph of the Revolution; for him, the hoisting of the Tricolor flag was the resurrection of the Mountain; and *this* time the nobles should be brought low by a surer method than the guillotine, in that its action should be less violent. A peerage for life only; a National Guard which stretches the marquis and the grocer from the corner shop on the same camp bed; the abolition of entail demanded by a bourgeois barrister; a Catholic Church deprived of its supremacy; in short, all the legislative inventions of August 1830 simply meant for du Bousquier the principles of 1793 carried out in a most ingenious manner.
Du Bousquier has been receiver-general of taxes since 1830. He relied for success upon his old connections with Egalité Orléans (father of Louis Philippe) and M. de Folman, steward of the Dowager Duchess. He is supposed to have an income of eighty thousand livres. In the eyes of his fellow-countrymen, *Monsieur* du Bousquier is a man of substance, honorable, upright, obliging, unswerving in his principles. To him, Alençon owes her participation in the industrial movement which makes her, as it were, the first link in a chain which some day perhaps may bind Brittany to the state of things which we nickname “modern civilization.” In 1816 Alençon boasted but two carriages, properly speaking; ten years afterwards, calèches, coupés, landaus, cabriolets, and tilburies were rolling about the streets without causing any astonishment.

===Chinese===

这是一场出于卑劣动机的不知恩的反对派，促成了 1830 年资产阶级和报界的得胜。因此，杜·布斯基耶同他所代表的那班人一样，有幸目送王室的送葬行列5 穿过他们的地区，而民众无一声同情的表示——民众同他们疏远的方式多不胜数，此处无法一一表列。
随后，这个老共和党人，肩着群众的重压在良心上，在民众的欢呼中降下了市政厅顶上的白旗。为了他的族仇，他装了十五年的戏；1830 年 8 月望着新王座升起的全法兰西，没有一个人比他更醉于复仇的欢喜。对他，幼支的继统意味着革命的胜利；对他，三色旗的升起意味着山岳党的复活；而这一回，压倒贵族要用一个比断头台更稳当的法子，动作也缓和些。终身制的贵族院；一支把侯爵和街角杂货铺老板拉上同一张行军床的国民自卫军；由市民律师提出的废除限定继承；被褫夺至高地位的天主教会；总之，1830 年 8 月的一切立法发明，在杜·布斯基耶看来，无非是 1793 年的各项原则，以一种再巧妙不过的方式得到贯彻。
杜·布斯基耶自 1830 年起任总税务官。他指望靠旧交情发迹：“平等奥尔良”（路易·菲利普之父）和太公爵夫人的总管德·福尔曼先生。据说他岁入八万里弗尔。在乡人眼里，杜·布斯基耶先生是位殷实君子，体面，正直，乐于助人，立场坚定。阿朗松能搭上工业运动的班车，多亏了他——那班车使阿朗松几乎成了链条的第一环，有朝一日也许会把布列塔尼拴在我们称作“现代文明”的那个状态上。1816 年，严格说来阿朗松统共只有两辆马车；十年之后，敞篷车、双座轿、活顶四轮、双轮篷车、双轮轻车满街跑，谁也不惊讶了。

===Original===

At first the townsmen and landowners were alarmed by the rise of prices, afterwards they discovered that the increased expenditure produced a corresponding increase in their incomes.
Du Ronceret’s prophetic words, “Du Bousquier is a very strong man,” were now taken up by the country. But, unfortunately for du Bousquier’s wife, the remark is a shocking misnomer. Du Bousquier the husband is a very different person from du Bousquier the public man and politician. The great citizen, so liberal in his opinions, so easy humored, so full of love for his country, is a despot at home, and has not a particle of love for his wife. The Cromwell of the Val-Noble is profoundly astute, hypocritical, and crafty; he behaves to those of his own household as he behaved to the aristocrats on whom he fawned, until he could cut their throats. Like his friend Bernadotte, he has an iron hand in a velvet glove. His wife gave him no children. Suzanne’s epigram, and the Chevalier de Valois’ insinuations, were justified; but the Liberals and Constitutional-Royalists among the townspeople, the little squires, the magistrature, and the “clericals” (as the *Constitutionnel* used to say), all threw the blame upon Mme. du Bousquier. M. du Bousquier had married such an elderly wife, they said; and besides, how lucky it was for her, poor thing, for at her age bearing a child meant such a risk. If, in periodically recurrent despair, Mme. du Bousquier confided her troubles with tears to Mme. du Coudrai or Mme. du Ronceret﻿—
“Why you must be mad, dear!” those ladies would reply. “You do not know what you want; a child would be the death of you.”
Men like M. du Coudrai, who followed du Bousquier’s lead because they fastened their hopes to his success, would prompt their wives to sing du Bousquier’s praises; and Rose must listen to speeches that wounded like a stab.
“You are very fortunate, dear, to have such a capable husband; some men have no energy, and can neither manage their own property nor bring up their children; you are spared these troubles.”
Or, “Your husband is making you queen of the district, fair lady. *He* will never leave you at a loss; he does everything in Alençon.”
“But I should like him to take less trouble for the public and rather﻿—”
“My dear Mme. du Bousquier, you are very hard to please; all the women envy you your husband.”

===Chinese===

起初，市民和地主被物价上涨吓着了，后来发现开销涨多少，进项也涨多少。
德·龙瑟雷那句预言“杜·布斯基耶是个强人”，如今被乡间接了过去。可惜对杜·布斯基耶太太而言，这话用在她丈夫身上是个可怕的错名。做丈夫的杜·布斯基耶，同作为公众人物和政治家的杜·布斯基耶，是两个人。那位伟大的公民，见解那样开明，性子那样随和，那样爱国，在家里却是个暴君，对妻子没有一粒爱。瓦卢瓦贵族街的克伦威尔深藏城府，伪善而狡诈；他对自家人的做派，就是他对贵族的做派——先舐着他们，直到能割他们的喉咙。像他的朋友贝尔纳多特一样，他天鹅绒手套里握着铁手。妻子没给他生孩子。苏珊娜的俏皮话和瓦卢瓦骑士的暗示都应验了；可是城里的自由党、立宪保王党、小乡绅、司法界和“教权派”（《宪政报》的用语），一股脑把罪名扣在杜·布斯基耶太太头上。杜·布斯基耶先生娶的是这样的老妻，他们说；再说她多走运啊，可怜的人，她那个年纪生孩子冒多大的险。倘若杜·布斯基耶太太在周期发作的绝望里，含着泪向德·杜库德雷太太或德·龙瑟雷太太吐苦水——
“你准是疯了，亲爱的！”太太们答。“你不知道自己要什么；生个孩子要你的命的。”
像德·杜库德雷先生那样的人——把指望拴在杜·布斯基耶的成功上，才跟着他跑——便指使太太们去唱杜·布斯基耶的赞歌；罗丝只好听着一句句像刀捅的话。
“您多有福气，亲爱的，摊上这么个能干的丈夫；有些男人没一点魄力，自己的产业管不好，孩子也养不好；您倒省了这些心。”
要么是：“您丈夫正把您捧成全区的王后呢，美丽的夫人。他永远不会叫您为难；阿朗松的事他样样都成。”
“可我倒愿意他为公众多操点心，少——”
“我亲爱的杜·布斯基耶太太，您可真难伺候；全天下的女人都艳羡您这个丈夫呢。”

===Original===

Unjustly treated by a world which condemned her without a hearing, she found ample scope for the exercise of Christian virtues in her inner life. She who lived in tears always turned a serene face upon the world. For her, pious soul, was there not sin in the thought which was always pecking at her heart﻿—“I loved the Chevalier de Valois, and I am du Bousquier’s wife!” Athanase’s love rose up like a remorse to haunt her dreams. After her uncle’s death and the revelation of all that he had suffered, the future grew yet more dreadful as she thought how grieved he would have been by such changes of political and religious doctrine. Unhappiness often falls like a thunderbolt, as upon Mme. Granson, for instance; but Rose’s misery gradually widened out before her as a drop of oil spreads over stuff, slowly saturating every fibre.
The Chevalier de Valois was the malignant artificer of her misfortune. He had it on his mind to snatch his opportunity and undeceive Mme. du Bousquier as to one of her articles of faith; for the Chevalier, a man of experience, saw through du Bousquier the married man, as he had seen through du Bousquier the bachelor. But it was not easy to take the astute Republican by surprise. His salon, naturally, was closed to the Chevalier de Valois, as to all others who discontinued their visits to the Maison Cormon at the time of his marriage. And besides, du Bousquier was above the reach of ridicule; he possessed an immense fortune, he was king of Alençon; and as for his wife, he cared about her much as Richard III might have cared for the loss of the horse with which he thought to win the battle. To please her husband, Mme. du Bousquier had broken with the Maison d’Esgrignon, but sometimes, when he was away at Paris for a few days, she paid Mlle. Armande a visit.
Two years after Mme. du Bousquier’s marriage, just at the time of the Abbé’s death, Mlle. Armande went up to her as she came out of church. Both women had been to St. Leonard’s to hear a *messe noire* said for M. de Sponde; and Mlle. Armande, a generous-natured woman, thinking that she ought to try to comfort the weeping heiress, walked with her as far as the Parade. From the Parade, still talking of the beloved and lost, they came to the forbidden Hôtel d’Esgrignon, and Mlle. Armande drew Mme. du Bousquier into the house by the charm of her talk.

被这个不审而判的世界冤待着，她在内心生活里找到了涵养基督徒德行的广阔天地。她生活在泪里，对世人却永远摆出一副安详的脸。在她，这虔诚的灵魂，那个一直啄着她心口的念头——难道不是罪么——“我爱过瓦卢瓦骑士，却做了杜·布斯基耶的妻子！”阿塔纳斯的爱像一桩悔恨，起来纠缠她的梦。舅公死后，他受的苦全部揭晓，前景愈发可怕——她想到，政见教义上这许多改换，他在天上该怎样痛心。不幸常常像霹雳一样當头劈下，比如格朗松太太；罗丝的苦难却像一滴油摊在布上，慢慢浸透每一根纤维。
瓦卢瓦骑士是她不幸的恶意营造师。他一心候着时机，好破除杜·布斯基耶太太的一项信条；因为阅世甚深的骑士，看穿做丈夫的杜·布斯基耶，正如当年看穿单身的杜·布斯基耶。可这个机警的共和党人不好偷袭。他的沙龙自然对瓦卢瓦骑士闭门——对他婚后那阵子不再登科尔蒙家门的客人一概如此。何况杜·布斯基耶刀枪不入于讥笑：他拥巨富，是阿朗松的王；至于太太，他疼她的程度，大约相当于理查三世疼那匹他指望赢下战役的马。为讨丈夫欢心，杜·布斯基耶太太同埃斯格里尼翁府断了来往；可有时他上巴黎去几天，她便去看望阿尔芒德小姐。
杜·布斯基耶太太婚后两年，恰逢神父去世，阿尔芒德小姐在教堂门口迎上她。两人都是到圣莱奥纳尔望一台为德·斯蓬德先生安魂的黑弥撒的；阿尔芒德小姐天生厚道，觉得该安慰安慰这位垂泪的继承人，便陪她一路走到练兵场。从练兵场，谈着那位敬爱而永逝的人，她们走到了那禁地埃斯格里尼翁公馆；阿尔芒德小姐靠谈吐的魅力，把杜·布斯基耶太太拉进了宅子。

===Original===

Perhaps the poor brokenhearted woman loved to speak of her uncle with someone whom her uncle had loved so well. And besides, she wished to receive the old Marquis’ greetings after an interval of nearly three years. It was half-past one o’clock; the Chevalier de Valois had come to dinner, and with a bow he held out both hands.
“Ah! well, dear, good, and well-beloved lady,” he said tremulously, “*we* have lost our sainted friend. Your mourning is ours. Yes; your loss is felt as deeply here as under your roof﻿—more deeply,” he added, alluding to du Bousquier.
A funeral oration followed, to which everyone contributed his phrase; then the Chevalier, gallantly taking the lady’s hand, drew it under his arm, pressed it in the most adorable way, and led her aside into the embrasure of a window.
“You are happy, at any rate?” he asked with a fatherly tone in his voice.
“Yes,” she said, lowering her eyes.
Hearing that “Yes,” Mme. de Troisville (daughter of the Princess Scherbelloff) and the old Marquise de Castéran came up; Mlle. Armande also joined them, and the group took a turn in the garden till dinner should be ready. Mme. du Bousquier was so stupid with grief that she did not notice that a little conspiracy of curiosity was on foot among the ladies.
“We have her here, let us find out the answer to the riddle,” the glances exchanged among them seemed to say.
“You should have children to make your happiness complete,” began Mlle. Armande, “a fine boy like my nephew﻿—”
Tears came to Mme. du Bousquier’s eyes.
“I have heard it said that it was entirely your own fault if you had none,” said the Chevalier, “that you were afraid of the risk.”
“*I!*,” she cried, innocently; “I would endure a hundred years in hell to have a child.”
The subject thus broached, Mme. la Vicomtesse de Troisville and the dowager Marquise de Castéran steered the conversation with such exceeding tact, that they entangled poor Rose until, all unsuspectingly, she revealed the secrets of her married life. Mlle. Armande laid her hand on the Chevalier’s arm, and they left the three matrons to talk confidentially. Then Mme. du Bousquier’s mind was disabused with regard to the deception of her marriage; and as she was still “a natural,” she amused her confidantes with her irresistible naivete. Before long the whole town was in the secret of du Bousquier’s manoeuvres, and knew that Mlle.

也许这心碎的可怜女子，就爱同一个舅公生前深爱的人谈论舅公。何况，隔了近三年，她也想领受老侯爵的问候。一点半钟；瓦卢瓦骑士来吃饭了，他鞠一躬，伸出双手。
“啊！是的，亲爱的、善良的、深受爱戴的夫人，”他声音发颤地说，“我们失去了我们圣洁的朋友。您的丧就是我们的丧。是的；您的损失，在这里同在您屋顶下一样深切——更深切。”他添一句，暗指杜·布斯基耶。
跟着是一篇悼词，人人凑上一句；随后骑士献殷勤地拉起夫人的手，掩进自己臂弯，用最可爱的方式握着，把她引到一扇窗的窗龛里。
“您总归是幸福的吧？”他用父亲般的口气问。
“是。”她垂着眼睛说。
听见这一声“是”，德·特鲁瓦维尔太太（谢尔贝洛夫公主的女儿）和老德·卡斯特朗侯爵夫人走了过来；阿尔芒德小姐也凑上去；一小群人便在花园里踱步，等开饭。杜·布斯基耶太太悲恸得发了木，竟没留意太太们中间正进行着一场好奇的小阴谋。
“她可来了，咱们把谜底套出来。”她们交换的眼色仿佛这样说。
“您该有几个孩子，幸福才算圆满，”阿尔芒德小姐开了头，“一个像我家侄儿那样的好男孩——”
杜·布斯基耶太太的眼里涌上泪来。
“我可是听说，没有孩子全怪您自己，”骑士说，“说您怕险。”
“我！”她天真地喊；“为有个孩子，地狱里一百年我也甘受。”
话头既开，特鲁瓦维尔子爵夫人和卡斯特朗太侯爵夫人便以过人的机巧把舵，把可怜的罗丝缠了个严实，直到她全无提防，把婚后生活的秘密一一抖了出来。阿尔芒德小姐把手搭上骑士的胳膊，两人撇下三位主妇密谈去了。于是，杜·布斯基耶太太的头脑被擦亮了，看清了婚姻的骗局；而她到底“是个憨人”，她那不可抗拒的天真把几位知音逗乐了。不多久，全城都知道了杜·布斯基耶的手腕，知道科尔蒙

===Original===

Cormon’s marriage was a mockery; but after the first burst of laughter, Mme. du Bousquier gained the esteem and sympathy of every woman in it. While Mlle. Cormon rushed unsuccessfully at opportunities of establishing herself, everyone had laughed; but people admired her when they knew the position in which she was placed by the severity of her religious principles. “Poor, dear Mlle. Cormon!” was replaced by “poor Mme. du Bousquier!”
In this way the Chevalier made du Bousquier both ridiculous and very unpopular for a while, but the ridicule died down with time; the slander languished when everybody had cut his joke; and besides, it seemed to many persons that the mute Republican had a right to retire at the age of fifty-seven. But if du Bousquier previously hated the Maison d’Esgrignon, this incident so increased his rancor that he was pitiless afterwards in the day of vengeance. Mme. du Bousquier received orders never to set foot in that house again; and by way of reprisals, he inserted the following paragraph in the *Orne Courier*, his own new paper:

> “**A Reward** of rente to bring in a thousand francs will be paid to any person who shall prove that one M. de Pombreton existed either before or after the Emigration.”

小姐的婚姻原来是一场笑柄；可第一阵哄笑过后，杜·布斯基耶太太赢得了全城每个女人的敬重和同情。当年科尔蒙小姐一次次扑空出丑，人人笑她；如今大家知道了她那严峻的教义给她摆下的处境，反倒敬她了。“可怜的、亲爱的科尔蒙小姐！”换成了“可怜的杜·布斯基耶太太！”
骑士就这样叫杜·布斯基耶又可笑又不得人心了一阵子；可笑随时间淡了，人人说够之后诽谤也慡了；何况在许多人看来，这位不声不响的共和党人到了五十七岁，原也有权告退。可杜·布斯基耶先前若只是恨埃斯格里尼翁府，这一场更把恨加浓了，浓得复仇之日一到，他毫不留情。杜·布斯基耶太太奉旨永不得再踏进那座宅子；而作为报复，他在自家的报纸《奥恩信使报》上登了下面这则启事：

> “悬赏：凡能证明某位德·蓬布雷东先生在流亡前后存在过者，赏一千法郎年金。

===Original===

Though Mme. du Bousquier’s happiness was essentially negative, she saw that her marriage had its advantages. Was it not better to take an interest in the most remarkable man in the place than to live alone? After all, du Bousquier was better than the dogs, cats, and canaries on which old maids centre their affections; and his feeling for his wife was something more genuine and disinterested than the attachment of servants, confessors, and legacy-hunters. At a still later period she looked upon her husband as an instrument in God’s hands to punish her for the innumerable sins which she discovered in her desires for marriage; she regarded herself as justly rewarded for the misery which she had brought on Mme. Granson, and for hastening her own uncle’s end. Obedient to a religious faith which bade her kiss the rod, she praised her husband in public; but in the confessional, or over her prayers at night, she often wept and entreated God to pardon the apostate who said one thing and thought another, who wished for the destruction of the order of nobles and the Church, the two religions of the Maison Cormon. Living in an uncongenial atmosphere, compelled to suppress herself, compelled likewise by a sense of duty to make her husband happy, and to injure him in nothing, she became attached to him with an indefinable affection, perhaps the result of use and wont. Her life was a perpetual contradiction. She felt the strongest aversion for the conduct and opinions of the man she had married, and yet it was her duty to take a tender interest in him; and if, as often happened, du Bousquier ate her preserves, or thought that the dinner was good, she was in the seventh heaven. She saw that his comfort was secured even in the smallest details. If he left the wrapper of his newspaper on the table, there it must remain.
“Leave it, René,” she would say, “the master had some reason for putting it there.”
Did du Bousquier go on a journey? She fidgeted over his traveling cloak and his linen; she took the most minute precautions for his material comfort. If he was going over to the Prébaudet, she began to consult the weather glass twenty-four hours beforehand. A sleeping dog has eyes and ears for his master, and so it was with Mme. du Bousquier; she used to watch the expression of her husband’s face to read his wishes.

杜·布斯基耶太太的幸福本质上是消极的，可她也看出这门婚事自有它的好处。关心本城最出色的人物，总强过独自活着吧？说到底，杜·布斯基耶总比老姑娘们寄托感情的那些狗、猫、金丝雀强；他对妻子的那份情，也比仆人、神师和遗产觊觎者的依恋更真、更少私心。更晚些时候，她把丈夫看作上帝手中的棍子，来罚她在出嫁之念里发现的无数罪过；她认为自己给格朗松太太带来的苦难、自己加速舅公的死，如今报在自己身上，公道。她顺从那叫她亲吻责罚之杖的信仰，当众夸丈夫；可在告解亭里，在夜里的祷告中，她常常哭求上帝饶恕那个使徒——他嘴上一套心里一套，他盼着毁掉贵族和教会，科尔蒙府的两大信仰。她活在不合的水土里，被迫收起自己，又迫于本分要叫丈夫幸福、丝毫不伤他，便对他生出一种说不出名目的情，也许是习惯使然。她的一生是一场没完的拧劲。她对她嫁的这个人的行径和见解怀有最强烈的憎恶，却又必须对他温存关切；而倘若杜·布斯基耶——这是常有的事——吃了她的果酱，夸一声晚饭好，她便上了七重天。他最细小的舒适她都看得周全。他若把报纸的封套撂在桌上，那就得撂在那儿。
“别动，勒内，”她会说，“主人把它放在那儿自有道理。”
杜·布斯基耶出门？她为他的旅行氅衣和衬衣揪心；为他肉身的舒适，她把小心使到毫巅。他若去普雷博代，她提前二十四小时就开始看晴雨表。睡着的狗还替主人留着眼睛耳朵呢，杜·布斯基耶太太也是这样；她惯于察看丈夫脸上的气色，去读他的意愿。

And if that burly personage, vanquished by duty-prescribed love, caught her by the waist and kissed her on the forehead, exclaiming, “You are a good woman!” tears of joy filled the poor creature’s eyes. It is probable that du Bousquier felt it incumbent upon him to make compensations which won Rose Marie Victoire’s respect; for the Church does not require that an assumption of wifely devotion should be carried quite so far as Mme. du Bousquier thought necessary. And yet when she listened to the rancorous talk of men who took Constitutional-Royalism as a cloak for their real opinions, the woman of saintly life uttered not a word. She foresaw the downfall of the Church, and shuddered. Very occasionally she would hazard some foolish remark, promptly cut in two by a look from du Bousquier. In the end this life at cross-purposes had a benumbing influence on Mme. du Bousquier’s wits; she found it both simpler and more dignified to keep her mind to herself, and led outwardly a mere animal existence. She grew slavishly submissive, making a virtue of the abject condition to which her husband had reduced her; she did her husband’s will without murmuring in the least. The timid sheep walked in the way marked out by the shepherd; never leaving the bosom of the Church, practising austerities, without a thought of the Devil, his pomps and works. And so, within herself she united the purest Christian virtues, and du Bousquier truly was one of the luckiest men in the kingdom of France and Navarre.
“She will be a simpleton till her last sigh,” said the cruel ex-registrar (now cashiered). But, all the same, he dined at her table twice a week.
The story would be singularly incomplete if it omitted to mention a last coincidence; the Chevalier de Valois and Suzanne’s mother died at the same time.
The Chevalier died with the Monarchy in August 1830. He went to Nonancourt to join the funeral procession; piously making one of the King’s escort to Cherbourg, with the Troisvilles, Castérans, d’Esgrignons, Verneuils, and the rest. He had brought with him his little hoard of savings and the principal which brought him in his annual income, some fifty thousand francs in all, which he offered to a faithful friend of the elder branch to convey to His Majesty.

而倘若那魁伟的人物，败给了责任规定的爱，搂住她的腰，吻她的额头，喊一句“你是个好女人！”——这可怜人眼里便涌满欢喜的泪。多半是杜·布斯基耶觉得理当有所补偿，赢得了罗丝·玛丽·维克图瓦的敬重；因为教会并不要求妻子式的奉献装到杜·布斯基耶太太认为必要的那个份上。可当她听着那班拿立宪保王当幌子遮真心思的人们发牢骚时，这位圣洁的女人一言不发。她预见教会的倾覆，发着抖。偶尔她冒出一句傻话，立刻叫杜·布斯基耶一眼拦腰斩断。到头来，这种拧着过的日子麻掉了杜·布斯基耶太太的心智；她发现把心思留给自己又省事又体面，外面上过起了纯然动物的日子。她变得奴性十足地顺从，把丈夫把她降到的卑贱地步修成德行；丈夫的意志她执行得毫无怨言。怯懦的绵羊沿着牧人划的道走；从不离教会的怀抱，苦修不擱，心里再不想魔鬼及其浮华功业。就这样，她一身聚齐了最纯的基督徒美德，而杜·布斯基耶实在算得上法兰西及纳瓦尔王国最有福的男人之一。
“她到咽气都是个憨子。”刻薄的前登记官（如今被撤了职）说。不过饶是如此，他一礼拜还是上她家吃两回饭。
这故事若不提最后一桩巧合，便大大不全了：瓦卢瓦骑士同苏珊娜的母亲，死在同一时刻。
骑士同君主制一齐死在 1830 年 8 月。他去农让库尔加入送葬的行列；虔敬地随特鲁瓦维尔、卡斯特朗、埃斯格里尼翁、韦尔纳伊各家和其余的人，护送国王到瑟堡。他随身带着他那一小笔积蓄和生息的本金，总共约五万法郎，要托长支的一位忠仆转呈陛下。

===Original===

His own death was very near, he said; the money had come to him through the King’s bounty; and, after all, the property of the last of the Valois belonged to the Crown. History does not say whether the Chevalier’s fervent zeal overcame the repugnance of the Bourbon who left his fair kingdom of France without taking one farthing into exile; but the King surely must have been touched by the old noble’s devotion; and this much is at least certain﻿—Césarine, M. de Valois’ universal legatee, inherited scarcely six hundred livres of income at his death. The Chevalier came back to Alençon, brokenhearted and spent with the fatigue of the journey, to die just as Charles X set foot on foreign soil.
Mme. du Val-Noble and her journalist protector, fearing reprisals from the Liberals, were glad of an excuse to return incognito to the village where the old mother died. Suzanne attended the sale of the Chevalier’s furniture to buy some relic of her first good friend, and ran up the price of the snuffbox to the enormous amount of a thousand francs. The Princess Goritza’s portrait alone was worth that sum. Two years afterwards, a young man of fashion, struck with its marvelous workmanship, obtained it of Suzanne for his collection of fine eighteenth century snuffboxes; and so the delicate toy which had been the confidant of the most courtly of love affairs, and the delight of an old age till its very end, is now brought into the semipublicity of a collection. If the dead could know what is done after they are gone, there would be a flush at this moment on the Chevalier’s left cheek.
If this history should inspire owners of sacred relics with a holy fear, and set them drafting codicils to provide for the fate of such precious souvenirs of a happiness now no more, by giving them into sympathetic hands; even so an enormous service would have been rendered to the chivalrous and sentimental section of the public; but it contains another and a much more exalted moral.﻿ ﻿… Does it not show that a new branch of education is needed? Is it not an appeal to the so enlightened solicitude of Ministers of Public Instruction to create chairs of anthropology, a science in which Germany is outstripping us?
Modern myths are even less understood of the people than ancient myths, eaten up with myths though we may be.

===Chinese===

自己的死期近了，他说；这笔钱本是国王的恩泽到他手里的；说到底，末代瓦卢瓦的财产理应归王室。史书没有记载：骑士的一片热忱，究竟压没压倒那位波旁的嫌弃——他离开法兰西的美好王国时，流亡行囊里没带一个铜板；但国王必定动容于老贵族的忠爱；至少有一点确凿——塞扎里娜，德·瓦卢瓦先生的全部遗产继承人，在他死后继承到的年金不足六百里弗尔。骑士回到阿朗松，心碎身竭，死在查理十世踏上外国土地的那一刻。
迪·瓦卢瓦贵族街夫人和她那位报人保护人，怕自由党报复，乐得有个由头，微服回到老母亲咽气的村子。苏珊娜去参加骑士遗物的拍卖，想买一件头一位好朋友的遗念，把那只鼻烟盒的价抬到了一千法郎的天数。单是戈里察公主的画像就值这个数。两年后，一位时髦青年看中那盒子惊人的做工，从苏珊娜手里为他的十八世纪鼻烟盒收藏把它弄了去；于是，这件精致的小玩意——它曾是最高雅的一场爱情的密友，陪一位老人到风烛尽头——如今进了半公开的收藏室。倘若死者能知道身后的事，此刻骑士的左颊上该有一片红晕。
这部历史倘若能叫圣物的收藏家们生出一点神圣的畏惧，叫他们起草附录条款，防备这些幸福纪念品的下场，把交到同情人手里；那么，纵然如此，也算是给了多情尚侠的那部分公众一个大大的效劳；但它还藏着另一重高得多的教义。……它岂不说明，教育需要开一支新的分科？岂不是向诸位如此开明的教育部长先生们呼吁，请设人类学的讲座——这门科学，德国已把我们甩在身后了。
现代神话比古代神话更不为民众所懂，尽管我们浸在神话里直到喉咙。

===Original===

Fables crowd in upon us on every side, allegory is pressed into service on all occasions to explain everything. If fables are the torches of history, as the humanist school maintains, they may be a means of securing empires from revolution, if only professors of history will undertake that their interpretations thereof shall permeate the masses in the departments. If Mlle. Cormon had had some knowledge of literature; if there had been a professor of anthropology in the department of the Orne; if (a final if) she had read her Ariosto, would the appalling misfortune of her marriage have befallen her? She would, perhaps, have found out for herself why the Italian poet makes his heroine Angelica prefer Medoro (a suave Chevalier de Valois) to Orlando, who had lost his mare, and could do nothing but work himself into a fury. Might not Medoro be taken as an allegorical figure as the courtier of woman’s sovereignty, whereas Orlando is revolution personified, an undisciplined, furious, purely destructive force, incapable of producing anything? This is the opinion of one of M. Ballanche’s pupils; we publish it, declining all responsibility.
As for the tiny Negroes’ heads, no information of any kind concerning them is forthcoming. Mme. du Val-Noble you may see any day at the Opéra. Thanks to the primary education given to her by the Chevalier de Valois, she looks almost like a woman who makes a necessity of virtue, while in truth she only exists by virtue of necessity.
Mme. du Bousquier is still living, which is to say, is it not, that her troubles are not yet over? At sixty, when women can permit themselves to make admissions, talking confidentially to Mme. du Coudrai, whose husband was reinstated in August 1830, she said that the thought that she must die without knowing what it was to be a wife and mother was more than she could bear.

===Chinese===

寓言四面八方朝我们挤过来，寓言被随时随地拉来解释一切。倘若如人文主义学派所主张，寓言是历史的火把，那么只要教历史的教授们肯下功夫，让他们的解释渗透到各省的群众里去，寓言未必不是使帝国免于革命的一件法器。倘若科尔蒙小姐懂一点文学；倘若奥恩省有一位人类学教授；倘若（最后一个倘若）她读过她的阿里奥斯托——她那桩骇人的婚姻不幸还会落到她头上么？她多半会自己弄明白，意大利诗人为什么让他的女主人公安杰莉卡挑中梅多罗（一位温文版的瓦卢瓦骑士），而不要奥兰多——那匹母马丢了、只会暴跳如雷的莽汉。梅多罗何尝不可读作“女王治下的朝臣”的寓言形象，而奥兰多正是革命的化身：一支无纪律、暴怒、纯破坏、无一天产出的力量？这是一位巴尔朗什先生门徒的高见；我们照登不误，责任概不承担。
至于那对小小的黑种小人头，没有任何相关消息可奉告。迪·瓦卢瓦贵族街夫人，您哪天去歌剧院都看得见。多亏瓦卢瓦骑士给她的启蒙教育，她看上去几乎像一位把需要变成德行的女人，而实际上她不过是靠德行才有了需要。
杜·布斯基耶太太还活着——这就是说，她的苦难还没受完，对不对？六十岁上，到了女人可以自认心迹的年纪，她同德·杜库德雷太太（丈夫于 1830 年 8 月官复原职）咬耳朵说：一想到死也不知道做妻子做母亲是什么滋味，她实在捱不住。
